The 3D BF model and Topological Insulators (2010)

Many years after the first studies on the quantum Hall effect and its edge states, a new kind of topological phase of matter was observed :

[
95
,
21
,
24
,
22
]
. These new materials belong to the category of Hall systems, and are distinguished by the fact that their bulk is invariant under time-reversal (
\mathcal{T}
) transformation, and so are its edge modes, which are characterized by spin currents. This gave them the name of
systems. Indeed, while for standard Hall systems the presence of a time-reversal breaking magnetic field is necessary, in the case of the
the edge states are protected by that symmetry, and a manifestation of a spin-orbit interaction
[
22
]
. Following this discovery, a natural question raised as to whether edge states of
systems could emerge from a
with boundary, as it happened for the
and Chern-Simons. In 2010 the answer arrived, and it was shown
[
34
]
that the edge states of three- and four-dimensional topological insulators could be related to the three- and four-dimensional BF models with boundary respectively. We remind that this is the other
of Schwarz type, which in the abelian 3D case is represented by the classical action (
3.33
). Indeed one can see that the bulk BF theory, compared to the Chern-Simons one, has the additional property of being invariant under time-reversal. In fact defining the Time-Reversal transformation
\mathcal{T}
to act on coordinates as

\mathcal{T}x^{0}=-x^{0}\quad;\quad\mathcal{T}x^{1}=x^{1}\quad;\quad\mathcal{T}x^{2}=x^{2}\ ,

(8.1)

the 3D BF action (
3.33
) is invariant for fields that transform as follows

\mathcal{T}A_{0}=A_{0}\quad;\quad\mathcal{T}A_{1,2}=-A_{1,2}\quad;\quad\mathcal{T}B_{0}=-B_{0}\quad;\quad\mathcal{T}B_{1,2}=B_{1,2}\ .

(8.2)

According to this the gauge field
A_{\mu}(x)
transforms as an electromagnetic potential, while
B_{\mu}(x)
as a spin current, remarking the physical relation with
. This invariance can also be transposed to the boundary states, thus mirroring the characteristic of topological insulators. In this Section we will review the 3D case that was studied as a
with boundary in
[
88
]
. This will serve as a starting point for the original work presented in the next Chapter.

The full action of the model with boundary is

S_{tot}=S_{BF}+S_{gf}+S_{J}+S_{bd}\ ,

(8.3)

composed of the following bulk, gauge-fixing, source and boundary terms

\displaystyle S_{BF}

\displaystyle=\kappa\int d^{3}x\theta(x^{2})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}B_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\rho}

(8.4)

\displaystyle S_{gf}

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\theta(x^{2})\left(bA_{2}+d\,B_{2}\right)

(8.5)

\displaystyle S_{J}

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\theta(x^{2})\left(J^{i}A_{i}+K^{i}B_{i}\right)

(8.6)

\displaystyle S_{bd}

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}\delta(x^{2})\left(\tfrac{a_{1}}{2}A_{i}A^{i}+\tfrac{a_{2}}{2}B_{i}B^{i}+a_{3}A_{i}B^{i}\right)\ ,

(8.7)

where again a flat boundary at
x^{2}=0
has been introduced through a theta term
\theta(x^{2})
, an axial gauge fixing has been chosen for both
A_{\mu}(x)
and
B_{\mu}(x)
gauge fields, and a covariant boundary term has been defined in (
8.7
). Notice that here a Lorentz-invariant boundary term has been chosen, and a particular combination of the coefficients (
a_{3}=0
) allows for it to be invariant under the time-reversal
\mathcal{T}
transformation defined in (
8.1
) and (
8.2
), thus mimicking the property of the bulk. This reflects on the boundary and the
, which, excluding Dirichlet solutions, give

a_{1}A^{i}-\epsilon^{0ij}B_{j}=0\ ,

(8.8)

for
a_{1}a_{2}=\kappa-1
, and
a_{3}=0
. The presence of the boundary implies a breaking of the Ward identity, one for each field, giving the following integrated quantities

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dx^{2}\partial_{i}J^{i}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\epsilon^{0ij}\partial_{i}B_{j}|_{x^{2}=0}

(8.9)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dx^{2}\partial_{i}K^{i}

\displaystyle=-\kappa\epsilon^{0ij}\partial_{i}A_{j}|_{x^{2}=0}\ .

(8.10)

Going on-shell (
i.e.
at vanishing external sources
K=J=0
) in (
8.9
) and (
8.10
) one can also recover the boundary
to be two scalar fields

\displaystyle\epsilon^{0ij}\partial_{i}B_{j}|_{x^{2}=0}

\displaystyle=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad B_{j}|_{x^{2}=0}=\partial_{j}\zeta

(8.11)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{0ij}\partial_{i}A_{j}|_{x^{2}=0}

\displaystyle=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad A_{j}|_{x^{2}=0}=\partial_{j}\Lambda\ .

(8.12)

A semidirect sum of
algebras can be finally derived by applying functional derivatives with respect to the currents in the broken identities (
8.9
) and (
8.10
). For instance differentiating (
8.9
) with respect to
J^{j}(x^{\prime})
one gets, at equal time,

\left[B_{1}(X)\ ,\ A_{1}(X^{\prime})\right]=\frac{i}{\kappa}\delta(x^{1}-x^{\prime 1})\ ,

(8.13)

which can be interpreted as a canonical commutation relation for the boundary scalar fields, thus identifying the canonical variables to be

q=\kappa\zeta\quad;\quad p=\partial_{1}\Lambda\ .

(8.14)

The induced 2D shift-invariant action compatible with the canonical variables identified in (
8.14
) and whose
matches the Time-Reversal-invariant
(
8.8
) of the bulk is the following

S_{2D}=\kappa\int d^{2}X\left[\partial_{0}\zeta\partial_{1}\Lambda+\frac{1}{2a_{1}}(\partial_{1}\zeta)^{2}+\frac{a_{1}}{2}(\partial_{1}\Lambda)^{2}\right]\ .

(8.15)

Notice that it seems that starting with a covariant boundary action
S_{bd}
(
8.7
) leads to a 2D action that shows a strong-weak coupling duality
i.e.\ \zeta\leftrightarrow\Lambda\ ,\ a_{1}\leftrightarrow\frac{1}{a_{1}}
, and the
can be understood as the continuity equations for fluids,

\partial_{0}\rho_{i}+v_{i}\partial_{1}\rho_{i}=0\ ,

(8.16)

with
i=1,2
,
v_{1}\equiv\frac{1}{a_{1}}\ ,\ v_{2}\equiv a_{1}
, and densities
\rho_{1}\equiv\partial_{1}\zeta,\ \rho_{2}\equiv\partial_{1}\Lambda
. This does not represent a time-reversal invariant current, which is described by chiral bosons moving at equal and opposite velocities. This property is however recovered if
a_{1}=1
, for which

\partial_{0}\rho_{\pm}\pm\partial_{1}\rho_{\pm}=0\ ,

(8.17)

where
\rho_{\pm}\equiv\partial_{1}\left(\zeta\pm\Lambda\right)
, which describes bosons moving at the speed of light in opposite directions. We will see in the next Chapter, and in particular in Section
10
, that by considering a non-covariant boundary action
S_{bd}
(
8.7
), we will be able to recover finite values for the velocities.

†
†
margin:

5

metric dependence of the edge states of TQFTs

We have just seen that, all measurable quantities related to edge states of Hall systems, like for instance filling factors
\nu
and chiral velocities of the edge modes
v
, have been obtained for
flat
bulk manifolds, with planar, or radial (in the case of Chern-Simons on a disk
[
90
]
for instance) boundary. However experiments are pushing theoretical investigations, since recently
accelerated
chiral bosons have been observed on the edge of some particular Hall systems
[
48
]
, which cannot be explained by the usual flat Chern-Simons with boundary paradigm (
on flat spacetime with planar boundary), which unavoidably yields
constant
edge velocities, as we have shown in Chapter
4
. The standard approach to deal with these cases is phenomenological, and basically consists in adding a suitable potential to the 2D Luttinger action, in order to reproduce time-dependent velocities
[
96
,
97
,
98
,
99
,
100
]
. The price of this way of solving the problem is that the whole “holographic” construction of finding the right lower dimensional action without any
ad\ hoc
extension fails. Similar experimental evidence is currently sought in the other relevant topological state of matter,
i.e.
the topological insulators. In all the cases we mentioned above, the lower dimensional edge dynamics depends on the bulk only through its parameters, and the details of the bulk manifold are somehow hidden by the particular flat choice. But is there any hidden dependence of the boundary physics on the bulk manifold? It appears interesting and reasonable to ask if, how and where the details of the metric reveal themselves in the edge observables, to see which are the quantities depending on the bulk metric and, maybe even more interestingly, which are the physical quantities really universal, or topologically protected. Thus the aim of this Chapter is to investigate an alternative approach to reproduce accelerated edge modes in Hall systems without the need of adding any empirical potential, while keeping the holographic construction intact. It consists in considering the bulk theory on a generic, rather that flat, background manifold. While the topological invariant action of course does not depend on the particular spacetime metric, Symanzik’s boundary term and the boundary itself certainly do, and it is an interesting issue to find out if, how and where this metric dependence reflects on the holographic 2D theory and, more interestingly, on physical observables. The holographic 2D theory induced on the boundary of the abelian Chern-Simons theory is the Floreanini-Jackiw action
[
101
]
, describing edge modes moving with constant chiral velocities, which are indeed observed
[
102
]
. The velocity of the edge excitations is the main observable of both Hall systems and
. We will see that a remnant of the bulk metric remains on these most relevant physical observables,
i.e.
the chiral velocity of the edge modes, which will become
local
. In other words, the edge modes of the Hall systems, when described by a
built on a generic manifold, are accelerated and, moreover, the velocities also depend on the position of the quasiparticle on the boundary, not only on time. The dependence on the bulk metric manifests only through the determinant of the induced metric on the boundary, hence it is mild, as one might expect due to the topological character of the bulk theory. Still, the local effects are reproduced, without any empirical modification of the 2D holographic Luttinger theory.

The Chapter is composed of two main Sections, each concerning the effect of a curved spacetime in one of the two abelian Schwarz
with boundary : Chern-Simons theory in Section
9
, and the 3D BF model in Section
10
.

9
Edge states of Chern-Simons theory

The present Section, which concenrns the Chern-Simons model in curved spacetime with boundary, is organized as follows

In particular in Section
9.1
the Chern-Simons theory on a cylindrical spacetime
\mathbb{R}\times D
, in a not necessarily flat, Lorentzian background is introduced, and the
, together with the most general
are derived. We find the Ward identity, broken by the boundary, and we study the existence of a
algebra, with constant and positive central charge. In Section
9.2
we derive the 2D theory, holographically induced on the boundary of the 3D Chern-Simons theory. The holographic contact is imposed, which relates the parameters of the edge theory to the bulk ones. The dependence on the bulk through the induced metric on the boundary is discussed. Our concluding remarks are summarized in Section
9.4
.

9.1
Chern-Simons with boundary at
r=R

It is convenient, in this first approach, to work in
(
), where the line element takes the form

ds^{2}=g_{\mu\nu}(x)dx^{\mu}dx^{\nu}=\ dr^{2}+\gamma_{ij}(x)dX^{i}dX^{j}\ .

(9.1)

The adoption of the
is particularly useful for calculations in which one is given a hypersurface
\partial\mathcal{M}
,
i.e.
a
(d-1)
-dimensional embedded submanifold of the d-dimensional manifold
\mathcal{M}

[
103
]
. In the case treated here, being
\mathcal{M}
diffeomorphic to a cylinder
\mathcal{M}\simeq\mathbb{R}\times D
, this choice is the most natural one, since it immediately provides the induced metric on the boundary. The
are characterized by the presence of one coordinate (the one normal to the hypersurface, in our case
r
) such that
g_{rr}=1
and the off-diagonal terms vanish :
g_{rt}=g_{r\theta}=0

[
104
]
. These are three extra conditions which might be seen as “gauge conditions” on the metric
[
105
,
106
]
, corresponding to the threefold coordinate freedom
x^{\mu}\rightarrow x^{\prime\;\mu}=x^{\prime\;\mu}(x)
. Simple examples of
are the Cartesian coordinates on Minkowski space, or polar coordinates in Euclidean 2 and 3-space, or the Robertson-Walker coordinates used in cosmology
[
105
]
.
Using these coordinates, the determinant of the induced metric on the boundary

\gamma_{ij}(X)=\frac{\partial x^{\mu}}{\partial X^{i}}\frac{\partial x^{\nu}}{\partial X^{j}}g_{\mu\nu}(x)\ ,

(9.2)

is
[
105
]

\sqrt{-\gamma}=\sqrt{-g}\ ,

(9.3)

which, in particular, holds on the boundary
r=R
.
Lastly, in curved spacetime the Heaviside step distribution is a scalar, and its derivative, as in flat spacetime, is (see Appendix
10.A
)

\nabla_{\mu}\theta(R-r)=-\delta_{\mu}^{r}\delta(R-r)\ .

(9.4)

The action

We consider the abelian 3D Chern-Simons theory on a cylindrical spacetime
\mathbb{R}\times D
, where the model is confined to the closed subspace
0\leq r\leq R
. This is achieved by introducing a Heaviside step distribution
\theta(R-r)
in the action. The Chern-Simons bulk term is then

S_{bulk}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\int d^{3}x\;\theta(R-r)\;\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho}\;A_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A_{\rho}\ .

(9.5)

We remark that although the coupling constant
\kappa
could be reabsorbed by a redefinition of the fields, it is useful to leave it explicit, in order to keep track of the contributions of the bulk theory to the physics of the boundary. Moreover, since the Chern-Simons theory, being topological, has vanishing energy momentum tensor, there is no constraint on the sign of
\kappa
from requiring a positive energy density. Still,
\kappa
should be a positive constant, as we shall see later.
The gauge fixing term is chosen to be

S_{gf}=\int d^{3}x\;\sqrt{-g}\;\theta(R-r)\;b\;n^{\mu}A_{\mu}\ ,

(9.6)

where
n^{\mu}=(0,1,0)
is a unit vector. The field
b(x)
is the Nakanishi-Lautrup Lagrange multiplier
[
107
,
108
]
which implements
the radial gauge choice

\frac{\delta S}{\delta b}=n^{\mu}A_{\mu}=A_{r}=0\ .

(9.7)

The choice of the radial gauge-fixing
A_{r}=0
is analogous to the choice of the
(
9.1
). Both are the most convenient choices in presence of the boundary
r=R
: the latter on the metric
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
and corresponds to the reparametrization invariance, the former on the gauge field
A_{\mu}(x)
and comes from gauge invariance. Both transformations (ordinary gauge symmetry and diffeomorphism invariance) are broken by the boundary, and physical results should not depend on them
[
28
,
29
]
.
External sources are coupled to the
A_{\mu}(x)
field, through the term

S_{ext}=\int d^{3}x\;\sqrt{-g}\;\theta(R-r)\;J^{\mu}A_{\mu}\ .

(9.8)

In addition, the presence of a boundary induces an extra term in the action, as the most general boundary term compatible with power counting:

S_{bd}=\int d^{3}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,\delta(r-R)\,\frac{1}{2}\;T^{ij}A_{i}A_{j}\ ,

(9.9)

where
T^{ij}=T^{ji}
is a symmetric matrix.
Notice that in a curved spacetime all the coefficients appearing in the action may depend on the metric, which is dimensionless, and hence on the coordinates, but only through the metric. In particular,
T^{ij}
in
S_{bd}
might depend implicitly on the coordinates
T^{ij}=T^{ij}(\gamma(X))
, where
\gamma(X)
is the induced metric. An explicit dependence of
T^{ij}
on the coordinates “outside” the induced metric
(T^{ij}=T^{ij}(\gamma(X);X))
is forbidden, since in the flat limit
T^{ij}
should reduce to a constant symmetric matrix :
\left.T^{ij}(\gamma(X))\right|_{\gamma^{ij}=\eta^{ij}}=T^{ij}
. A similar boundary term appears also in
[
109
]
for Chern-Simons theory. In both cases, the main reason is to provide, by means of the modified equations of motion, the most general
which need to be fixed. The introduction of such a local functional allows to introduce the
in a systematic way by means of a variational principle, and not by hand, the boundary term must only satisfy the general requirements of power counting and locality. Gauge invariance and/or residual 2D covariance must not be required on it, otherwise, one would not recover the boundary dynamics which characterizes
(see for instance
[
109
]
, where the prescription of introducing a non covariant boundary term
\propto\int_{\partial Y}TrA_{1}A_{2}
is adopted in order to get consistent
).
Finally, the total action of the theory, considering the bulk, gauge-fixing, external source and boundary terms, is

S=S_{bulk}+S_{gf}+S_{ext}+S_{bd}\ .

(9.10)

Equations of motion and boundary conditions

From the action
S
(
9.10
) we get the

\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\lambda}}=\theta(R-r)\left(\kappa\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}+b\;n^{\lambda}+J^{\lambda}\right)+\delta(R-r)\delta^{\lambda}_{j}\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\epsilon^{i1j}+T^{ij}\right)A_{i}=0\ .

(9.11)

Applying the operator

\lim_{\epsilon\to R}\int_{\epsilon}^{R}dr
to the
(
9.11
), the following
can be derived

\left.\left(\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\tilde{\epsilon}^{1ij}}{\sqrt{-g}}+T^{ij}\right)A_{j}\right|_{r=R}=0\ .

(9.12)

In a more compact way, the
can be written in matricial form

M^{ij}A_{j}=0\ ,

(9.13)

where

M=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}c_{1}&c_{2}-\tilde{\kappa}\\
c_{2}+\tilde{\kappa}&c_{3}\end{array}\right)\ ,

(9.14)

having defined

c_{1}\equiv T^{00}\ ;\ c_{2}\equiv T^{02}=T^{20}\ ;\ c_{3}\equiv T^{22}

(9.15)

and

\tilde{\kappa}\equiv\frac{\kappa}{2}\frac{\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}}{\sqrt{-g}}\ .

(9.16)

Even though in (
9.16
) the value of the 012-component of the Levi-Civita symbol is
\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}=1
, we choose to keep it explicit, to enhance the
fact that
\tilde{\kappa}(X)
is a scalar, and not a scalar density, as it would appear by hiding
\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}
.
The most general solution of the
(
9.13
) which does not involve vanishing components of the gauge field is

A_{t}+vA_{\theta}=0\ ,

(9.17)

where the coefficient
v(X)
in (
9.17
) must be different from zero and is not constant, since it depends on the parameters appearing in the action
S
(
9.10
) according to the following relations

•

if
c_{1}=0\ ;\ c_{2}=\tilde{\kappa}\ ;\ c_{3}\neq 0

2\tilde{\kappa}A_{t}+c_{3}A_{\theta}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad v=\frac{c_{3}}{2\tilde{\kappa}}\ ;

(9.18)

•

if
c_{1}\neq 0\ ;\ c_{2}=-\tilde{\kappa}\ ;\ c_{3}=0

c_{1}A_{t}-2\tilde{\kappa}A_{\theta}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad v=-\frac{2\tilde{\kappa}}{c_{1}}\ ;

(9.19)

•

if
c_{1}\neq 0\ ;\ c_{2}\neq\pm\tilde{\kappa}\ ;\ c_{3}\neq 0\ ;\ c_{1}c_{3}-c_{2}^{2}+\tilde{\kappa}^{2}=0

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}c_{1}A_{t}+(c_{2}-\tilde{\kappa})A_{\theta}=0&\Rightarrow\ v=\frac{c_{2}-\tilde{\kappa}}{c_{1}}\\
\mbox{or}&\\
(c_{2}+\tilde{\kappa})A_{t}+c_{3}A_{\theta}=0&\Rightarrow\ v=\frac{c_{3}}{c_{2}+\tilde{\kappa}}\ .\end{array}\right.

(9.20)

The parameter
v
appearing in the
(
9.17
) depends on
c_{i}
(
9.15
), which are the components of the boundary parameter
T^{ij}
appearing in
S_{bd}
(
9.9
), and on
\tilde{\kappa}
(
9.16
). Hence,
v
may depend on the coordinates through the induced metric
\gamma^{ij}
and on its determinant
\gamma
(which in
is equal to
g
). No explicit dependence on the coordinates
X=\{t,\theta\}
is possible because, as we said, in the flat limit
T^{ij}
should be constant. The
(
9.17
) is of the same type of that derived in
[
110
]
by an action principle, in analogy to our approach. The difference is that in
[
110
]

the analogue of the matrix
T^{ij}
is constant, which can be obtained after a rescaling by
\sqrt{-g}
. As a consequence, the parameter
v
appearing in the
in
[
110
]
is constant. In other words, it is always possible to rescale
v
to a constant by means of a coordinate choice. The main goal of this Section, as we shall see, is that we will be able to relate the parameter
v
to a measurable quantity, which is the velocity of the chiral edge modes. For this reason we do not rescale
v
to an arbitrary constant value, but we let
v
to be determined by a phenomenological input. It is important not to rescale
v
to a constant value in order to be able to account for
accelerated
chiral bosons on the edge of certain Hall systems, which are not explained by a constant
v
.
This is an important point, which concerns two different perspectives. Our approach is the same to the abelian Chern-Simons description of the Hall systems. From a pure field theoretical point of view, abelian Chern-Simons model is a free theory, with no coupling constant, for the simple reason that it can be reabsorbed by a rescaling of the gauge field. It is only the nonabelian extension of Chern-Simons theory which displays a true coupling constant. In condensed matter theory the perspective is different, somehow opposite. The coupling constants are not rescaled by field redefinitions, but are fixed by external phenomenological inputs. This reflects even in a different terminology. For instance, the abelian Chern-Simons “coupling constant”, which is oxymoric in field theory, is often called “Chern-Simons level” by a condensed matter-oriented reader, because it is fixed by its relation to the filling factor of Landau levels :
\kappa=\frac{1}{2\pi\nu}
. In curved spacetime the metric plays the role of a dynamical field, which we treat exactly in the same way : we do not rescale the metric by choosing a particular coordinates set, but we let the parameter
v
to be constrained by the observed chiral velocities of the edge modes. We have seen that
v
depends on the induced metric and its determinant, and hence we relate the induced metric (which depends on the bulk metric of Chern-Simons theory) to an observable quantity. And this is the only way, so far, to take into account the observed accelerated chiral velocities, which are not explained by a flat background of Chern-Simons theory alone. If we were not interested in a phenomenological interpretation of our model, we would rescale
\kappa
to one and
v
to whatever value, including zero, which would correspond to a Dirichlet condition on one component of the gauge field.

Ward identity

The covariant derivative of the
(
9.11
) is

\begin{split}\nabla_{\lambda}\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\lambda}}&=\theta(R-r)\left[\kappa\epsilon^{\lambda\mu\nu}\nabla_{\lambda}\nabla_{\mu}A_{\nu}+\nabla_{\lambda}\left(b\;n^{\lambda}\right)+\nabla_{\lambda}J^{\lambda}\right]-\\
&-\delta(R-r)\left(\kappa\epsilon^{1kj}\partial_{k}A_{j}+b+J^{r}\right)=0\ ,\end{split}

(9.21)

where the
(
9.12
) have been used to cancel the
\delta(R-r)
term on the r.h.s. of (
9.11
).
Noting that

\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}A_{\rho}=0\;,

(9.22)

we find

\theta(R-r)\nabla_{\lambda}\left(b\;n^{\lambda}+J^{\lambda}\right)-\delta(R-r)\left(\kappa\epsilon^{1kj}\partial_{k}A_{j}+b+J^{r}\right)=0\ .

(9.23)

Keeping in mind that the covariant divergence is

\nabla_{\mu}V^{\mu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}\left(V^{\mu}\sqrt{-g}\right)\quad;\quad\nabla_{k}V^{k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{k}\left(V^{k}\sqrt{-g}\right)\ ,

(9.24)

where we used (
9.3
) in the second identity, multiplying (
9.23
) by
\sqrt{-g}
and integrating
along the coordinate normal to the boundary, we get
5
5

5

Notice that in general for an integration along
r
we should use the invariant measure
\sqrt{g_{rr}}\;dr
in order to preserve the transformation properties under diffeomorphisms, but
g_{rr}=1
in GNG (
9.1
).

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{0}dr\;\left\{\theta(R-r)\left[\partial_{r}(b\sqrt{-g})+\partial_{\lambda}\left(J^{\lambda}\sqrt{-g}\right)\right]-\delta(R-r)\left(\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{1kj}\partial_{k}A_{j}\right)\right\}-

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{\infty}dr\;\delta(R-r)\left[\left(b+J^{r}\right)\sqrt{-g}\right]

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{0}dr\;\left[\theta(R-r)\partial_{k}\left(J^{k}\sqrt{-g}\right)-\delta(R-r)\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{1kj}\partial_{k}A_{j}\right]-\left[\left(b+J^{r}\right)\sqrt{-g}\right]_{r=0}

\displaystyle=\int^{\infty}_{0}dr\;\sqrt{-g}\left[\theta(R-r)\nabla_{k}J^{k}-\delta(R-r)\kappa\epsilon^{1kj}\partial_{k}A_{j}\right]\ ,

(9.25)

where we integrated by parts and used the fact that, evaluating the
(
9.11
) for
\lambda=r
and then going at
r=0
, we have
\left[b(x)+J^{r}(x)\right]_{r=0}=0
.
In fact, (
9.11
) at
r=0
(
\lambda=r
) reads

\left[b+J^{r}-2\tilde{\kappa}\,\Big(\partial_{\theta}A_{t}-\partial_{t}A_{\theta}\Big)\right]_{r=0}=0\ ,

(9.26)

and we notice that
A_{\theta}(x)
necessarily vanishes at
r=0
, together with its time derivatives, while
A_{t}(x)
, which in principle might not vanish at
r=0
, at the origin must have vanishing angular derivatives, in order to be well defined, hence the result.
Therefore, the contribution involving the Lagrange multiplier and
J^{r}
cancel
out, leaving only

\int_{0}^{R}dr\;\sqrt{-g}\nabla_{k}J^{k}=\left.\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{1kj}\partial_{k}A_{j}\right|_{r=R}\ .

(9.27)

Eq.(
9.27
) is the Ward identity of the theory, broken at its r.h.s. by the presence of the boundary, which is crucial for the determination of the boundary algebra and of the 2D theory holographically induced on the boundary. Notice that it holds for any bulk metric, and it is simply the curved extension of its flat counterpart (
7.11
)
[
87
]
. From (
9.27
), at vanishing external sources
J^{k}(x)=0
(
i.e.
going on-shell), we find

\left.\epsilon^{1kj}\partial_{k}A_{j}\right|_{r=R;J=0}=0\ ,

(9.28)

which describes a conserved current on the closed boundary
r=R
, whose most general solution is
[
111
,
112
]

A_{i}(X)=\partial_{i}\Phi(X)+\delta_{i2}\;C\ ,

(9.29)

where
C
is a constant (which we will show shortly being equal to zero), and
\Phi(X)
is a scalar field that will play the role of
of the 2D boundary theory. The components of the gauge field on the boundary are then

A_{t}(t,\theta)=\partial_{t}\Phi(t,\theta)\quad;\quad A_{\theta}(t,\theta)=\partial_{\theta}\Phi(t,\theta)+C\ .

(9.30)

Since we are considering a closed boundary, we also have to impose periodicity conditions on the fields :

A_{i}(t,\theta)=A_{i}(t,\theta+2\pi)\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Phi(t,\theta)=\Phi(t,\theta+2\pi)\ .

(9.31)

The value of the constant
C
in (
9.29
) is found by applying the mean value theorem for holomorphic functions, which states that if
f
is analytic in a region
D
, and
a\in D
, then
f(a)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{{\cal C}(a)}f
, where
{\cal C}(a)
is a circle centered in
a
.
In our case (3D) taking for
\cal C
the circular boundary
r=R
centered at
r=0
allows us to write

\oint_{ring\;R}A_{\theta}(x)=\cancel{\left.\Phi(t,\theta)\right|_{\theta=0}^{\theta=2\pi}}+2\pi\;C\ ,

(9.32)

where we used the periodicity condition of the boundary field
\Phi(X)
(
9.31
). This is the value of the bulk field at the center of the ring, and using the fact that
A_{\theta}(t,r=0,\theta)=0
, we finally get

C=0\ .

(9.33)

Algebra

The generating functional of the connected Green functions
Z_{c}[J]
is defined, as usual, in the following way

e^{iZ_{c}[J]}=\int[dA][db]\;e^{iS[A,b;J]}\ ,

(9.34)

where
S
is the total action (
9.10
). From (
9.34
) we get the 1- and 2-points Green functions

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\delta Z_{c}[J]}{\delta J^{i}(x)}\right|_{J=0}=

\displaystyle\;\langle A_{i}(x)\rangle

(9.35)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\delta^{(2)}Z_{c}[J]}{\delta J^{i}(x)\delta J^{j}(x^{\prime})}\right|_{J=0}\equiv

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}(x,x^{\prime})=i\langle T(A_{i}(x)A_{j}(x^{\prime}))\rangle\ ,

(9.36)

where the time-ordered product is defined as

\langle T(A_{j}(x)A_{l}(x^{\prime}))\rangle\equiv\theta(t-t^{\prime})\langle A_{j}(x)A_{l}(x^{\prime})\rangle+\theta(t^{\prime}-t)\langle A_{l}(x^{\prime})A_{j}(x)\rangle\ .

(9.37)

The algebra is obtained by making the functional derivative with respect to
J^{l}(x^{\prime})
of the Ward identity (
9.27
), and then going on-shell,
i.e
putting
J=0
:

\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{l}(x^{\prime})}\int_{0}^{R}dr\;\sqrt{-g}\;\nabla_{k}\,J^{k}(x)=\kappa\;\tilde{\epsilon}^{1kj}\;\partial_{k}\left.\frac{\delta^{(2)}Z_{c}}{\delta J^{l}(x^{\prime})\delta J^{j}(x)}\right|_{r=R;J=0}\ .

(9.38)

Therefore the first step is to compute

\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{l}(x^{\prime})}\nabla_{k}J^{k}(x)=\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{l}(x^{\prime})}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{k}\left(J^{k}\sqrt{-g}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{l}\tilde{\delta}^{(3)}(x-x^{\prime})\ ,

(9.39)

where we used the relation (
10.A.1
) betweeen the scalar and density delta function and the fact of working in
(
9.3
).
Using (
9.39
) on the l.h.s. of (
9.38
), we obtain

\partial_{l}\tilde{\delta}^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})=i\kappa\left.\tilde{\epsilon}^{1kj}\partial_{k}\langle T(A_{j}(x)A_{l}(x^{\prime}))\rangle\right|_{r=R}\ .

(9.40)

To write this we used the fact that for any
r^{\prime}\leq R
we have
\int_{0}^{R}dr\,\tilde{\delta}(r-r^{\prime})=1
, in fact, since by definition
\tilde{\delta}(r-r^{\prime})\equiv-\partial_{r}\theta(r^{\prime}-r)
:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}dr\,\partial_{r}\theta(r^{\prime}-r)f(r)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{R}\left\{\partial_{r}\left[\theta(r^{\prime}-r)f(r)\right]-\theta(r^{\prime}-r)\partial_{r}f(r)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left.\theta(r^{\prime}-r)f(r)\right|_{0}^{R}-\int_{0}^{R}dr\,\theta(r^{\prime}-r)\partial_{r}f(r)

(9.41)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccccc}f(R)-f(0)-\int^{R}_{0}dr\,\partial_{r}f(r)&=&0&&\mbox{if}\ r^{\prime}>R&\\
0-f(0)-\int^{r^{\prime}}_{0}dr\,\partial_{r}f(r)&=&-f(r^{\prime})&&\mbox{if}\ r^{\prime}\leq R&\ .\end{array}\right.

Choosing
l=\theta
in (
9.40
) we have :

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})

\displaystyle=i\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{1kj}\partial_{k}\left[\theta(t-t^{\prime})\langle A_{j}(X)A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\rangle+\theta(t^{\prime}-t)\langle A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})A_{j}(X)\rangle\right]

\displaystyle=i\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{102}\left(\partial_{t}\theta(t-t^{\prime})\right)\left(\langle A_{\theta}(X)A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\rangle-\langle A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})A_{\theta}(X)\rangle\right)

(9.44)

\displaystyle+\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{1kj}\left[i\theta(t-t^{\prime})\langle\bcancel{\partial_{k}A_{j}(X)}A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\rangle+i\theta(t^{\prime}-t)\langle A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\bcancel{\partial_{k}A_{j}(X)}\rangle\right]\ ,

where we used the on-shell condition (
9.28
). By defining

\left[A_{j}(X),A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\right]\equiv\langle A_{j}(X)A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\rangle-\langle A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})A_{j}(X)\rangle\ ,

(9.45)

we get

\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})=-i\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\partial_{t}\theta(t-t^{\prime})\left[A_{\theta}(X),A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\right]\ .

(9.46)

Finally, integrating over time we find the equal time commutator

\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left.\left[A_{\theta}(X),A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\right]\right|_{t=t^{\prime}}=\frac{i}{\kappa}\,\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ ,

(9.47)

By applying the same procedure to the case
l=t
of (
9.40
), we find the equal time commutator

\left.\left[A_{\theta}(X),A_{t}(X^{\prime})\right]\right|_{t=t^{\prime}}=0\ .

(9.48)

Eq.(
9.47
) represents an abelian
algebra
identical to the one found in the case of Chern-Simons theory with planar boundary in flat space (
7.12
). We see that the existence of a
algebra and its central charge depends neither on the bulk metric nor on the details of the boundary. The central charge

c=\frac{1}{\kappa}

(9.49)

must be positive (for the unitarity of the associated

[
73
,
74
]
), and for this reason the coupling constant of the Chern-Simons theory has to be positive as well

\kappa>0\ .

(9.50)

Notice that this algebraic method is the only way to determine the sign of the Chern-Simons coupling constant, since this theory, being topological, has vanishing stress-energy tensor, and hence the usual argument based on the positivity of the energy density cannot be applied here.

9.2
2D boundary theory

The 2D action

In Section
9.1
, we identified the solution of the conserved current equation (
9.28
) as the boundary
. This will allow us to find the 2D dynamics on the boundary, in fact we can express the equal time commutation relation (
9.47
) in terms of the boundary field
\Phi(X)
, by using (
9.30
)

[\partial_{\theta}\Phi(X),\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\partial_{\theta^{\prime}}\Phi(X^{\prime})]=i\frac{1}{\kappa}\;\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ ,

(9.51)

which, rewritten as

[\Phi(X),\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\partial_{\theta^{\prime}}\Phi(X^{\prime})]=i\;\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ ,

(9.52)

can be interpreted as a canonical commutation relation in curved spacetime

[q(t,x),p(t,x^{\prime})]=i\;\tilde{\delta}(x-x^{\prime})\ ,

(9.53)

with
p(t,x)
a density
[
105
]
, provided that we match the canonical variables of the 2D theory as follows

q(X)\equiv\Phi(X)\quad;\quad p(X)\equiv\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\kappa\partial_{\theta}\Phi(X)\ .

(9.54)

Here again, the presence of the
\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}
factor in (
9.54
) is of great importance, since it makes
p(X)
a scalar density, according to the standard definition
[
113
]
. Once identified the canonical variables, we can begin the search for the induced boundary theory, by writing the most general Lagrangian compatible with power counting. Noting that the scalar field has vanishing mass dimension, we find

\mathcal{L}_{2D}=\sqrt{-\gamma}\;(a^{ij}\partial_{i}\Phi\partial_{j}\Phi+b^{i}\partial_{i}\Phi+c)\ ,

(9.55)

where the coefficients
a^{ij},\ b^{i},\ c

1.

must be tensor quantities,
i.e.
symmetric tensor of rank (2,0), contravariant vector and scalar respectively, in order that the Lagrangian (
9.55
) is a scalar density  ;

2.

must have mass dimension 0, 1 and 2, respectively  ;

3.

may depend on the scalar field
\Phi(X)
, the metric
\gamma_{ij}(X)
and/or its determinant
\gamma(X)
(but not on their derivatives), since in 2D the scalar field
\Phi(X)
, like the metric, is dimensionless, and thus the power counting is preserved. In other terms, the 2D action (
9.55
) is written as a derivative expansion.

We also notice that the scalar field
\Phi(X)
is defined by means of (
9.30
) up to a shift transformation

\delta_{shift}\Phi=\alpha\ ,

(9.56)

with
\alpha
constant, which implies that the action

S_{2D}=\int d^{2}X\;{\cal L}_{2D}

(9.57)

describing the boundary theory should possess the same symmetry as well,
i.e.

\delta_{shift}S_{2D}=0\;.

(9.58)

An immediate consequence of the shift symmetry (
9.58
) is that
a^{ij}
and
c
must be constant with respect to
\Phi
. For what concerns
b^{i}
, it is easily seen that it may admit a linear dependence on the scalar field. In fact, if
b^{i}=b_{1}^{i}+b_{2}^{i}\Phi
, where
b_{1}^{i}
and
b_{2}^{i}
do not depend on
\Phi
, but may depend on the induced metric
\gamma_{ij}
and on its determinant
\gamma
, we have that

\delta_{shift}\int d^{2}X\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;b^{i}\partial_{i}\Phi=\int d^{2}X\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;\frac{\partial b^{i}}{\partial\Phi}\alpha\partial_{i}\Phi=\alpha\int d^{2}X\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;\nabla_{i}\left(\frac{\partial b^{i}}{\partial\Phi}\Phi\right)\ ,

(9.59)

which is a vanishing boundary term.
Therefore, the corresponding term in the 2D Lagrangian (
9.55
) does not contribute, being a boundary term as well.

\int d^{2}X\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;b^{i}\partial_{i}\Phi=\int d^{2}X\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;(b_{1}^{i}\partial_{i}\Phi+b_{2}^{i}\Phi\partial_{i}\Phi)=\int d^{2}X\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;\nabla_{i}(b_{1}^{i}\Phi+\tfrac{1}{2}b_{2}^{i}\Phi^{2})\ .

(9.60)

The most general 2D action satisfying the shift symmetry is

\begin{split}S_{2D}&=\int d^{2}X\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;(a^{ij}\partial_{i}\Phi\partial_{j}\Phi+c)\\
&=\int d^{2}X\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;\left(a_{0}\partial_{t}\Phi\partial_{t}\Phi+2a_{1}\partial_{t}\Phi\partial_{\theta}\Phi+a_{2}\partial_{\theta}\Phi\partial_{\theta}\Phi+c\right),\end{split}

(9.61)

where
a_{0}\equiv a^{00}
,
a_{1}\equiv a^{02}
and
a_{2}\equiv a^{22}
. The last term does not contribute to the equations of motion of the scalar field
\Phi
and will be omitted. The most general dependence of the dimensionless
a^{ij}
on the induced metric
\gamma^{ij}
is

a^{ij}=\alpha^{ij}(\gamma)+\beta(\gamma)\;\gamma^{ij}\ ,

(9.62)

where
\alpha^{ij}(\gamma)
and
\beta(\gamma)
may depend at most on the metric determinant. What is left now is to verify under which conditions the Lagrangian
{\cal L}_{2D}
is compatible with the canonical variables identified from the
algebra through (
9.54
). This is reached by requiring

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{2D}}{\partial\dot{q}}=p=\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\kappa\partial_{\theta}\Phi\ .

(9.63)

The l.h.s. of (
9.63
) is

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{2D}}{\partial\dot{q}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{2D}}{\partial(\partial_{t}\Phi)}=\sqrt{-\gamma}(2a_{0}\partial_{t}\Phi+2a_{1}\partial_{\theta}\Phi)\ ,

(9.64)

therefore we must ask

a_{0}=0\quad;\quad a_{1}=\tilde{\kappa}\quad\quad\;(a_{2}\ \mbox{free})\ ,

(9.65)

where
\tilde{\kappa}
is the scalar function related to the Chern-Simons coupling constant through (
9.16
). Eq.(
9.65
) represents a constraint on the metric dependence of
a^{ij}
in (
9.62
), in fact it must be

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=a_{0}=a^{00}=\alpha^{00}+\beta\;\gamma^{00}

(9.66)

\displaystyle\tilde{\kappa}

\displaystyle=a_{1}=a^{02}=\alpha^{02}+\beta\;\gamma^{02}\ .

(9.67)

Since we do not want to impose any unnecessary condition on the components of the induced metric
\gamma^{ij}
, that leads to the request

\beta=0\ ,

(9.68)

therefore

a_{2}=a^{22}=\alpha^{22}(\gamma)\ ,

(9.69)

which means that
a_{2}
is a free parameter depending at most on the metric determinant :
a_{2}=a_{2}(\gamma)
. Hence, up to terms which do not contribute to the equation of motion of the scalar field
\Phi(X)
, the action
S_{2D}
(
9.57
) is

S_{2D}=\int d^{2}X\sqrt{-\gamma}\;\left(\tilde{\kappa}\partial_{t}\Phi+a_{2}\partial_{\theta}\Phi\right)\partial_{\theta}\Phi\ .

(9.70)

9.3
Holographic contact

From the action
S_{2D}
(
9.70
) we get the following

\partial_{\theta}\left(\partial_{t}\Phi+\frac{a_{2}}{\tilde{\kappa}}\;\partial_{\theta}\Phi\right)=0\;,

(9.71)

where we divided by the Chern-Simons coupling constant
\kappa
. Our task is now to find under which conditions the
(
9.17
), written in terms of the boundary field
\Phi(X)
by means of (

C=0
(
9.33

\partial_{t}\Phi+v\,\partial_{\theta}\Phi=0\ ,

(9.72)

can be related to the
(
9.71
).
The
(

)
is the equation of a chiral boson whose velocity

\gamma^{ij}
through the coefficients
T^{ij}

(

v
appearing in the
(
9.17
) as the chiral velocity of the edge modes living on the boundary of Chern-Simons theory, which are known, in the framework of
, to be measurable quantities. In most Hall systems, the observed chiral velocity is constant, and this is achieved by a Chern-Simons theory built on a flat 3D spacetime with planar boundary
[
87
]
. The novelty we are finding here, is that
v
is now a
local
quantity, in particular depending on time, which is a consequence of considering the Chern-Simons theory on a curved, instead of flat, spacetime. This corresponds to
accelerated
edge chiral bosons, which have been indeed recently observed, and which cannot be explained by putting a boundary on a flat background. The fact that
v
is a phenomenological parameter requires that it should be determined by experimental inputs, hence, as stressed at the beginning of the Section, a choice of coordinates, which would set
v
to an arbitrary, possibly constant, or even vanishing, value, should not be done
a\ priori
. We might rather say that the
right
choice of coordinates is that of the laboratory frame where the chiral local velocity is measured.
Compatibility between the
(
9.72

9.71

\equiv\partial_{\theta}

7

\rho(t,\theta)\equiv\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}\Phi(t,\theta)
. In any case, the following condition must hold

v=\frac{a_{2}}{\tilde{\kappa}}\ .

(9.73)

Eq.(
9.73
) relates the
parameters of the two theories, thus establishing a (holographic) link between the bulk (
v,\tilde{\kappa}
) and the induced boundary (
a_{2}
).
From (

appearing in the

.
Eq. (

for the holographic bulk-boundary contact to be possible. Therefore the edge velocity depends on the
for the Chern-Simons gauge field.
The issue of the determination of the chiral velocities has been discussed in details by Wen (in
[
96
]
and
[
97
]
for instance), who remarked that it cannot be determined from the bulk action (nor be rescaled to an arbitrary value), and that it is thus appropriate to take the velocity

as a phenomenological parameter. This is the phenomenological counterpart of the considerations made in
[
114
]
: Chern-Simons theory provides merely an effective large-distance description which captures the general symmetries of the manybody theory and has, as such, intrinsic limitations. For instance, it should not be expected to capture certain details such as the velocity of the edge chiral bosons. In
[
114
]
it is correctly argued that
the determination of the chiral velocity should come from gauge-breaking terms present in the full Lagrangian, which, however cannot be identified with the gauge fixing term, since physical results should not depend on the gauge choice, hopefully. In our approach, it is clear that gauge-breaking term present in the full Lagrangian thanks to which the
acquires local degrees of freedom is
S_{bd}
(2.5).
It is the breaking of the gauge symmetry which starts the game: the breaking of the gauge symmetry due to the boundary manifests itself in the breaking of the Ward identity (
9.27
), which yields the irrotationality condition (
9.28
), which identifies the local bosonic boundary degrees of freedom. It is from the broken Ward identity that the algebra (
9.47
) is derived, and then all the rest follows, as described. None of these results depend on the particular gauge choice, as we already remarked.
The 2D action (
9.70
) can now be entirely written in terms of the parameters appearing in the 3D bulk theory (
9.10
) as follows

S_{2D}=\frac{\kappa}{2}\int d^{2}X\;\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\;\left(\partial_{t}\Phi+v\,\partial_{\theta}\Phi\right)\partial_{\theta}\Phi\ ,

(9.74)

where
\kappa
is the Chern-Simons coupling constant and

(

\gamma(t,\theta)
.
We observe that the only dependence of the action
S_{2D}
on the bulk metric is concealed in

.
We might say that, in this sense, the metric form of (
9.74

[

]
, relevant example of the bosonization phenomenon, for which the density operator
n(t,\theta)

n(t,\theta)=:\psi^{\dagger}(t,\theta)\,\psi(t,\theta):\ .

(9.75)

We have seen for the flat case discussed in Section
7
that bosonization,
i.e.
fermion/boson correspondence, is achieved through the identification

n(t,\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}\Phi(t,\theta)\ ,

(9.76)

where
\Phi(X)
is just the chiral boson (

[n(t,\theta),n(t,\theta^{\prime})]=i\frac{\nu}{2\pi}\partial_{\theta}\delta(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ ,

(9.77)

where

is the filling factor of the

is exactly the

as the density operator

(

Here we also notice how the boundary theory makes sense for
any

on

. Indeed, from the perspective of (

\partial_{t}n+\partial_{\theta}(vn)=0\ ,

(9.78)

which represents the continuity equation of a 1D fluid of velocity

As a consequence, we can associate the parameters of the action

\kappa=\frac{1}{2\pi\nu}\;.

(9.79)

Hence, from (

\Phi(X)
.
Notice that, because of (

a_{2}
