), being
\tilde{\kappa}
positive due to the positivity of the central charge. This is in agreement with
[
89
]
, where the edge excitations were studied
on a disc and on a cylinder,
i.e.
on curved boundaries in flat spacetime, which belong to the cases studied in this Section. It is interesting to remark that we recover those results, in particular the algebra and the Luttinger theory, in a quite different way.

9.4
Summary of results and discussion

The aim of the original work presented in this Section was to understand if the geometry of the bulk spacetime affected in some way the boundary physics of Chern-Simons theory, which, in the flat case, we have seen to reproduce, on the boundary, the theory of edge states of the
. Naively, starting from a
one would expect a mild dependence on the bulk metric. Instead, our analysis shows that the velocity of the edge chiral boson indirectly depends on the bulk metric, through the determinant of the induced metric. Our work might therefore be the first step towards a theoretical framework for the recently observed accelerated chiral boson on the edge of particular Hall systems, which cannot be explained by the usual description in terms of flat Chern-Simons theory with boundary. On the other hand, on the boundary of Chern-Simons theory we find the usual
algebraic structure of the flat background case, with the same central charge proportional to the inverse of the Chern-Simons coupling. Therefore, the
algebra appears to be insensitive to both the bulk metric and the type of boundary (at least, it is the same for planar and radial boundary). What is certainly true is that the Chern-Simons bulk theory in flat spacetime, when considered with a boundary, generates local
on the 2D induced theory, corresponding to the edge states of
, with
constant
chiral velocities. The possibility of local velocities is not captured by the flat approach alone.

A few remarks are in order.

•

Technical remarks :
the determinant of the metric depends on the coordinates that are chosen, and locally one might always find coordinates such that the determinant is a constant. Moreover, in 2+1 dimensions the boundary of Chern-Simons theory is well known to be a
, hence depends only upon the conformal class of the boundary metric, and in every conformal class there is a metric of constant determinant. This would correspond to choosing a reference frame where the velocity of the chiral boson is normalized at a given constant value. This represents a good, physical reason for not choosing a metric with constant determinant. The boundary conformal structure is ensured by the existence of an algebraic structure of the
type. Had we finished here, we would not have a good reason for not choosing, amongst the metrics in
, those with constant determinant. This would have been a without loss of generality choice, and we would have achieved our result as far as only the conformal structure described by the boundary algebra is concerned. But this is only half of our results : on the
planar
boundary of Chern-Simons theory with
flat
bulk metric it is known to exist the Luttinger theory of the chiral boson. We derived the corresponding action
following the procedure described in Section
9.2
. The action (
9.74
) is not invariant under change of coordinates, which hence is not allowed at this stage. Nonetheless, the Luttinger theory which we found on the boundary of the Chern-Simons theory with non-flat metric is not
independent from its bulk counterpart. We would not have found this result if, in the previous step, we had frozen the bulk metric by choosing coordinates with constant determinant (or a reference frame where the chiral velocity is constant). Instead, a memory of the bulk metric survives, in the velocity of the chiral boson, which depends only on the determinant of the bulk metric. Not having made the choice of constant determinant leaves therefore the possibility of highlighting the residual dependence on the bulk metric, and also of selecting the bulk metric by means of a physical input, which is the velocity of the edge chiral bosons, which is a physical observable. This is not so different from what is usually done with the Chern-Simons coupling constant, which is fixed by means of the physical requirements of the incompressible Hall fluid, by normalizing the external current coupled to the electromagnetic gauge field to the known relationship between Chern-Simons coupling constant and filling factor
\kappa=\frac{1}{2\pi\nu}
as in (
7.3
). Yet, speaking of a coupling constant for the
abelian
Chern-Simons theory is rather inappropriate, since it is always possible to reabsorb the coupling constant by means of a redefinition of the gauge fields. Only in the non-abelian case a true coupling constant for Chern-Simons theory exists. Nevertheless, normalizing to one the Chern-Simons coupling constant would be equivalent to loosing the entire structure of Landau levels of
(F)QHE
, and for that reason we keep it alive. We see an analogy between the two situations: as we do not normalize to one the Chern-Simons coupling constant in the abelian case in order to describe the filling factors of the
, for the same reason we do not choose coordinates such that the determinant is constant, in order to keep the possibility of fixing the bulk metric by measuring the (possibly time dependent) velocity of the edge chiral boson.

•

The phenomenological approach :
it is useful to make a comparison with the approach usually adopted in the Literature to describe non-constant velocities of the edge excitations in the Hall systems. It is known that the fermions in a quantum Hall droplet are constrained by a confining potential whose gradient determines the velocity of the chiral edge modes
[
98
]
. For instance, the confinement may involve potentials that depend on space and/or time due to deformations or interactions in the sample
[
99
]
and typically, in the phenomenological framework, one can invoke these considerations in order to take into account non-constant chiral velocities, which therefore, in the microscopic model, depend on the different possible potentials in the sample.
In general, the paradigmatic way to describe the edge excitations has been illustrated by Wen
[
96
,
97
,
100
]
, and well summarized in
[
98
]
. One starts with a 3D Chern-Simons action in
flat
spacetime, from which the bulk
are eliminated by integrating out the time-component of the gauge field
A_{0}(x)
. Then, the irrotational constraint on the spatial components of the gauge field is
imposed
:
\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{A}=0
. The boundary
is then identified by the scalar field characterizing the solution
\vec{A}=\vec{\nabla}\Phi
. Finally, the “appropriate effective action” at the edge is written as the sum of two terms: the Luttinger action, describing a chiral boson with
constant
velocity, to which is possibly added an interaction term, which depends on the form of the edge confining potential and on the details of the electron-electron interactions. If this latter interaction is assumed to be local, this obviously implies non-constant velocities. Usually, to simplify the description, the interactions are taken as piecewise functions, in order to take into account, for instance, the screening effects which are present in the leads. More details can be found in
[
116
]
and in
[
117
]
. This latter paper is related to an interesting experiment
[
118
]
. Other experiments are described in
[
119
,
120
]
.
To make it short, the total interaction is split into two parts : a constant velocity piece
v
contained in the Luttinger action and an additional local velocity term in the interaction. This is the framework for the description of edge excitations with non-constant velocities. Alternatively, according to our approach, there is no need of
imposing
the irrotational condition which gives rise to the scalar boundary degree of freedom. Rather, it emerges as the breaking term of the Ward identity (
9.27
), evaluated on shell. Similarly, the 2D action (
9.74
) is not
chosen
as an effective action, but is recovered by means of a general procedure which we described as an “holografic contact” between the
on the bulk gauge field and the
of the 2D scalar field. Finally, we showed that a bulk Chern-Simons model on curved spacetime yields a possible
non
-
constant
chiral velocity, where the locality property is inherited solely from the induced metric determinant from a bulk-boundary correspondence.
An intriguing way to interpret this fact, in relation to the microscopic models, is to consider it as an equivalent description : a change of potential can be effectively encoded in the Chern-Simons theory by a bulk metric. As the Chern-Simons coupling constant
\kappa
encodes all the possible values of the filling factor
\nu
, similarly the Chern-Simons bulk metric encodes the spacetime properties of the (confining) potential, in terms of the induced metric determinant.

•

On current experiments :
one of the main results of this Thesis is that the measurable chiral velocity
v
of the edge modes is related not only to the Chern-Simons level
\kappa
, as it is known already for a flat background, but also to the determinant of the metric, through which
v
can acquire a time dependence, which can be measured.
It is indeed possible to detect time dependent edge chiral velocity, as well as to investigate non-standard geometries (for what concerns the quantum Hall effect). In
[
48
]
the boundary of a Hall system with
\nu=2
is considered. The two channels (left and right) interact, and the problem diagonalizes into one fast and one slow mode, and the velocity is not constant and is measured. For what concerns non conventional bulk geometries, recently, in
[
121
]
,
the
has been observed in graphene. Here, the electrons live on a two-dimensional “suspended” membrane made by carbon atoms. There are some differences with respect to the standard case (Dirac-type linear dispersion relation rather than quadratic, presence of an additional degree of freedom like a pseudo-spin, for instance), which makes the phenomenology similar to that of
, but with different values of the plateaux. The nice thing is that graphene technically allows to make samples with various shapes like Corbino discs, and observations seem sensitive to the bulk geometry. Moreover, always using graphene, there are attempts to realize quantum Hall systems in curved space, like described in

[
122
]
and in
[
123
]
. Now, we do not know if the results presented in this Section can immediately be applied to these particular experimental sets, but the remarkable fact is that a recent experimental research activity exists, which concerns the main topic of this Chapter : the possibility of having boundary chiral modes whose time dependent velocities are sensitive to the bulk geometry.

10
BF description of 2D accelerated chiral edge modes

This Section, which concerns the study of the 3D BF model in curved space as a natural continuation of the results obtained in the previous Section for Chern-Simons, is organized as follows

In particular in Section
10.1
we prepare the tools to face the problem : we introduce a radial boundary in the gauge-fixed 3D BF action, and derive à la Symanzik the most general
on the two gauge fields of the theory. As we said, the boundary breaks gauge invariance, and this reflects in the breakings of the two Ward identities describing the broken gauge symmetry. The breaking are particularly fruitful, as they lead us to identify the 2D scalar degrees of freedom and the
algebra formed by the edge conserved currents. Requiring the positivity of the
central charge constrains the BF coupling to be positive. In Section
10.2
the 2D theory is derived as the holographic projection of the 3D bulk theory. The contact is realized in Section
10.3
by matching the
on the bulk side and the
on the boundary side. The resulting action involves two scalar fields and is more complicated than the simple Luttinger theory found on the edge of Chern-Simons model. We give a physical interpretation of the 2D theory, and we find three possibilities for the motion of the edge quasiparticles: same directions, opposite directions and a single-moving mode. But, requiring that the Hamiltonian of the 2D theory is bounded by below, the case of edge modes moving in the same direction is ruled out. We are therefore left with physical situations characterized by edge excitations moving with opposite velocities, (examples are
with
\nu=1-1/n
, with
n
positive integer
[
124
]
, and Helical Luttinger Liquids phenomena
[
125
]
)
or a single-moving mode (Quantum Anomalous Hall
[
22
,
126
,
127
]
). In Section
10.4
a strong restriction is obtained by requiring Time Reversal symmetry, which uniquely identifies modes with equal and opposite velocities, and we know that this is the case of Topological Insulators. The novelty, with respect to the flat bulk background, is that the modes have local velocities, which corresponds to
with
accelerated
edge modes. In Section
10.5
we summarize and discuss our results.

10.1
The model : bulk and boundary

The action

We consider the abelian 3D BF model on a manifold diffeomorphic to a cylinder of radius
R
. The boundary is introduced by means of a Heaviside step function in the bulk action, constraining the radial coordinate to
r\leq R
. The BF bulk action is

S_{BF}=\kappa\int d^{3}x\,\theta(R-r)\,\tilde{\epsilon}^{\mu\nu\rho}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}B_{\rho}\ ,

(10.1)

where
A_{\mu}(x)
and
B_{\mu}(x)
are two gauge fields with mass dimensions
[A]=[B]=1
and
\kappa
is a constant which will be determined by physical inputs, as we shall see later. Differently from the previous case, in this Section we decide not to fix
(we will see that indeed this does not affect the final results). According to this the properties of distributions in general curved spacetimes (such as the derivative of the Heaviside step function) are given in Appendix
10.A
. We choose the radial gauge, implemented by the following gauge-fixing term

S_{gf}=\int d^{3}x\sqrt{-g}\,\theta(R-r)\left[\left(bA_{\mu}+d\;B_{\mu}\right)n^{\mu}\right]\ ,

(10.2)

where
n^{\mu}=(0,1,0)
is a vector and
b(x),\ d(x)
are scalar Nakanishi-Lautrup Lagrange multipliers
[
107
,
108
]
:

\frac{\delta S}{\delta b}=n^{\mu}A_{\mu}=A_{r}=0\quad;\quad\frac{\delta S}{\delta d}=n^{\mu}B_{\mu}=B_{r}=0\ .

(10.3)

The external source term is

S_{ext}=\int d^{3}x\sqrt{-g}\,\theta(R-r)\,\left(J^{\mu}A_{\mu}+\hat{J}^{\mu}B_{\mu}\right)\ ,

(10.4)

where
J^{\mu}(x)
and
\hat{J}^{\mu}(x)
are vectors. The presence of a boundary induces, as an additional contribution, the most general boundary term compatible with power-counting and locality
[
10
]

S_{bd}=\int d^{3}x\,\sqrt{-\gamma}\,\delta(r-R)\left(\frac{\alpha^{ij}}{2}A_{i}A_{j}+\frac{\beta^{ij}}{2}B_{i}B_{j}+\zeta^{ij}A_{i}B_{j}\right)\ ,

(10.5)

where
\alpha^{ij}=\alpha^{ji},\ \beta^{ij}=\beta^{ji}
and
\zeta^{ij}
are dimensionless tensors which depend, at most, on the induced metric (components
\gamma^{ij}
and/or determinant
\gamma
) in the following way

\displaystyle\alpha^{ij}=

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}^{ij}({\gamma})+\hat{\alpha}({\gamma})\gamma^{ij}

\displaystyle=\hat{\alpha}^{ij}+\hat{\alpha}\gamma^{ij}

(10.6)

\displaystyle\beta^{ij}=

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}^{ij}({\gamma})+\hat{\beta}({\gamma})\gamma^{ij}

\displaystyle=\hat{\beta}^{ij}+\hat{\beta}\gamma^{ij}

(10.7)

\displaystyle\zeta^{ij}=

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}^{ij}({\gamma})+\hat{\zeta}({\gamma})\gamma^{ij}

\displaystyle=\hat{\zeta}^{ij}+\hat{\zeta}\gamma^{ij}\ .

(10.8)

Thus, as in the previously discussed Chern-Simons case, a non-covariant boundary term has been chosen. Notice that covariance could be recovered by taking
\hat{\alpha}^{ij}=\hat{\beta}^{ij}=\hat{\zeta}^{ij}=0
. In the flat limit the parameters
\alpha^{ij}
,
\beta^{ij}
and
\zeta^{ij}
in
S_{bd}
are constant. Finally, the total action, containing BF bulk, gauge fixing, external sources and boundary terms, is

S=S_{BF}+S_{gf}+S_{ext}+S_{bd}\ .

(10.9)

Equations of motion and boundary conditions

From the total action
S
(
10.9
), the
of the theory follow

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\theta(R-r)\left[-\kappa\epsilon^{\mu\lambda\rho}\partial_{\mu}B_{\rho}+b\,n^{\lambda}+J^{\lambda}\right]+\delta^{\lambda}_{i}\tfrac{\sqrt{-\gamma}}{\sqrt{-g}}\delta(r-R)\left[-\kappa\frac{\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}B_{j}+\alpha^{ij}A_{j}+\,\zeta^{ij}B_{j}\right]=0

(10.10)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta B_{\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\theta(R-r)\left[\kappa\epsilon^{\mu\nu\lambda}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}+d\,n^{\lambda}+\hat{J}^{\lambda}\right]+\delta^{\lambda}_{i}\tfrac{\sqrt{-\gamma}}{\sqrt{-g}}\delta(r-R)\left[\beta^{ij}B_{j}+\zeta^{ji}A_{j}\right]=0\ .

(10.11)

From the
(
10.10
), (
10.11
) we get the
of the theory by applying the operator
\lim_{\epsilon\to R}\int^{R}_{\epsilon}
,
i.e.

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to R}\int^{R}_{\epsilon}dr\eqref{BF-2.9}:\qquad

\displaystyle\left.\alpha^{ij}A_{j}+\left(-\kappa\frac{\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}+\,\zeta^{ij}\right)B_{j}\right|_{r=R}=0

(10.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to R}\int^{R}_{\epsilon}dr\eqref{BF-2.12}:\qquad

\displaystyle\left.\zeta^{ji}A_{j}+\beta^{ij}B_{j}\right|_{r=R}=0\ .

(10.13)

Eq.(
10.12
) and (
10.13
) can be written in a compact form as follows

\left.\Lambda^{IJ}X_{J}\right|_{r=R}=0\ ,

(10.14)

where
I,J=\{i;j\}=\{0,2;0,2\}
,

\Lambda^{IJ}\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha^{ij}&\zeta^{ij}-\kappa\epsilon^{i1j}\\
\zeta^{ji}&\beta^{ij}\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\alpha^{00}&\alpha^{02}&\zeta^{00}&\zeta^{02}-\hat{\kappa}\\
\alpha^{20}&\alpha^{22}&\zeta^{20}+\hat{\kappa}&\zeta^{22}\\
\zeta^{00}&\zeta^{20}&\beta^{00}&\beta^{02}\\
\zeta^{02}&\zeta^{22}&\beta^{02}&\beta^{22}\end{array}\right)\ ,

(10.15)

and

X_{J}\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A_{j}\\
B_{j}\end{array}\right)\ ,

(10.16)

where we defined

\hat{\kappa}\equiv\frac{\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}\ .

(10.17)

Here again we leave
\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}
explicit (instead of simply putting it equal to 1) to keep explicit the tensor nature of all quantities. For instance, in this way, it is immediate to see that
\hat{\kappa}
is a scalar function. The
(
10.14
) defines a linear, homogeneous system of four equations and four variables
A_{i}|_{r=R}
and
B_{i}|_{r=R}
, for which, requiring
\det\Lambda=0
, it is possible to write three of the fields in terms of one:

\displaystyle B_{\theta}(X)

\displaystyle=-l_{1}B_{t}(X)

(10.18)

\displaystyle A_{\theta}(X)

\displaystyle=-l_{2}B_{t}(X)

(10.19)

\displaystyle A_{t}(X)

\displaystyle=-l_{3}B_{t}(X)\ ,

(10.20)

where
l_{1,2,3}
depend on the coefficients of the total action (
10.9
) and therefore, in general, are local functions of the induced metric on the boundary
\gamma_{ij}(X)
. Their explicit form can be found in Appendix
11.A
((
11.A.8
), (
11.A.9
) and (
11.A.10
)). Notice that to exclude Dirichlet-like solutions (
i.e.\ A_{i}|_{r=R}=B_{i}|_{r=R}=0
), which would trivialize the boundary 2D physics, we must require
l_{i}\neq 0
and
l_{i}^{-1}\neq 0
.

Ward identities

The covariant divergence of the
(
10.10
) is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\lambda}\frac{\delta S}{\delta A_{\lambda}}=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\lambda}\left[\theta(R-r)\left(-\kappa\epsilon^{\mu\lambda\rho}\partial_{\mu}B_{\rho}+n^{\lambda}\,b+J^{\lambda}\right)\right]

(10.21)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\delta(r-R)\,\kappa\,\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}B_{j}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\lambda}\left[\theta(R-r)n^{\lambda}\,b\sqrt{-g}\right]+\nabla_{\lambda}\left[\theta(R-r)J^{\lambda}\right]=0\ ,

where we used the
(
10.12
), the fact that
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\nabla_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}B_{\rho}=0
, the definition of covariant derivative of the step function (
10.A.14
) and the formula for the covariant divergence (
9.24
)

\nabla_{\mu}V^{\mu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}\left(V^{\mu}\sqrt{-g}\right)\ .

(10.22)

By multiplying (
10.21
) by
\sqrt{-g}
and integrating over the coordinate normal to the boundary
r=R
, we get

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{+\infty}dr\,\left\{\delta(r-R)\,\kappa\,\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}B_{j}+\partial_{\lambda}\left[\theta(R-r)n^{\lambda}\,b\sqrt{-g}\right]+\sqrt{-g}\,\nabla_{\lambda}\left[\theta(R-r)J^{\lambda}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left.\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}B_{j}\right|_{r=R}-\cancel{b\,\sqrt{-g}|_{r=0}}+\int_{0}^{+\infty}dr\,\sqrt{-g}\,\nabla_{\lambda}\left[\theta(R-r)J^{\lambda}\right]\ ,

(10.23)

where we adopted the convention according to which the
\theta
components of the fields, their
\theta
-derivatives and the Lagrange multipliers vanish at
r=0
:

\left.A_{\theta}=B_{\theta}=\partial_{\theta}A_{t}=\partial_{\theta}B_{t}=b=d\right|_{r=0}=0\ .

(10.24)

A comment here may be useful : the invariant measure for integrating along
r
can be identified as induced from the bulk, as follows

\int d^{3}x\sqrt{-g}\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})=\int d^{3}x\sqrt{-g}\frac{\tilde{\delta}^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}=\int dr\frac{\sqrt{-g}}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}=\int dr\,\sqrt{g_{rr}}\ ,

(10.25)

where we used (
10.A.12
), and
X=(t,\theta)
are the boundary coordinates. However, in the specific case of (
10.23
), the
\sqrt{g_{rr}}
factor in the integration can be omitted, simply dividing (
10.21
) by
\sqrt{g_{rr}}
. Using (
10.A.14
) in (
10.23
), we finally find

\int_{0}^{R}dr\,\sqrt{-g}\nabla_{\lambda}J^{\lambda}=\left(-\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}B_{j}+\sqrt{-g}J^{r}\right)_{r=R}\ ,

(10.26)

which is the Ward identity corresponding to the gauge transformation of the gauge field
A_{\mu}(x)
, broken at its r.h.s. by the presence of the boundary. By applying the same procedure to the
(
10.11
), with
d|_{r=0}=0
, we obtain a second broken Ward identity :

\int_{0}^{R}dr\,\sqrt{-g}\nabla_{\lambda}\hat{J}^{\lambda}=\left(-\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}A_{j}+\sqrt{-g}\hat{J}^{r}\right)_{r=R}\ .

(10.27)

From (
10.26
) and (
10.27
), going on-shell (
i.e.\ J=\hat{J}=0
), we find

\displaystyle\left.\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}B_{j}\right|_{r=R}=

\displaystyle 0

(10.28)

\displaystyle\left.\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}A_{j}\right|_{r=R}=

\displaystyle 0\ ,

(10.29)

which describe two conserved currents on the boundary
r=R
. The most general solutions to these equations are
[
111
,
112
]

\displaystyle B_{i}(X)=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\psi(X)+\delta_{i2}\hat{C}

(10.30)

\displaystyle A_{i}(X)=

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\varphi(X)+\delta_{i2}C\ ,

(10.31)

where
C
and
\hat{C}
are two constants and
\varphi(X)
and
\psi(X)
are scalar boundary fields with zero mass dimensions,
i.e.\ [\varphi]=[\psi]=0
, which should be identified with the boundary
. Being on a closed, periodic boundary, we need to specify periodicity conditions

\displaystyle A_{i}(t,\theta)=A_{i}(t,\theta+2\pi)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\varphi(t,\theta)=\varphi(t,\theta+2\pi)

(10.32)

\displaystyle B_{i}(t,\theta)=B_{i}(t,\theta+2\pi)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\psi(t,\theta)=\psi(t,\theta+2\pi)\ .

(10.33)

The values of the constants
C
and
\hat{C}
in (
10.30
), (
10.31
) are found by applying the mean value theorem for holomorphic functions, as in the previous Section
9
, which states that if
f
is analytic in a region
D
, and
a\in D
, then
f(a)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\oint_{{\cal C}(a)}f
, where
{\cal C}(a)
is a circle centered in
a
. In our case taking for
\cal C
the circular boundary
r=R
centered at
r=0
allows us to write

A_{\theta}(t,r=0,\theta)=\oint_{ring\;R}A_{\theta}(x)=\cancel{\left.\varphi(t,\theta)\right|_{\theta=0}^{\theta=2\pi}}+2\pi\;C\ .

(10.34)

From the requirement
A_{\theta}(t,r=0,\theta)=0
, it follows that

C=0\ .

(10.35)

Analogously, we also get

\hat{C}=0\ .

(10.36)

Algebra

The generating functional of the connected Green functions
Z_{c}[J,\hat{J}]
is defined

e^{iZ_{c}[J,\hat{J}]}=\int[dA][dB][db][dd]e^{iS[A,B,b,d;J,\hat{J}]}\ ,

(10.37)

from which the 1- and 2-points Green functions are derived

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\delta Z_{c}[J]}{\delta J^{i}(x)}\right|_{J=0}=

\displaystyle\;\langle A_{i}(x)\rangle

(10.38)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\delta^{(2)}Z_{c}[J]}{\delta J^{i}(x)\delta J^{j}(x^{\prime})}\right|_{J=0}\equiv

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}(x,x^{\prime})=i\langle T(A_{i}(x)A_{j}(x^{\prime}))\rangle\ ,

(10.39)

where
T
is the time-ordered product

\langle T(A_{l}(x)A_{j}(x^{\prime}))\rangle\equiv\theta(t-t^{\prime})\langle A_{l}(x)A_{j}(x^{\prime})\rangle+\theta(t^{\prime}-t)\langle A_{j}(x^{\prime})A_{l}(x)\rangle\ .

(10.40)

In order to compute the propagator (
10.39
), we need the following result

\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{i}(x^{\prime})}\nabla_{\lambda}J^{\lambda}(x)=\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{i}(x^{\prime})}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\lambda}\left(J^{\lambda}\sqrt{-g}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{i}\tilde{\delta}^{(3)}(x-x^{\prime})\ ,

(10.41)

where we used (
10.22
) and the relation between scalar and density Dirac delta distributions (
10.A.1
). Taking now the functional derivative with respect to the external sources
J,\hat{J}
of the broken Ward identities (
10.26
) and (
10.27
), we get

\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{k}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{BF-ward1}\;:

\begin{split}\int^{R}_{0}dr\;\partial_{k}\tilde{\delta}^{(3)}(x-x^{\prime})&=\left.-\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}\frac{\delta^{(2)}Z_{c}}{\delta J^{k}(X^{\prime})\delta\hat{J}^{j}(X)}\right|_{J=\hat{J}=0}\\
\partial_{k}\tilde{\delta}^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})&=-i\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}\langle T\left(A_{k}(X^{\prime})B_{j}(X)\right)\rangle\\
&=-i\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[B_{\theta}(X),A_{k}(X^{\prime})\right]\partial_{t}\theta(t-t^{\prime})\ ,\end{split}

(10.42)

where we used (
10.39
), (
10.40
) and (
10.41
). Going on-shell,
i.e.
using (
10.28
), we have

\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[B_{\theta}(X),A_{k}(X^{\prime})\right]\partial_{t}\theta(t-t^{\prime})=\frac{i}{\kappa}\partial_{k}\tilde{\delta}^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ .

(10.43)

Setting
k=t
in (
10.43
) and integrating over time, we get the equal time commutator

\left[B_{\theta}(X),A_{t}(X^{\prime})\right]=0\ .

(10.44)

Analogously, choosing
k=\theta
we get

\left.\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[B_{\theta}(X),A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\right]\right|_{t=t^{\prime}}=\frac{i}{\kappa}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ .

(10.45)

We can repeat this to find the whole current algebra

\frac{\delta}{\delta\hat{J}^{k}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{BF-ward1}\;:

\begin{split}0=\left.\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}\frac{\delta^{(2)}Z_{c}}{\delta\hat{J}^{k}(X^{\prime})\delta\hat{J}^{j}(X)}\right|_{J=0}&=\tilde{\epsilon}^{i1j}\partial_{i}\langle T\left(B_{k}(X^{\prime})B_{j}(X)\right)\rangle\\
&=\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[B_{\theta}(X),B_{k}(X^{\prime})\right]\partial_{t}\theta(t-t^{\prime})\ ,\end{split}

(10.46)

which leads to

\left.\left[B_{\theta}(X),B_{k}(X^{\prime})\right]\right|_{t=t^{\prime}}=0\ .

(10.47)

In the same way, from
\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{k}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{BF-ward2}
we get

\left[A_{\theta}(X),A_{k}(X^{\prime})\right]_{t=t^{\prime}}=0\ .

(10.48)

Finally, from
\frac{\delta}{\delta\hat{J}^{k}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{BF-ward2}
we have

\displaystyle\left[A_{\theta}(X),B_{t}(X^{\prime})\right]_{t=t^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle 0

(10.49)

\displaystyle\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[A_{\theta}(X),B_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\right]_{t=t^{\prime}}=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\kappa}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ .

(10.50)

Summarizing, the equal time commutators are

\displaystyle\left[B_{\theta}(X),A_{t}(X^{\prime})\right]=

\displaystyle 0

(10.51)

\displaystyle\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[B_{\theta}(X),A_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\kappa}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})

(10.52)

\displaystyle\left[B_{\theta}(X),B_{k}(X^{\prime})\right]=

\displaystyle 0

(10.53)

\displaystyle\left[A_{\theta}(X),A_{k}(X^{\prime})\right]=

\displaystyle 0

(10.54)

\displaystyle\left[A_{\theta}(X),B_{t}(X^{\prime})\right]=

\displaystyle 0

(10.55)

\displaystyle\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[A_{\theta}(X),B_{\theta}(X^{\prime})\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\kappa}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ .

(10.56)

One can observe that by using the property of the delta function
\delta^{\prime}(x)=-\delta^{\prime}(-x)
, the commutators (
10.52
) and (
10.56
) represent the same relation. Eq.(
10.51
)-(
10.56
) describe a semidirect sum of
algebras
[
26
,
27
]
with central charge

c=\frac{1}{\kappa}\ ,

(10.57)

which, as one can expect, inherit the topological nature of the bulk theory, being independent from the metric. Again we remark that from the positivity of the central charge, being necessary for the unitarity of the

[
73
,
74
]
, we get a constraint on the coupling constant of the BF bulk model

{\kappa}>0\ .

(10.58)

Typically, the constraint on the coupling constants of
is derived by asking the positivity of the energy density, which is the 00-component of the energy momentum tensor
T^{00}
. This cannot be achieved in
, which have vanishing Hamiltonian, as it is well known. The constraint in that case is obtained by asking the positivity of the central charge of the edge current algebra, as in the present case, and in the previous (Section
9
).

10.2
2D boundary theory

We now focus on the construction of the boundary theory, whose
are the boundary scalar fields
\varphi(X)
and
\psi(X)
defined by
the solutions (
10.30
) and (
10.31
) of the conserved currents equations (
10.28
) and (
10.29
). To build the 2D induced theory we follow three steps :

1.

identification of the 2D canonical variables in terms of boundary fields ;

2.

derivation of the most general 2D action ;

3.

bulk-boundary correspondence (holographic contact).

2D canonical variables

The first step is to write the commutator (
10.56
) in terms of the boundary fields
\varphi(X)
,
\psi(X)
, using (
10.30
) and (
10.31
) (with
C=\hat{C}=0
) :

\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[\partial_{\theta}\varphi(X),\partial_{\theta^{\prime}}\psi(X^{\prime})\right]=\frac{i}{\kappa}\partial_{\theta}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\quad\Rightarrow\quad\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[\varphi(X),\partial_{\theta^{\prime}}\psi(X^{\prime})\right]=\frac{i}{\kappa}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ .

(10.59)

Analogously, from (
10.52
) we get

\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left[\psi(X),\partial_{\theta^{\prime}}\varphi(X^{\prime})\right]=\frac{i}{\kappa}\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ .

(10.60)

Both the relations (
10.59
) and (
10.60
) can be interpreted as canonical commutation relations of the type

\left[q(X),p(X^{\prime})\right]=i\tilde{\delta}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\ ,

(10.61)

once the following identifications are done :

\displaystyle q_{1}\equiv\varphi\quad;

\displaystyle\quad p_{1}\equiv\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\partial_{\theta}\psi

(10.62)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}q_{2}\equiv\psi\quad;

\displaystyle\quad\color[rgb]{0,0,0}p_{2}\equiv\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\partial_{\theta}\varphi\ .

(10.63)

Therefore the
of the 2D theory are equivalently described by either of the sets of canonical variables (
10.62
) or (
10.63
).

The 2D action

To find the most general 2D action, we make a derivative expansion in the boundary fields
\varphi(X)
and
\psi(X)
, compatible with power-counting (we remind that
[\varphi]=[\psi]=0
)

\displaystyle S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}X\,\mathcal{L}_{2D}

(10.64)

\displaystyle=\int d^{2}X\,\sqrt{-\gamma}\left(a^{ij}\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+b^{ij}\partial_{i}\psi\partial_{j}\psi+c^{ij}\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\psi+d^{i}\partial_{i}\varphi+f^{i}\partial_{i}\psi+h\right)\ ,

where
a^{ij}=a^{ji},

b^{ij}=b^{ji}
and
c^{ij}
are tensors,
d^{i}
and
f^{i}
are vectors and
h
is a scalar, with mass dimensions

[a]=[b]=[c]=0\quad;\quad[d]=[f]=1\quad;\quad[h]=2\ .

(10.65)

The coefficients appearing in (
10.64
) may depend on the induced metric
\gamma_{ij}(X)
and/or on the boundary fields, but not on their derivatives. The definitions of the scalar fields
\varphi(X)
and
\psi(X)
(
10.30
) and (
10.31
) are invariant under the shift transformations
\delta_{s},\delta^{\prime}_{s}
defined as

\displaystyle\delta_{s}\varphi=

\displaystyle\eta

(10.66)

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{s}\psi=

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}\ .

(10.67)

Consequently, the 2D lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{2D}
in (
10.64
) must be shift-invariant as well

\delta_{s}\mathcal{L}_{2D}=\delta^{\prime}_{s}\mathcal{L}_{2D}=0\ ,

(10.68)

which implies

S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]=\int d^{2}X\,\sqrt{-\gamma}\left(a^{ij}\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\varphi+b^{ij}\partial_{i}\psi\partial_{j}\psi+c^{ij}\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{j}\psi+d^{i}\partial_{i}\varphi+f^{i}\partial_{i}\psi\right)\ ,

(10.69)

with

\begin{split}\frac{\partial a^{ij}}{\partial\varphi}=\frac{\partial b^{ij}}{\partial\varphi}=\frac{\partial c^{ij}}{\partial\varphi}=0&\\
\frac{\partial a^{ij}}{\partial\psi}=\frac{\partial b^{ij}}{\partial\psi}=\frac{\partial c^{ij}}{\partial\psi}=0&\ ,\end{split}

(10.70)

and

\begin{split}&\frac{\partial d^{i}}{\partial\varphi}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{\partial f^{i}}{\partial\varphi}\partial_{i}\psi=0\\
&\frac{\partial d^{i}}{\partial\psi}\partial_{i}\varphi+\frac{\partial f^{i}}{\partial\psi}\partial_{i}\psi=0\ .\end{split}

(10.71)

Hence, the coefficients
d^{i}
and
f^{i}
may still depend on the boundary fields
\varphi(X)
and
\psi(X)
, provided that the constraints (
10.71
) hold.
In
S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]
(
10.69
) we omitted the scalar term
h
because, being metric-dependent only, it does not contribute to the
of the boundary theory.
As we did for
S_{bd}
(
10.5
), we parametrize the metric dependence of the rank-2 tensors as follows :

\displaystyle a^{ij}

\displaystyle=\hat{a}^{ij}+\hat{a}\,\gamma^{ij}

(10.72)

\displaystyle b^{ij}

\displaystyle=\hat{b}^{ij}+\hat{b}\,\gamma^{ij}

(10.73)

\displaystyle c^{ij}

\displaystyle=\hat{c}^{ij}+\hat{c}\,\gamma^{ij}\ ,

(10.74)

where the hat means dependence on the metric determinant at most. As observed for the coefficients of the boundary action
S_{bd}
(
10.5
), in the flat limit the tensors appearing in the action (
10.69
) must reduce to constant matrices, and, in particular,
c^{ij}
to a constant symmetric matrix. The compatibility of the 2D Lagrangian in (
10.69
) with the canonical boundary structure is ensured if the relation

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{2D}}{\partial\dot{q}}=p\ ,

(10.75)

holds for both
q_{1},p_{1}
in (
10.62
) and
q_{2},p_{2}
in (
10.63
), being equivalent descriptions of the boundary
. From (
10.69
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{2D}}{\partial\dot{\varphi}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{-\gamma}\left(2a^{0i}\partial_{i}\varphi+c^{0i}\partial_{i}\psi+d^{0}\right)

(10.76)

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{2D}}{\partial\dot{\psi}}

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\sqrt{-\gamma}\left(2b^{0i}\partial_{i}\psi+c^{i0}\partial_{i}\varphi+f^{0}\right)\ ,

(10.77)

and the request (
10.75
) is fulfilled if

a^{0i}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}b^{0i}=}c^{00}=d^{0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=f^{0}}=0\quad;\quad c^{02}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=c^{20}}=\hat{\kappa}\quad;\quad(a^{22},\ b^{22},\ c^{22}\ \mbox{free})\ ,

(10.78)

where
\hat{\kappa}(\gamma)
has been defined in (
10.17
) and
d^{2},\ f^{2}
are constrained by (
10.71
). Eq.(
10.78
) represents a constraint on the metric dependence of the coefficients (
10.72
)-(
10.74
), for which we have

\begin{split}a^{0i}=\hat{a}^{0i}+\hat{a}\,\gamma^{0i}&=0\quad;\quad c^{00}=\hat{c}^{00}+\hat{c}\,\gamma^{00}=0\\
b^{0i}=\hat{b}^{0i}+\hat{b}\,\gamma^{0i}&=0\quad;\quad c^{02}=\hat{c}^{02}+\hat{c}\,\gamma^{02}=\hat{\kappa}\ .\end{split}

(10.79)

Since we do not want to impose unnecessary conditions on the induced metric
\gamma^{ij}
(we are interested in determining if and how the 2D theory keeps memory of the bulk through the induced metric on the boundary), we must ask

\hat{a}=\hat{b}=\hat{c}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad a^{ij}=\hat{a}^{ij}(\gamma)\quad;\quad b^{ij}=\hat{b}^{ij}(\gamma)\quad;\quad c^{ij}=\hat{c}^{ij}(\gamma)\ .

(10.80)

From (
10.78
) and (
10.80
), we also get

\hat{a}^{0i}=\hat{b}^{0i}=\hat{c}^{00}=0\quad;\quad\hat{c}^{02}=\hat{c}^{20}=\hat{\kappa}\ .

(10.81)

Applying (
10.78
) and (
10.80
) to the action
S_{2D}
(
10.69
), we obtain

\begin{split}S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]=\int d^{2}X\sqrt{-\gamma}&\left[\hat{a}^{22}(\partial_{\theta}\varphi)^{2}+\hat{b}^{22}(\partial_{\theta}\psi)^{2}+2\hat{\kappa}\partial_{t}\varphi\partial_{\theta}\psi+\hat{c}^{22}\partial_{\theta}\varphi\partial_{\theta}\psi+d^{2}\partial_{\theta}\varphi+f^{2}\partial_{\theta}\psi\right]\ ,\end{split}

(10.82)

where all the coefficients (but
d^{2}
and
f^{2}
) may depend on the determinant
\gamma(X)
of the induced metric
\gamma_{ij}(X)
, but not on its components.
The
of the action
S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]
are

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]}{\delta\varphi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}\partial_{\theta}\left[\sqrt{-\gamma}\left(2\hat{a}^{22}\partial_{\theta}\varphi+2\hat{\kappa}\partial_{t}\psi+\hat{c}^{22}\partial_{\theta}\psi+d^{2}\right)\right]=0

(10.83)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]}{\delta\psi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}\partial_{\theta}\left[\sqrt{-\gamma}\left(2\hat{b}^{22}\partial_{\theta}\psi+2\hat{\kappa}\partial_{t}\varphi+\hat{c}^{22}\partial_{\theta}\varphi+f^{2}\right)\right]=0\ ,

(10.84)

where we used (
10.71
) and the fact that
\sqrt{-\gamma}\hat{\kappa}
is constant, in order to write these equations as
\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\gamma}}\partial_{\theta}[...]
,
i.e.
as a
\theta
-derivative.

10.3
Holographic contact

We now consider the generic solutions of the
(
10.18
)-(
10.20
), where the bulk gauge fields
A_{i}(X)
and
B_{i}(X)
are now replaced by their boundary values
\partial_{i}\varphi(X)
and
\partial_{i}\psi(X)
, defined in (
10.30
)-(
10.31
) and with
C=\hat{C}=0
(
10.35
)-(
10.36
) :

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\psi

\displaystyle=-l_{1}\partial_{t}\psi

(10.85)

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\varphi

\displaystyle=-l_{2}\partial_{t}\psi

(10.86)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi

\displaystyle=-l_{3}\partial_{t}\psi\ .

(10.87)

Eq.(
10.85
) describes a chiral boson
\psi(X)
moving at the 2D edge of the bulk with velocity
v_{\psi}=\frac{1}{l_{1}}
. In the same way, by using (
10.86
) in (
10.87
), we find that
\varphi(X)
is a chiral boson as well, satisfying

\partial_{t}\varphi-\frac{l_{3}}{l_{2}}\partial_{\theta}\varphi=0\ ,

(10.88)

moving with velocity
v_{\varphi}=-\frac{l_{3}}{l_{2}}
. What is important to remark now is that, differently from what happens in flat spacetime (Chapter
4
and
[
11
,
87
,
88
]
) and in analogy to the case of Chern-Simons theory in curved spacetime with radial boundary (Section
9
and
[
2
]
), on the edge of a generic bulk manifold we find two chiral bosons moving with
local
, rather than constant, velocities. In fact, both velocities, at this stage, can depend on the determinant and/or on the components of
\gamma_{ij}
which, in general, are local quantities.
To establish the holographic contact, we consider the
(
10.83
), (
10.84
) with
d^{2}=f^{2}=0
(since the
are homogeneously linear in the derivatives)

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\left(2\frac{\hat{a}^{22}}{\hat{\kappa}}\partial_{\theta}\varphi+2\partial_{t}\psi+\frac{\hat{c}^{22}}{\hat{\kappa}}\partial_{\theta}\psi\right)=0

(10.89)

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\left(2\frac{\hat{b}^{22}}{\hat{\kappa}}\partial_{\theta}\psi+2\partial_{t}\varphi+\frac{\hat{c}^{22}}{\hat{\kappa}}\partial_{\theta}\varphi\right)=0\ .

(10.90)

The holographic contact is realized by inserting into the
(
10.89
), (
10.90
) the
solutions (
10.85
)-(
10.87
), which gives

\displaystyle-2\frac{l_{2}}{\hat{\kappa}}\hat{a}^{22}+2-\frac{l_{1}}{\hat{\kappa}}\hat{c}^{22}

\displaystyle=0

(10.91)

\displaystyle-2\frac{l_{1}}{\hat{\kappa}}\hat{b}^{22}-2l_{3}-\frac{l_{2}}{\hat{\kappa}}\hat{c}^{22}

\displaystyle=0\ .

(10.92)

We can write two of the three boundary parameters (
e.g.\ \hat{a}^{22},\hat{b}^{22}
) in terms of the remaining one (
\hat{c}^{22}
) and of the bulk coefficients (
\hat{\kappa},l_{i}
):

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{22}

\displaystyle=+\hat{\kappa}\frac{1}{l_{2}}\left(1-\frac{l_{1}}{2\hat{\kappa}}\hat{c}^{22}\right)

(10.93)

\displaystyle\hat{b}^{22}

\displaystyle=-\hat{\kappa}\frac{l_{3}}{l_{1}}\left(1+\frac{l_{2}}{2\hat{\kappa}l_{3}}\hat{c}^{22}\right)\ .

(10.94)

Remember that
\hat{a}^{22}
and
\hat{b}^{22}
, defined in (
10.72
) and (
10.73
), must depend on the determinant of the induced metric only, and not on its components. On the other hand, the coefficients
l_{i}
(
11.A.8
)-(
11.A.10
) may depend on both the determinant and the components of
\gamma_{ij}
. Therefore, we have to tune the parameters of the boundary action (
10.5
) in order that
\hat{a}^{22}
and
\hat{b}^{22}
have the right dependence. For instance, one easy way to achieve this is to set
\hat{\alpha}=\hat{\beta}=\hat{\zeta}=0
in (
10.6
)-(
10.8
).
These two equations are consequence of the bulk (
)-boundary (
) correspondence, from which we can find out the physics of the 2D induced theory. We do this by inserting them back into the 2D action (
10.82
) (with
d^{2}=f^{2}=0
):

S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]=\kappa\int d^{2}X\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\left\{\left[\tfrac{1}{l_{2}}(\partial_{\theta}\varphi)^{2}-\tfrac{l_{3}}{l_{1}}(\partial_{\theta}\psi)^{2}\right]-\tfrac{\hat{c}^{22}}{2\hat{\kappa}}\tfrac{l_{1}}{l_{2}}\left(\partial_{\theta}\varphi-\tfrac{l_{2}}{l_{1}}\partial_{\theta}\psi\right)^{2}+2\partial_{t}\varphi\partial_{\theta}\psi\right\}\ .

(10.95)

Physical interpretation

The holographic contact has been imposed by inserting into the
the
which represent two chiral bosons. Therefore we expect that the 2D theory should describe two chiral bosons as well. This fact appears evident by considering the following linear combination

\Phi^{\pm}\equiv\varphi\pm\psi\ .

(10.96)

In terms of these new fields the action
S_{2D}[\varphi,\psi]
(
10.95
) writes

S_{2D}[\Phi^{+},\Phi^{-}]=S_{2D}[\Phi^{+}]+S_{2D}[\Phi^{-}]+\kappa\int d^{2}X\;\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\partial_{\theta}\Phi^{+}\partial_{\theta}\Phi^{-}\left(\frac{2\hat{\kappa}(l_{1}+l_{2}l_{3})-\hat{c}^{22}(l_{1}^{2}-l_{2}^{2})}{4\hat{\kappa}l_{1}l_{2}}\right)\ ,

(10.97)

where

S_{2D}[\Phi^{\pm}]=\frac{\kappa}{2}\int d^{2}X\;\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}\partial_{\theta}\Phi^{\pm}(\pm\partial_{t}\Phi^{\pm}+v_{\pm}\partial_{\theta}\Phi^{\pm})\ ,

(10.98)

and

v_{\pm}=\tfrac{2\hat{\kappa}(l_{1}-l_{2}l_{3})-\hat{c}^{22}(l_{1}\mp l_{2})^{2}}{4\hat{\kappa}l_{1}l_{2}}\ .

(10.99)

The action
S_{2D}[\Phi^{+},\Phi^{-}]
(
10.97
) decouples into the sum of the Luttinger actions (
10.98
)

S_{2D}[\Phi^{+},\Phi^{-}]=S_{2D}[\Phi^{+}]+S_{2D}[\Phi^{-}]\ ,

(10.100)

provided that the following condition on the parameters of the theory holds

2\hat{\kappa}(l_{1}+l_{2}l_{3})-\hat{c}^{22}(l_{1}^{2}-l_{2}^{2})=0\ .

(10.101)

Once decoupled, we may identify the fields
\Phi^{+}(X)
and
\Phi^{-}(X)
as Right (R) and Left (L) modes moving at the radial edge of the 3D bulk theory with velocities
\pm v_{\pm}
respectively
[
94
]
, where

v_{\pm}=\frac{1\mp l_{3}}{l_{2}\pm l_{1}}\qquad\mbox{if}\ \ l_{1}^{2}-l_{2}^{2}\neq 0\ ,

(10.102)

and

\begin{array}[]{lcllcl}v_{+}=\dfrac{1}{l_{1}}&;&v_{-}=\dfrac{1}{l_{1}}-\dfrac{\hat{c}^{22}}{\hat{\kappa}}&\quad\mbox{if}\ \ l_{1}=l_{2}&;&l_{3}=-1\\
v_{+}=-\dfrac{1}{l_{1}}+\dfrac{\hat{c}^{22}}{\hat{\kappa}}&;&v_{-}=-\dfrac{1}{l_{1}}&\quad\mbox{if}\ \ l_{1}=-l_{2}&;&l_{3}=1\ .\end{array}

(10.103)

As a consequence of the holographic contact, the metric dependence of the boundary parameters (
10.80
) is transferred to the
l_{i}
coefficients through (
10.93
) and (
10.94
). This makes
v_{\pm}
depend on the determinant of the induced metric
v_{\pm}=v_{\pm}(\gamma)
. We therefore remark the crucial point that the fact of dealing with a curved bulk spacetime has the primary consequence that the velocities of the edge modes depend on both time and space
v_{\pm}=v_{\pm}(t,\theta)
, differently to what happens for flat backgrounds.
Hence, from
v_{\pm}
we see that the edge action
S_{2D}
(
10.100
) may describe three classes of physical situations, tuned by the
local
bulk parameters
l_{i}
and by
\hat{c}^{22}
:

1.

\boldsymbol{v_{+}v_{-}>0}
: LR movers with opposite velocities.

It is realized if

\frac{1-l^{2}_{3}}{l_{2}^{2}-l_{1}^{2}}>0\qquad\mbox{if}\ \ l_{2}^{2}-l_{1}^{2}\neq 0\ ,

(10.104)

or

\hat{\kappa}-\hat{c}^{22}l_{1}>0\qquad\mbox{if}\ \ l_{1}=\pm l_{2}\ \ ,\ \ l_{3}=\mp 1\ .

(10.105)

This situation describes generic chiral Luttinger liquids
[
128
]
, but also helical ones
[
125
]
.
In fact
ordinary

[
24
,
21
,
23
,
22
,
34
]
, characterized by edge modes moving in opposite directions with equal velocities

v_{+}=v_{-}\qquad\mbox{(Topological Insulators)}\ ,

(10.106)

fall into this category. It is easy to see that the condition (
10.106
) is satisfied provided that

\displaystyle l_{1}+l_{2}l_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(10.107)

\displaystyle\hat{c}^{22}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\ .

(10.108)

The equal and opposite edge velocities therefore are

v_{+}=v_{-}=\frac{1}{l_{2}}\ ,

(10.109)

which still, for a generic bulk manifold, may have a spacetime dependence.

2.

\boldsymbol{v_{+}v_{-}<0}
: LR movers in the same direction.

It is realized if

\frac{1-l^{2}_{3}}{l_{2}^{2}-l_{1}^{2}}<0\qquad\mbox{if}\ l_{2}^{2}-l_{1}^{2}\neq 0

(10.110)

or

\hat{\kappa}-\hat{c}^{22}l_{1}<0\qquad\mbox{if}\ l_{1}=\pm l_{2}\ \ ,\ \ l_{3}=\mp 1\ .

(10.111)

Also in this case we can recover the particular case of a pair of Hall systems
[
124
]
, with edge excitations moving in the same direction with the same velocity

v_{+}=-v_{-}\qquad\mbox{(pair of Hall systems)}\ ,

(10.112)

realized if

\displaystyle l_{2}+l_{1}l_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(10.113)

\displaystyle\hat{c}^{22}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2\hat{\kappa}}{l_{1}}\ .

(10.114)

The velocities of the edge modes in this case are

v_{+}=-v_{-}=\frac{1}{l_{1}}\ .

(10.115)

3.

\boldsymbol{v_{+}v_{-}=0}
: L or R mover not moving, which characterizes the
(
) Insulators
[
22
]
. This happens when

l_{3}=\pm 1\qquad\mbox{if}\ \ l_{1}\mp l_{2}\neq 0\ ,

(10.116)

which means

v_{\pm}=0\quad;\quad v_{\mp}=\frac{2}{l_{2}\mp l_{1}}\quad;\quad\hat{c}^{22}=\frac{2\hat{\kappa}}{l_{1}\mp l_{2}}\ .

(10.117)

Some comments are in order. First of all we notice that, since the BF coupling constant
\kappa
must be positive,

\hat{c}^{22}=0
uniquely identifies L and R modes moving on the edge of the 3D bulk with opposite velocities (
\boldsymbol{v_{+}=v_{-}}
).
Hence, as for chiral velocities,
\hat{c}^{22}
should be determined either by a phenomenological input or by a symmetry principle. Now,
belong to this class of edge excitations, and are topological phases of electrons which respect Time Reversal (
\mathcal{T}
) symmetry
[
22
,
34
]
. This suggests that
\hat{c}^{22}=0
might be related to the conservation of
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry and, conversely,
\hat{c}^{22}\neq 0
to its violation, like it happens, for instance, in the case of the
Insulators
[
22
]
, described by case 3.
We shall come back to this point in the next Subsection.
A second comment comes from the fact that
it is necessary that the Hamiltonian corresponding to the action (
10.100
) is positive definite. This request yields constraints on the bulk parameters of the model,
i.e.
the “coupling” constant
\hat{\kappa}
(
10.17
), the parameters
\alpha^{ij}
(
10.6
),
\beta^{ij}
(
10.7
) and
\zeta^{ij}
(
10.8
) appearing in
S_{bd}
(
10.5
), together with the parameter
\hat{c}^{22}
. We recall that the parameters
l_{i}
depend on the bulk through (
11.A.8
), (
11.A.9
) and (
11.A.10
) and appear in the action
S_{2D}[\Phi^{+},\Phi^{-}]
through
v_{\pm}
.
The canonical variables defined in (
10.62
) and (
10.63
) in terms of
\Phi^{\pm}
(
10.96
) write

\begin{split}q_{1}&=\frac{\Phi^{+}+\Phi^{-}}{2}\quad;\quad p_{1}=\frac{\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}}{2}\partial_{\theta}\left(\Phi^{+}-\Phi^{-}\right)\\
q_{2}&=\frac{\Phi^{+}-\Phi^{-}}{2}\quad;\quad p_{2}=\frac{\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}}{2}\partial_{\theta}\left(\Phi^{+}+\Phi^{-}\right)\ .\end{split}

(10.118)

The Hamiltonian density of the model therefore is

\begin{split}\mathcal{H}_{2D}&=p_{1}\dot{q}_{1}+p_{2}\dot{q}_{2}-\mathcal{L}_{2D}\\
&=-\frac{1}{2\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}}\left[v_{+}(p_{1}+p_{2})^{2}+v_{-}(p_{1}-p_{2})^{2}\right]\\
&=-\frac{\kappa\tilde{\epsilon}^{012}}{2}\left[v_{+}(\partial_{\theta}\Phi^{+})^{2}+v_{-}(\partial_{\theta}\Phi^{-})^{2}\right]\ .\end{split}

(10.119)

Positive energy density means
\mathcal{H}_{2D}>0
, and since
\kappa>0
(
10.58
), requiring the coefficients of the squared terms to be positive gives the following constraint

\mathcal{H}_{2D}>0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad v_{+}\leq 0,\ v_{-}\leq 0\ .

(10.120)

Therefore, we observe that the physical situation of edge modes moving in the same direction (
\boldsymbol{v_{+}v_{-}<0}
) is not compatible with the positivity conditions (
10.120
). The fact that the Hamiltonian is not bounded by below would lead us to discard this case, leaving us only with the cases 1 and 3.

10.4
The role of Time-Reversal symmetry

In this Section we present two alternative ways, a) and b), of introducing the Time Reversal
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry in the theory with boundary. It will turn out that these two approaches, although seemingly quite different, are indeed equivalent.
\mathcal{T}
-transformation is defined in the usual way as
\mathcal{T}x^{0}=-x^{0}
. Due to the invariance of the line element
ds^{2}
, we have

\mathcal{T}\gamma_{t\theta}=-\gamma_{t\theta}\ .

(10.121)

The only components of the gauge fields which change sign under
\mathcal{T}
are:

\begin{split}\mathcal{T}A_{r}(t,r,\theta)&=-A_{r}(-t,r,\theta)\\
\mathcal{T}A_{\theta}(t,r,\theta)&=-A_{\theta}(-t,r,\theta)\\
\mathcal{T}B_{t}(t,r,\theta)&=-B_{t}(-t,r,\theta)\ .\end{split}

(10.122)

According to this definition
A_{\mu}(x)
may be associated to an electric potential and
B_{\mu}(x)
to a spin current, like in the flat case
[
88
]
.

a)
When
\mathcal{T}S_{bd}=S_{bd}

It is immediate to see that the bulk action
S_{BF}
(
10.1
) is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant
7
7

7

Other choices of
\mathcal{T}
are possible which leave
S_{BF}
invariant, like for instance
\mathcal{T}A_{t}=-A_{t},\ \mathcal{T}B_{r}=-B_{r},\ \mathcal{T}B_{\theta}=-B_{\theta}
, which correspond to
A_{\mu}\leftrightarrow B_{\mu}
. One can show that these choices are equivalent
[
88
]
.
, and it is interesting to study which are the consequences of imposing Time-Reversal also on the boundary term
S_{bd}
(
10.5
),
i.e.

\mathcal{T}S_{bd}=S_{bd}\ .

(10.123)

Due to (
10.121
), to the fact that
\mathcal{T}\gamma=\gamma
and to the form of the coefficients (
10.6
), (
10.7
) and (
10.8
), the parameters appearing in
S_{bd}
(
10.5
) which transform non-trivially under
\mathcal{T}
are

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\alpha^{02}=

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}^{02}-\hat{\alpha}\gamma^{02}

(10.124)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\beta^{02}=

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}^{02}-\hat{\beta}\gamma^{02}

(10.125)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\zeta^{02}=

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}^{02}-\hat{\zeta}\gamma^{02}

(10.126)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\zeta^{20}=

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}^{20}-\hat{\zeta}\gamma^{20}\ .

(10.127)

Requiring the invariance (
10.123
), from (
10.122
) and (
10.124
)-(
10.127
), we get the following constraints

\hat{\alpha}^{02}=\hat{\alpha}^{20}=0\quad;\quad\hat{\beta}^{02}=\hat{\beta}^{20}=0\quad;\quad\zeta^{00}=0\quad;\quad\zeta^{22}=0\quad;\quad\hat{\zeta}=0\ .

(10.128)

The resulting
\mathcal{T}
-invariant boundary term
S_{bd}
(
10.5
) is

\begin{split}S_{bd}=\int d^{3}x\,&\sqrt{-\gamma}\,\delta(r-R)\left[\frac{\gamma^{ij}}{2}\left(\hat{\alpha}A_{i}A_{j}+\hat{\beta}B_{i}B_{j}\right)+\frac{\hat{\alpha}^{00}}{2}A_{t}A_{t}+\right.\\
&\left.\ +\frac{\hat{\alpha}^{22}}{2}A_{\theta}A_{\theta}+\frac{\hat{\beta}^{00}}{2}B_{t}B_{t}+\frac{\hat{\beta}^{22}}{2}B_{\theta}B_{\theta}+\hat{\zeta}^{02}A_{t}B_{\theta}+\hat{\zeta}^{20}A_{\theta}B_{t}\right]\ ,\end{split}

(10.129)

and we recall that all the coefficients appearing in (
10.129
) might still depend on the determinant
\gamma
of the induced metric
\gamma_{ij}
, being therefore local quantities and not simply constants. It will be interesting to investigate which are the consequences, if any, of imposing
\mathcal{T}
on
S_{bd}
on the holographically induced 2D theory.

Generic non-diagonal metric
{\gamma_{t\theta}\neq 0}

As a consequence of (

Diagonal metric

Non-Dirichlet solutions are

\displaystyle A_{t}(X)=

(10.144)

where

l_{a}=\frac{\beta^{22}}{\hat{\zeta}^{02}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad l_{b}=\frac{\beta^{00}}{\hat{\zeta}^{20}}\ ,

(10.145)

\displaystyle\left(\hat{\zeta}^{02}-\hat{\kappa}\right)\hat{\zeta}^{02}-\alpha^{00}\beta^{22}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(10.146)

10.5
Summary of results and discussion

In this Section we considered the abelian 3D BF model on a manifold described by a generic metric, with a radial boundary which spoils the topological character of the theory. Following a method introduced by Symanzik, we added to the action a boundary term constrained only by locality and power counting, in order to find out the most general
on the two gauge fields involved in the theory. The boundary breaks gauge invariance, and this reflects in two broken Ward identities, from which we identified the boundary degrees of freedom, represented by two scalar fields, deriving also a semidirect sum of two
algebras formed by two on-shell conserved currents. As in the Chern-Simons case of Section
9
, the central charge of the
algebra is proportional to the inverse of the BF coupling, and this constrains the BF coupling to be positive. Once written in terms of the scalar boundary degrees of freedom, the
algebra can be interpreted as the commutation relation of canonical variables, which led us to derive the corresponding 2D action. The bulk/boundary holographic correspondence is achieved by matching the
on the 3D gauge fields and the equations of motion of the 2D action. The resulting 2D action is a complicated functional of the pair of scalar fields but, by means of a simple linear redefinition, the action can be written in terms of two Luttinger actions for two chiral fields, plus a mixed term, which we force to vanish by imposing a decoupling condition. At this point, the parameters which survived to the holographic contact and to the decoupling condition allow for interesting physical interpretations of the theory, which may indeed describe :

1.

two edge excitations moving in opposite directions. This is realized in
Hall systems, like
with
\nu=1-1/n

[
124
]
, and edge modes of
systems, like
(when
v_{+}=v_{-}
), possibly interacting
[
129
]
, or nanowires
[
130
]
with additional magnetic fields acting on the velocities up to switching one off
[
131
,
132
]
. In higher dimensions an effect of renormalization of chiral velocities (
i.e.\ v_{+}v_{-}>0
) can be achieved by adding magnetic fields
[
133
,
134
]
, or by structural deformations
[
135
]
;

2.

two chiral bosons moving in the same direction. This physically corresponds to Hall systems like, for instance, Quantum Hall with
\nu=2
, or
with
\nu=2/5

[
48
,
124
,
136
]
possibly with non-constant interactions or confining potentials
[
96
,
97
,
98
,
99
,
100
]
;

3.

one static and one moving mode. This is the situation of

, where magnetic impurities break the
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry of

[
22
,
126
,
127
]
. This could also be explained as an extremal effect of a magnetic field acting on the modes of the nanowires mentioned above.

In all cases described above, the chiral velocities depend on the induced metric on the boundary, hence on time and space. Therefore, their local nature is a direct consequence of the non-flatness of the bulk metric, which thus appear as a general property of (abelian)
in curved spacetimes. Moreover, we calculated the Hamiltonian corresponding to the 2D action and we found that the request of the existence of a lower bound rules out the case 2 : Hall systems with parallel velocities, like Fractional Quantum Hall with
\nu=2/5

[
124
]
or Integer Quantum Hall with
\nu=2

[
136
]
, cannot be described by a BF theory with boundary. We then considered the role of Time Reversal symmetry, which distinguishes the two main Schwarz-type
:
\mathcal{T}
-violating Chern-Simons and
\mathcal{T}
-respecting BF.
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry can be introduced in two different ways : from the bulk side asking that the “Symanzik’s” boundary term of the 3D action is
\mathcal{T}
-invariant, which has consequences on the subsequent steps till the holographically induced 2D action.
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry may be imposed also on the boundary side directly on the 2D action, starting from the definition of the scalar degrees of freedom. These two seemingly inequivalent ways of requiring
\mathcal{T}
-invariance lead to the same outcome : the only physical case which respects
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry is a subclass of case 1 above, namely the one involving two edge excitations moving in opposite directions with the same velocity, which, in the general case of non-Minkowskian bulk metric, might be local,
i.e.
time and space dependent. This is the case of
. Hence, imposing
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry is much more restrictive than asking for a lower bounded Hamiltonian. Topological phases of matter displaying accelerated edge modes have been observed for instance in the Integer Quantum Hall with
\nu=2

[
48
]
. But, to our knowledge, generalized Topological Insulators with accelerated chiral edge modes have not been discovered yet. Here we thus predict that they should, and we presented a theoretical framework for their existence, as a direct consequence of a non-Minkowskian bulk background. In our opinion, this represents a cleaner alternative to adding an
ad hoc
local potential to the pair of the decoupled Luttinger actions, which would spoil the whole holographic construction.

An overview for the non-Topological case

Since the introductory part of Chapter
1
, we have encountered many cases of physical results induced by the presence of boundaries. The Casimir effect
[
8
]
perhaps is the first highly nontrivial example of boundary effect which has been thoroughly studied in a systematic way. The role of boundaries has been largely discussed in 2D Conformal Field Theory
[
15
,
137
]
. In particular, in
[
15
]
the zoo of Conformal Field Theories has been tamed by means of a boundary put on the 3D topological Chern-Simons theory. In
[
137
]
, instead, the role of the boundary, and in particular of the
, has been exploited for the study of the Virasoro algebras and their extensions (
, superconformal, W-algebras). We have seen that
have vanishing Hamiltonian and energy-momentum tensor, and it is rather surprising that for such non-physical theories it has been possible to establish
[
138
,
76
,
29
]

that on their lower dimensional edge, conserved currents exist, which form
algebras

[
26
,
27
]
, whose central charge is inversely proportional to the coupling constant of the bulk theory, and directly related to the velocity of the boundary propagating

[
