Fracton action in terms of electric and magnetic tensor fields

We have seen that the fracton action (
12.8
), originally written in terms of the field
A_{\mu\nu}(x)
, can be written in terms of the tensor
F_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)
as (
12.20
). This makes apparent the strong analogy with the classical electromagnetic Maxwell theory, of which the fracton theory appears to be the higher rank generalization. This analogy is even more spectacular when the four equations (
12.58
), (
12.59
), (
12.60
) and (
12.61
) governing the theory are considered, which can be written in terms of the two electric and magnetic tensor fields
E^{ij}(x)
(
12.36
) and
B_{i}^{\ j}(x)
(
12.48
).
As in Maxwell theory, two equations, namely (
12.58
) and (
12.61
), are the
of the action (
12.20
), while the other two, (
12.59
) and (
12.60
), are consequences of the “Bianchi” identity written in Table
2
for the tensor
F_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)
, hence have a geometrical nature. The analogy with electromagnetism can be pushed further by noting that the fracton action (
12.20
) can be written in terms of the electric and magnetic tensor fields as follows :

\begin{split}S_{fract}&={\frac{g_{1}}{6}}\int d^{4}x\,F_{\mu\nu\rho}F^{\mu\nu\rho}\\
&={\frac{g_{1}}{6}}\int d^{4}x\left(F^{0ij}F_{0ij}+F^{ij0}F_{ij0}+F^{i0j}F_{i0j}+F^{ijk}F_{ijk}\right)\\
&={\frac{g_{1}}{6}}\int d^{4}x\left(\frac{3}{2}F^{ij0}F_{ij0}+F^{ijk}F_{ijk}\right)\\
&={\frac{g_{1}}{6}}\int d^{4}x\left[-\frac{3}{2}{\frac{1}{g_{1}^{2}}}E^{ij}E_{ij}-{\frac{2}{g^{2}}\epsilon^{0kmn}B^{\;l}_{n}\epsilon_{0kab}B_{\;c}^{b}\left(\delta^{a}_{m}\delta^{c}_{l}+\delta^{a}_{l}\delta^{c}_{m}\right)}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x\left(-{\frac{1}{2g_{1}}}E^{ij}E_{ij}+{\frac{2g_{1}}{g^{2}}}B^{\;j}_{i}B_{\;j}^{i}\right)\ ,\end{split}

(12.63)

where, besides the definitions of the electric and magnetic tensor fields (
12.36
) and (
12.48
), we used the properties of
F_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)
(
12.37
) and (
12.38
), and the tracelessness of the tensor magnetic field (
12.52
). The result (
12.63
) closely reminds the electromagnetic action, whose Lagrangian is proportional to
E^{2}-B^{2}
, provided that

g^{2}=4g_{1}^{2}\ ,

(12.64)

which is compatible with the previously found constraint (
12.62
), that we will assume from now on.

12.3
Stress-energy tensor and fracton Lorentz force

The energy-momentum tensor and conservation laws

The stress-energy tensor for the fracton action
S_{fract}
(
12.20
) is

\begin{split}T_{\alpha\beta}&=\left.-\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S_{fract}}{\delta g^{\alpha\beta}}\right|_{g^{\alpha\beta}=\eta^{\alpha\beta}}\\
&=\left.-\frac{{g_{1}}}{3\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta}{\delta g^{\alpha\beta}}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}g^{\mu\lambda}g^{\nu\gamma}g^{\rho\sigma}F_{\lambda\gamma\sigma}F_{\mu\nu\rho}\right|_{g^{\alpha\beta}=\eta^{\alpha\beta}}\\
&={\frac{g_{1}}{6}}\eta_{\alpha\beta}F^{2}-\frac{g_{1}}{3}\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\eta_{\beta\lambda}\left(2F^{\lambda\nu\rho}F_{\ \,\nu\rho}^{\gamma}+F^{\mu\nu\lambda}F_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \gamma}\right)\ .\end{split}

(12.65)

Notice that taking the trace of
T_{\mu\nu}
, we have, in
d
-spacetime dimensions

T=\eta^{\alpha\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}=\left.g_{1}\frac{(d-6)}{6}F^{2}\right|_{d=4}=-\frac{g_{1}}{3}F^{2}\ ,

(12.66)

which does not vanish in
d=4
, differently from what happens in Maxwell theory. The tracelessness of the stress-energy tensor would be recovered in
d=6
, which, as already remarked in the introductory part at the beginning of Section
12
, seem to be the most natural, although unphysical, spacetime dimensions for fractons. The non-vanishing of the trace of the fracton stress-energy tensor is the sign that the theory, already at classical level, is not scale invariant. This suggests the existence of an energy scale. Now, since tracelessness is eventually related to the masslessness of the photon, the fact that the trace (
12.66
) does not vanish might suggest the existence of a mass (as the typical energy scale) for the fractons, which can be introduced in a similar way as in

[
214
,
215
]
.
The components of the stress-energy tensor are physically interpretable as follows :

•

\alpha=\beta=0
gives the energy density
T_{00}=u

\begin{split}T_{00}=u&=-\frac{g_{1}}{6}F^{2}+\frac{g_{1}}{3}\left(2F^{0\mu\nu}F_{0\mu\nu}+F^{\mu\nu 0}F_{\mu\nu 0}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{4g_{1}}\left(E^{ij}E_{ij}-B^{\;j}_{i}B_{\;j}^{i}\right)+\frac{g_{1}}{2}\left(-{\frac{1}{g_{1}}}\right)\left({\frac{1}{g_{1}}}\right)E^{ij}E_{ij}\\
&=-\frac{1}{4g_{1}}\left(E^{ij}E_{ij}+B^{\;j}_{i}B_{\;j}^{i}\right)\ ,\end{split}

(12.67)

where (
12.37
), (
12.38
), (
12.63
), (
12.40
) and (
12.64
) have been used. Again, this expression is formally identical to the corresponding electromagnetic result
u\propto E^{2}+B^{2}
.
From the positivity constraint of the energy density
u
it must be

g_{1}<0\ ,

(12.68)

and, from now on, we choose

g_{1}=-1\ ;

(12.69)

•

\alpha=0,\ \beta=i
gives the Poynting vector
T_{0i}=S_{i}

\begin{split}T_{0i}=S_{i}=&\frac{1}{3}\eta_{i\lambda}\left(2F^{\lambda\nu\rho}F_{0\nu\rho}+F^{\mu\nu\lambda}F_{\mu\nu 0}\right)\\
=&\frac{1}{3}\eta_{ij}\left(2F^{jkl}F_{0kl}+F^{klj}F_{kl0}\right)\\
=&\frac{1}{6}\eta_{ij}E_{kl}\left(-2\epsilon^{0jlp}B_{p}^{\;k}-\cancel{\epsilon^{0klp}B_{p}^{\;j}}+\epsilon^{0kjp}B_{p}^{\;l}\right)\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0ilp}E^{kl}B^{p}_{\;k}\ ,\end{split}

(12.70)

which, as in Maxwell electromagnetism, is the vector product of the electric and magnetic tensor fields
\vec{S}\propto\vec{E}\times\vec{B}
;

•

\alpha=i,\ \beta=j
gives the stress tensor
T_{ij}=\sigma_{ij}

\displaystyle T_{ij}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\eta_{ij}F^{2}+\frac{1}{3}\eta_{jk}\left(2F^{k\mu\nu}F_{i\mu\nu}+F^{\mu\nu k}F_{\mu\nu i}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\eta_{ij}F^{2}-\eta_{jk}E^{ka}E_{ia}+\frac{1}{3}\eta_{jk}\left(2F^{kab}F_{iab}+F^{abk}F_{abi}\right)

(12.71)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\eta_{ij}F^{2}-\eta_{jk}E^{ka}E_{ia}+\frac{1}{6}\eta_{jk}\left(2\delta^{k}_{i}B_{a}^{\;b}B^{a}_{\;b}-2B_{i}^{\;a}B^{k}_{\;a}+4B_{a}^{\;k}B^{a}_{\;i}-\epsilon^{0akp}\epsilon_{0biq}B_{p}^{\;b}B^{q}_{\;a}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6}\eta_{ij}F^{2}-\eta_{jk}E^{ka}E_{ia}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{jk}\left(\delta^{k}_{i}B_{a}^{\;b}B^{a}_{\;b}-B_{i}^{\;a}B^{k}_{\;a}+B_{a}^{\;k}B^{a}_{\;i}\right)

\displaystyle=\eta_{ij}T_{00}-\eta_{jk}\eta_{il}E^{ka}E^{l}_{\;a}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{jk}\eta_{il}\left(B^{la}B^{k}_{\;a}-B_{a}^{\;k}B^{al}\right)\ ,

where we used

\displaystyle 2F^{kab}F_{iab}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\epsilon^{0kbp}B_{p}^{\;a}+\epsilon^{0abp}B_{p}^{\;k}\right)\left(\epsilon_{0ibq}B^{q}_{\;a}+\epsilon_{0abq}B^{q}_{\;i}\right)

(12.72)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta^{k}_{i}B_{a}^{\;b}B^{a}_{\;b}-B_{i}^{\;a}B^{k}_{\;a}+4B_{a}^{\;k}B^{a}_{\;i}\right)

\displaystyle F^{abk}F_{abi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\left(\epsilon^{0akp}B_{p}^{\;b}+\epsilon^{0bkp}B_{p}^{\;a}\right)\left(\epsilon_{0aiq}B^{q}_{\;b}+\epsilon_{0biq}B^{q}_{\;a}\right)

(12.73)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta^{k}_{i}B_{a}^{\;b}B^{a}_{\;b}-B_{i}^{\;a}B^{k}_{\;a}-\epsilon^{0akp}\epsilon_{0biq}B_{p}^{\;b}B^{q}_{\;a}\right)\ ,

and, from
[
216
]
,

\epsilon^{0akp}\epsilon_{0biq}=-\delta^{a}_{b}(\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{p}_{q}-\delta^{p}_{i}\delta^{k}_{q})+\delta^{a}_{i}(\delta^{k}_{b}\delta^{p}_{q}-\delta^{p}_{b}\delta^{k}_{q})-\delta^{a}_{q}(\delta^{k}_{b}\delta^{p}_{i}-\delta^{p}_{b}\delta^{k}_{i})\ ,

(12.74)

so that

\epsilon^{0akp}\epsilon_{0biq}B_{p}^{\;b}B^{q}_{\;a}=-\delta^{k}_{i}B_{a}^{\;b}B^{a}_{\;b}+B_{i}^{\;a}B_{\;a}^{k}+B_{a}^{\;k}B^{a}_{\;i}\ ,

(12.75)

because of the tracelessness of the magnetic tensor (
12.52
).
Finally, we used also the fact that, because of
(
12.63
) and (
12.67
) we have
T_{00}=-\frac{1}{6}F^{2}+\frac{1}{2}B_{a}^{\;b}B^{a}_{\;b}
.
As expected, the stress tensor is symmetric
i\leftrightarrow j
:

\begin{split}T_{ij}=\frac{1}{4}\eta_{ij}\left(E^{ab}E_{ab}+B^{\;b}_{a}B_{\;b}^{a}\right)-\eta^{ab}E_{ia}E_{jb}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{ab}\left(B_{ia}B_{jb}-B_{aj}B_{bi}\right)\ .\end{split}

(12.76)

Once again, the analogy with Maxwell theory, for which the stress tensor is

\sigma_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\eta_{ij}\left(E^{2}+B^{2}\right)-E_{i}E_{j}-B_{i}B_{j}\ ,

(12.77)

is impressive.

Let us now discuss the (on-shell) conservation of the stress-energy tensor

\partial^{\nu}T_{\mu\nu}=0\ ,

(12.78)

whose components are

•

\mu=0
:

\begin{split}\partial^{\nu}T_{\nu 0}&=\partial^{0}T_{00}+\partial^{i}T_{i0}\\
&=\partial^{0}u+\partial^{i}S_{i}\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\left[E_{ab}\partial_{0}E^{ab}+B^{a}_{\;b}\partial_{0}B_{a}^{\;b}-\epsilon_{0ilp}\partial^{i}\left(E^{kl}B_{\;k}^{p}\right)\right]\\
&=-\frac{1}{2}\left[\epsilon^{0akl}E_{ab}\partial_{k}B_{l}^{\;b}+\epsilon_{0amn}B^{a}_{\;b}\partial^{m}E^{bn}-\epsilon_{0ilp}\partial^{i}\left(E^{kl}B_{\;k}^{p}\right)\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0amn}\left[E^{ab}\partial^{m}B^{n}_{\;b}+B^{n}_{\;b}\partial^{m}E^{ab}-\partial^{m}\left(E^{ab}B^{n}_{\;b}\right)\right]\\
&=0\ ,\end{split}

(12.79)

where we used the
(
12.50
) and (
12.56
). The continuity equation is therefore verified on-shell

\partial^{i}S_{i}+\partial^{0}u=0\ .

(12.80)

•

\mu=i
:

\displaystyle\partial^{\nu}T_{\nu i}

\displaystyle=\partial^{0}T_{0i}+\partial^{j}T_{ji}

\displaystyle=\partial^{0}S_{i}+\partial^{j}\sigma_{ji}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0imn}\partial_{0}\left(E^{am}B^{n}_{\;a}\right)+\partial_{i}u-\eta_{il}\partial_{k}\left(E^{ka}E^{l}_{\;a}\right)-\eta_{il}\partial_{k}\left(B^{la}B^{k}_{\;a}-B_{a}^{\;k}B^{al}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0imn}\left\{\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\epsilon^{0akl}\partial_{k}B_{l}^{\;m}+\epsilon^{0mkl}\partial_{k}B_{l}^{\;a}\right)\right]B^{n}_{\;a}-\epsilon^{0nbc}\partial_{b}E_{ac}E^{am}\right\}+

\displaystyle\quad+\partial_{i}u-\eta_{il}\partial_{k}\left(E^{ka}E^{l}_{\;a}\right)-\eta_{il}\partial_{k}\left(B^{la}B^{k}_{\;a}-B_{a}^{\;k}B^{al}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4}\left[\epsilon_{0imn}\epsilon^{0akl}\partial_{k}B_{l}^{\;m}+\left(\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{l}_{n}-\delta^{k}_{n}\delta^{l}_{i}\right)\partial_{k}B_{l}^{\;a}\right]B^{n}_{\;a}-

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta^{b}_{i}\delta^{c}_{m}-\delta^{b}_{m}\delta^{c}_{i}\right)E^{am}\partial_{b}E_{ac}+\partial_{i}u-\eta_{il}\partial_{k}\left(E^{ka}E^{l}_{\;a}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\eta_{il}\partial_{k}\left(B^{la}B^{k}_{\;a}-B_{a}^{\;k}B^{al}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\left[\left(3B^{n}_{\;i}\partial_{m}B_{n}^{\;m}+2B_{n}^{\;m}\partial_{m}B^{n}_{\;i}\right)-2E^{ab}\partial_{b}E_{ai}\right]

(12.81)

\displaystyle\neq 0\ ,

where (
12.50
), (
12.56
), (
12.74
) and (
12.42
) have been used. Differently from the continuity equation (
12.80
), the spatial components of the divergence of the stress-energy tensor do not vanish. Now, what should we expect actually ? If we think of the stress-energy tensor as the conserved current associated to the diffeomorphism symmetry, as the definition (
12.65
) suggests, it should not be conserved in a theory like the fracton one, which is not diffeomorphism invariant (
12.12
). On the other hand, we are facing here with a partial conservation of the stress-energy tensor, because its time component is indeed conserved, yielding the continuity equation (
12.80
), which relates the flux of the energy density to the divergence of the momentum density. The partial conservation of the stress-energy tensor might be explained by observing that the fracton symmetry (
12.5
) is indeed a diff transformation (
12.6
) with a particular choice of the vector diff parameter.

Fracton Lorentz force

It is interesting to study how the physics is modified if matter is introduced by means of a symmetric rank-2 tensor
J^{\mu\nu}(x)=J^{\nu\mu}(x)
coupled to the fracton field
A_{\mu\nu}(x)

S_{fract}\rightarrow S_{tot}=S_{fract}+S_{J}\ ,

(12.82)

where
S_{fract}
is the pure fractonic action (
12.8
) (or (
12.20
)), and
S_{J}
is the matter action

S_{J}\equiv-\int d^{4}x\,J^{\mu\nu}A_{\mu\nu}\ .

(12.83)

The
(
12.33
) modifies as

\partial_{\mu}F^{\alpha\beta\mu}=-J^{\alpha\beta}\ .

(12.84)

We observe that, due to the cyclicity identity in Table
2
,
J^{\alpha\beta}
is conserved in the following sense

\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}J^{\alpha\beta}=0\ .

(12.85)

The components of the
(
12.84
) are

•

\alpha=\beta=0
:

\partial_{i}F^{00i}=-J^{00}=0\ ,

(12.86)

which vanishes because of (
12.37
), consequence of (
12.35
). Hence, there is no coupling with
A_{00}(x)
, as expected, since it is not a dynamical degree of freedom of the theory, due to (
12.23
).

•

\alpha=0,\ \beta=i
:

\partial_{j}F^{0ij}=-J^{0i}\ ,

(12.87)

which, using (
12.40
), becomes

\partial_{j}E^{ij}=2J^{i0}\ .

(12.88)

Taking the divergence of (
12.88
) we find the analogous of the Gauss law

\partial_{i}\partial_{j}E^{ij}=\rho\ ,

(12.89)

where we defined the charge density

\rho\equiv 2\partial_{i}J^{i0}\ .

(12.90)

This equation plays a central role in
[
54
,
55
,
160
,
161
,
175
]
, since it yields not only the charge neutrality condition, but also the vanishing of the total dipole moment. In fact, integrating (
12.89
), we get

\int dV\partial_{i}\partial_{j}E^{ij}=\int dV\,\rho=0\ ,

(12.91)

which states that the total charge inside an infinite volume is zero. Moreover, from (
12.89
) we also have

\int dVx^{k}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}E^{ij}=\int dV\,x^{k}\rho=\int dV\,p^{k}=0\ ,

(12.92)

according to which the dipole moment density, defined as

p^{k}=x^{k}\rho\ ,

(12.93)

of an infinite volume vanishes.

•

\alpha=i,\ \beta=j
:

\partial_{\mu}F^{ij\mu}=-J^{ij}\ ,

(12.94)

which, using (
12.50
), becomes

-\partial_{0}E^{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\epsilon^{0ikl}\partial_{k}B^{\ j}_{l}+\epsilon^{0jkl}\partial_{k}B^{\ i}_{l}\right)=J^{ij}\ .

(12.95)

Differentiating with
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}
, we have

\begin{split}0&=\partial_{0}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}E^{ij}+\partial_{i}\partial_{j}J^{ij}\\
&=\partial_{0}\rho+\partial_{i}\partial_{j}J^{ij}\ ,\end{split}

(12.96)

where (
12.89
) has been used. It is a kind of continuity equation
[
160
,
213
,
172
]
, which can also be obtained from the conservation equation (
12.85
)

0=\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}J^{\alpha\beta}=2\partial_{0}\partial_{i}J^{0i}+\partial_{i}\partial_{j}J^{ij}=\partial_{0}\rho+\partial_{i}\partial_{j}J^{ij}\ ,

(12.97)

where we have used the definition of the density charge
\rho(x)
(
12.90
) and the fact that
J^{00}(x)=0
(
12.86
).

It is also interesting to see how the (partial) conservation of the stress-energy tensor is modified by the presence of matter.
The continuity equation (
12.80
) is modified as

\partial^{\nu}T_{\nu 0}=\partial^{0}T_{00}+\partial^{i}T_{i0}=\partial^{0}u+\partial^{i}S_{i}=E_{ab}J^{ab}\ ,

(12.98)

while the spatial components of (
12.81
) acquire the term in the last row

\displaystyle\partial^{\nu}T_{\nu i}=\partial^{0}T_{0i}+\partial^{j}T_{ji}=\partial^{0}S_{i}+\partial^{j}\sigma_{ji}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\left[\left(3B^{n}_{\;i}\partial_{m}B_{n}^{\;m}+2B_{n}^{\;m}\partial_{m}B^{n}_{\;i}\right)-2E^{ab}\partial_{b}E_{ai}\right]

(12.99)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0imn}J^{am}B^{n}_{\ a}-2J^{a0}E_{ia}\ ,

The additional terms appearing in (
12.98
) and (
12.99
) can be easily interpreted if, again, we think to the standard Maxwell theory of electromagnetism, where the divergence of the stress-energy tensor in presence of matter involves the 4D “Lorentz force” per unit volume on matter
f^{\mu}
:

\partial_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}+f^{\mu}=0\ .

(12.100)

At the right hand side of (
12.98
) appears

f^{0}=E_{ab}J^{ab}\ ,

(12.101)

which is the analogous of the electromagnetic power
\vec{E}\cdot\vec{J}
. The last term at the right hand side of (
12.99
)

f^{i}=2J_{a0}E^{ia}-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{0imn}J_{am}B_{n}^{\ a}

(12.102)

can be traced back to the generalized Lorentz force on a dipole
p^{i}(x)
moving with velocity
v^{i}
proposed in
[
160
]
once we take

J^{0i}\sim p^{i}\ ,

(12.103)

and

J^{ij}\sim p^{i}v^{j}+p^{j}v^{i}\ .

(12.104)

The first identification (
12.103
) is compatible with (
12.92
), when (
12.88
) is taken into account, and the second relation (
12.104
) agrees with the microscopic lattice definition of the current of a dipole made in
[
160
,
198
]
. What is remarkable is that we recover here as part of the conservation law of the stress-energy tensor the picture conjectured in
[
160
]
: the isolated electric monopoles of the theory described by the action (
12.20
) are fractons, which do not respond to the electromagnetic fields and, hence, do not move, due to the dipole conservation constraint (
12.89
). What we find here is that, instead, dipole motion, which preserves the global dipole moment, does respond to the electromagnetic field tensors
E^{ij}(x)
and
B_{i}^{\ j}(x)
according to (
12.102
), like a conventional charge particle responds to an ordinary electromagnetic field. Hence, from (
12.102
), we confirm the “intuition” proposed in
[
160
]
concerning the Lorentz force on a fracton dipole.

12.4

\theta
-term

In ordinary vector gauge field theory, it is known that a term can be added to the Maxwell action (or to its non-abelian extension, namely the Yang-Mills theory) : the so called
\theta
-term, which has the form

S_{\theta}\sim\theta\int d^{4}x\;\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F_{\mu\nu}F_{\rho\sigma}\sim\theta\int d^{4}x\;\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}\ ,

(12.105)

where
\theta
is a constant parameter. The
\theta
-term represented by (
12.105
) is topological, since it does not depend on the spacetime metric, and it is a total derivative, hence it does not contribute to the
. Nonetheless, the
\theta
-term is relevant in several contexts, like axion electrodynamics, the Witten effect and the strong CP problem (see for instance
[
179
,
206
,
207
,
208
,
209
]
).
For what concerns the fracton theory, in
[
175
]
the
\theta
-term has been generalized as
E_{ij}B^{ij}
, in analogy with the fractonic Hamiltonian density, assumed to be proportional to
(E_{ij}E^{ij}+B_{ij}B^{ij})
.
Considering a compact tensor gauge field, which implies a “magnetic” monopole
(\partial_{i}B^{ij}=g^{i}\neq 0)
, the introduction of the
\theta
-term gives to the Gauss constraint an additional contribution related to the “magnetic” field. As for dyons in the Witten effect
[
180
]
, the “electric” charge thus gains an additional contribution related to the “magnetic” vector charge
[
54
,
55
,
175
]
. On the other hand the possibility of a non-constant
\theta
-term has not yet been investigated in the context of fractons.
The motivation for such a generalization comes from
, which are characterized by a step function
\theta
-term, which switches between
\theta=0
outside the material and
\theta=\pi
inside. Another example is given by
axion models
[
206
,
207
]
, which
describe a dynamical field coupled to photons via a local
\theta(x)
-term. This generates modified Maxwell equations
[
208
,
209
]
as follows

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{E}

\displaystyle=\rho-\vec{\nabla}\theta\cdot\vec{B}

(12.106)

\displaystyle\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}-\partial_{t}\vec{E}

\displaystyle=\vec{J}+\partial_{t}\theta\,\vec{B}+\vec{\nabla}\theta\times\vec{E}\ ,

(12.107)

where the additional terms contribute as an excess of charge (
\vec{\nabla}\theta\cdot\vec{B}
) and current (
\partial_{t}\theta\,\vec{B}+\vec{\nabla}\theta\times\vec{E}
) densities. Thus axion models are frequently used in the context of condensed matter and
to mimic a non-constant
\theta
-term, like for instance in
[
181
]
. As we are dealing with a rank-2 tensor theory, an interesting example is the case studied in
[
205
]
in the context of LG, where modified gravitoelectromagnetic
[
204
]
equations analogous to (
12.106
) and (
12.107
) are recovered.
According to the formalism presented in this Section, based on the Maxwell-like construction of a consistent theory for fractons, the analogous of the
\theta
-term should be the following

S_{\theta}=\frac{1}{9}\int d^{4}x\,\theta\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}F^{\lambda\mu\nu}F_{\lambda}^{\;\rho\sigma}\\
=\int d^{4}x\,\theta\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial^{\mu}A^{\lambda\nu}\partial^{\rho}A_{\lambda}^{\sigma}\ .

(12.108)

We shall see that this is indeed the case by comparing the consequences of adding this term to the action
S_{fract}
(
12.8
) to the known results concerning the
\theta
-term in the theory of fractons
[
175
,
54
,
55
]

and of

[
205
]
. Notice that, differently from the standard
\theta
-term introduced to solve the strong CP problem
[
179
,
217
]
,
S_{\theta}
is not topological, due to the contraction of the
\lambda
-indices in (
12.108
). Moreover, here
\theta(x)
is not constant, like in
[
205
]
. For instance,
\theta(x)
might be the Heaviside step function, which would correspond to introducing a boundary at
x=0
, as we have seen in the previous Chapters
[
88
,
1
,
2
,
12
,
3
]
. The contribution of
S_{\theta}
to the
is

\frac{\delta S_{\theta}}{\delta A^{\alpha\beta}}=-(\delta^{\gamma}_{\alpha}\delta^{\sigma}_{\beta}+\delta^{\sigma}_{\alpha}\delta^{\gamma}_{\beta})\partial^{\rho}\theta\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\eta_{\lambda\gamma}\partial^{\mu}A^{\lambda\nu}\ .

(12.109)

It is interesting to observe that this contribution is the same as the one that in
[
205
]
gives the
\theta
-modified term of the gravitoelectromagnetic equations, where the electric and magnetic fields are vectors.
The
(
12.33
) acquire an additional term

\frac{\delta S_{fract}}{\delta A^{\alpha\beta}}+\frac{\delta S_{\theta}}{\delta A^{\alpha\beta}}=0\ ,

(12.110)

whose components are

•

\alpha=\beta=0

\partial_{i}F^{00i}-2\partial^{k}\theta\,\epsilon_{0ijk}\partial^{i}A^{0j}=0\ ,

(12.111)

which is still solved by
A^{0\mu}=\partial^{\mu}A^{0}
(
12.35
) ;

•

\alpha=0,\ \beta=i

\partial_{j}E^{ij}-\partial^{j}\theta\,B_{j}^{\ i}=0\ ,

(12.112)

which is the tensorial extension of (
12.106
)

\vec{\nabla}\cdot\vec{E}=-\vec{\nabla}\theta\cdot\vec{B}\penalty\ ;

(12.113)

•

\alpha=i,\ \beta=j

\delta_{ab}^{ij}\left[\partial_{0}E^{ab}-\epsilon^{0akl}\partial_{k}B_{l}^{\;b}+\eta^{ac}\left(\epsilon^{0nmb}\partial_{n}\theta\,E_{mc}+\partial_{0}\theta\,B^{b}_{\;c}\right)\right]=0\ ,

(12.114)

where we defined the symmetrized delta

\delta^{ab}_{ij}\equiv\frac{1}{2}(\delta^{a}_{i}\delta^{b}_{j}+\delta^{b}_{i}\delta^{a}_{j})\ ,

(12.115)

which agrees with (
12.107
)

\vec{\nabla}\times\vec{B}-\partial_{t}\vec{E}=\partial_{t}\theta\,\vec{B}+\vec{\nabla}\theta\times\vec{E}\ .

(12.116)

Therefore Eqs. (
12.110
) are generalized tensorial
\theta
-modified Maxwell equations. In particular, as in the standard modified Maxwell equations
[
209
]
, we can interpret the
\theta
-dependent terms as an excess of charge and current densities:

\partial_{j}E^{ij}=\tilde{\rho}^{i}\quad;\quad-\partial_{0}E^{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\epsilon^{0ikl}\partial_{k}B^{\ j}_{l}+\epsilon^{0jkl}\partial_{k}B^{\ i}_{l}\right)=\tilde{J}^{ij}\ ,

with

\tilde{\rho}^{i}\equiv\partial^{j}\theta\,B_{j}^{\ i}\quad;\quad\tilde{J}^{ij}\equiv\delta_{ab}^{ij}\eta^{ac}\left(\epsilon^{0nmb}\partial_{n}\theta\,E_{mc}+\partial_{0}\theta\,B^{b}_{\;c}\right)\ .

We have a further confirmation that
S_{\theta}
(
12.105
) is indeed the correct
\theta
-term when we write it in terms of the electric and magnetic tensor fields (
12.36
) and (
12.48
)

S_{\theta}=-\frac{1}{3}\int d^{4}x\,\theta\eta_{lm}\epsilon_{0ijk}F^{il0}F^{mjk}=-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x\,\theta E^{il}B_{il}\ ,

(12.117)

which is the tensorial extension of the standard
\theta
-term
S_{\theta}\sim\theta\int\vec{E}\cdot\vec{B}
for constant
\theta

[
179
]
.

12.5
Final remarks

In Section
11
we have observed that in the Literature the usual approach to deal with a theory of fractons, is to treat space and time separately, hence non covariantly. The standard way to proceed is to take the spatial Gauss contraint (
11.2
), written for a “tensor electric field”
E_{ij}(x)
, as the tool to realize the defining property of fractons,
i.e.
their limited mobility, by extending to fracton dipoles the usual conservation law holding for electric charges. This is usually achieved by introducing a “tensor”, instead of a vector, potential
A_{ij}(x)
, obeying the generalized
spatial
gauge transformation (
11.3
). As we explained, the tensor field
E_{ij}(x)
was, somehow, defined as the
spatial
canonical momentum, as in (
12.31
). We say
somehow
because in the definition of
E_{ij}(x)
a scalar field
A_{0}(x)
appears, as a multiplier introduced by hand in order to enforce the Gauss constraint.
Moreover, given the fracton limited mobility, it comes naturally to ask which is the generalization of the Lorentz force, and how the absence of motion could be compatible with the existence of an electromagnetic Lorentz force, and, above all, which is the elementary object on which such a force acts. Last but not least, in the Literature fractons are often seen in relation to gravity in a nontrivial way, starting from
[
177
]
. In this Section we adopted a covariant approach to the theory of fractons. This is not only a matter of formalism, but, rather, it allows to better understand the nature itself of these quasiparticles. The main contribution has thus been to show that, embedding the usual spatial and non-covariant theory of fractons in a more general
covariant gauge field theory
, everything goes to the right place naturally, without introducing by hand any external ingredient. Our unique and starting point, as usual in field theory, is the covariant transformation (
12.1
), from which the most general
covariant
invariant action (
12.7
) is derived. To cite a few new results following this approach, the relation with linearized gravity appears immediately, since the action (
12.7
) consists of two terms, one of which, namely (
12.9
), just describes linearized gravity. The theory of fractons as “emergent electromagnetism”, as often has been called, is evident from the beginning as well, once we defined the rank-three “electromagnetic” field strength (
12.19
) by means of which the fracton Lagrangian writes as
F^{2}
, just like Maxwell theory (from which the title of the Section). According to our field-theoretical point of view, the Gauss constraint is not an external constraint anymore, but turns out to be one of the equations of motion, the others formally coinciding with the Maxwell equations, which is mostly interesting, in our opinion. Finally, studying the conservation of the stress-energy tensor, we recovered the Lorentz force (
12.102
), as it was correctly guessed in
[
160
]
, from which we see that, as one might expect, the force acts on fracton dipoles, and not on isolated charges, thus preserving the absence of mobility for isolated fractons. Here, we remark a subtle point which concerns the stress-energy tensor of the fracton action. In Section
12.3
we computed the stress-energy tensor, defined as

T_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S}{\delta g^{\alpha\beta}}\ ,

(12.118)

and we show that it has the correct components, which are exactly the higher rank generalizations of the Maxwell energy density (
T_{00}(x)
), of the Poynting vector (
T_{0i}(x)
) and of the stress tensor (
T_{ij}(x)
). The time component is conserved on shell,
i.e.\ \partial_{\mu}T^{\mu 0}(x)=0,
and gives the continuity equation relating the energy density to the Poynting vector. So far so good. But the space component of the stress-energy tensor conservation law is not exactly conserved. We find a breaking term which might be interpreted as follows. The stress-energy tensor (
12.118
) is the conserved current associated to the infinitesimal diff invariance (
12.2
)
[
105
]
, which is not a symmetry of the theory defined by (
12.1
). Hence, the stress-energy tensor (
12.118
) should not be conserved. Nonetheless, the fracton transformation (
12.1
) is a particular case of the general diff transformation (
12.2
). Hence, it is not that unexpected that the stress-energy tensor is
almost
conserved. A further confirmation that
T_{\alpha\beta}(x)
(
12.118
) is the correct one is that, when matter is added, quite remarkably the conservation equation gives exactly the Lorentz force for fracton dipoles that has been conjectured in
[
160
]
.

13
Gauge for Two

The aim here is to study the theory of fractons from the field theoretical point of view where, again, peculiar and unusual features appear. In our approach, presented in the previous Section, fractons are described by a gauge field theory involving a symmetric tensor field
A_{\mu\nu}(x)
transforming as

\delta A_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\Lambda\ ,

(13.1)

where
\Lambda(x)
is a scalar local gauge parameter. The transformation (
13.1
) is a particular case of the infinitesimal diffeomorphisms

\delta_{diff}A_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\Lambda_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\Lambda_{\mu}\ ,

(13.2)

when the vector gauge parameter
\Lambda_{\mu}(x)
is the derivative of a scalar field

\Lambda_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\Lambda\ .

(13.3)

The most general action
S_{inv}
(
12.7
) invariant under the fracton symmetry (
13.1
) is the sum of two terms, one of which, (
12.9
), can be immediately identified as

[
105
,
211
]
. This term is to be expected since the infinitesimal diffeomorphisms (
13.2
), which is the defining symmetry of
, embeds the fracton symmetry (
13.1
) through (
13.3
). We have shown in the previous Section that the other term (
12.8
) describes a theory with pure fractonic features
[
4
]
: from this term one can recover all the properties characterizing the so called “scalar charge theory” of fractons
[
160
]
, including Maxwell-like equations of which the Gauss law is at the origin of the limited mobility property that defines fractons
[
189
,
161
,
160
,
54
,
55
]
. Since the transformation (
13.1
) is a particular case of the more general infinitesimal diffeomorphism (
13.2
), which is a gauge transformation
[
105
,
211
,
215
,
218
]
,
the theory of the symmetric tensor field
A_{\mu\nu}(x)
defined by the symmetry (
13.1
) is a
gauge
field theory as well.
Evidence of this appears when trying to compute the propagator from the quadratic action
S_{inv}
(
12.7
), which, similarly to the electromagnetic Maxwell theory, leads to a non invertible matrix. Therefore a gauge fixing term must be added. For any other gauge field theory, this procedure goes smoothly thanks to the Faddeev-Popov procedure
[
71
]
, which focuses on the gauge parameter, and in particular on its tensorial character. In (
13.1
) the gauge parameter is a scalar, and this would require a scalar gauge fixing condition. The most general covariant one is

\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}A_{\mu\nu}+\kappa\partial^{2}A=0\ ,

(13.4)

where
\kappa
is a constant gauge fixing parameter. This is analogous to the covariant Lorentz condition for the vector field
A_{\mu}(x)

\partial^{\mu}A_{\mu}=0\ .

(13.5)

The standard situation in gauge field theory is that of having a gauge field, represented by a
p
-tensor field, and a
(p-1)
-tensorial gauge parameter. This is the case of all known gauge theories. We already mentioned Maxwell theory, or its non-abelian counterpart, the Yang-Mills theory, but this is also true for higher rank theories, like the topological
BF
theories in any dimensions
[
14
]
.
, described by a symmetric tensor field and by the symmetry (
13.2
), does not escape this rule. In this case, the commonly used gauge fixing condition is vectorial
[
211
,
214
,
219
]

\partial^{\nu}A_{\mu\nu}+\kappa\partial_{\mu}A=0\ .

(13.6)

Due to the presence of the parameter
\kappa
, the gauge fixing conditions (
13.4
) and (
13.6
) represent a class of covariant gauges. For instance, in (
13.6
) the particular case
\kappa=-\frac{1}{2}
corresponds to the “harmonic” gauge fixing
[
105
,
218
]
. The theory we are considering here is, again, quite peculiar. Not only because, as we have seen, it displays a “non-coupling” constant, but also because it is defined by the gauge transformation (
13.1
), which associates a scalar gauge parameter to a rank-2 symmetric tensor field. Concerning the gauge fixing procedure, one might therefore look at both sides of (
13.1
), each of which opens a different path. Looking at the right hand side of (
13.1
), one sees a scalar gauge parameter and this leads to adopt the scalar gauge condition (
13.4
). This standard way has been followed in
[
178
]
, where the propagators have been derived and the degrees of freedom have been studied. The scalar gauge condition (
13.4
) has two important drawbacks. The first is that the Landau gauge
\xi=0
turns out to be mandatory. The theory seems not to be defined outside this gauge. Now, it is true that physical results should not depend on the gauge choice, but, still, being forced to a unique choice is unpleasant, and it would be much preferable to find all the physical results in a generic gauge and to show that they do not, indeed, depend on a particular choice. The second reason is that the scalar gauge condition (
13.4
) does not allow to reach the limit of pure
, which necessarily needs the vector gauge condition (
13.2
). This results in a singularity both in the propagators of the theory and in the degrees of freedom, which indeed has been found in
[
178
]
: the theory with the scalar gauge condition (
13.4
) is not defined in the limit of pure
. The alternative, which we consider in this Section, is to
focus on the left hand side of (
13.1
), where the same symmetric rank-2 tensor field of
appears, and decide to adopt the same vectorial gauge condition (
13.6
) as
. In doing so, two questions should be answered :

1.

is the vector gauge condition a good gauge fixing, or, equivalently, do the propagators exist in the pure fractonic limit, possibly without being forced to choose a particular gauge ?

2.

in gauge field theory the gauge fixing condition serves to eliminate the redundant degrees of freedom which render infinite the Green functions’ generating functional
Z[J]

Z[J]=\int[dh_{\mu\nu}]e^{iS_{inv}+\int J^{\mu\nu}A_{\mu\nu}}\ .

(13.7)

Do the fact of imposing four (vector) gauge conditions instead of one (scalar) affect the number of physical degrees of freedom of the whole theory
S_{inv}
(
12.7
) ?

The above are legitimate and well posed questions and, naively, one might answer positively to both. To the first simply because four conditions are more than one, and one expects that they are more than enough to invert the gauge fixed action to find the propagators; to the second for the same reason : four conditions are more than one, and hence the degrees of freedom which are eliminated are too much and differ from those “killed” by the scalar gauge choice (
13.4
). We shall see that the propagators are not singular in the limits of pure fractons or pure
and that the number of physical degrees of freedom is the same for the two gauge fixing choices (
13.4
) and (
13.6
), which therefore are equivalent, with the advantage that the vectorial choice (
13.6
) does not constrain us to the Landau gauge and allows to easily recover
.

The analysis of the gauge structure of the covariant fracton theory presented in this Section is organized as follows :

In Section
13.1
, starting from the symmetry (
13.1
), the action of the theory is introduced, which consists of two terms:
and a fractonic term. The vector gauge condition (
13.6
) is realized by adding a gauge fixing term to the action. In Section
13.2
the propagators are computed, and the singularities are studied, which correspond to particular phases of the theory. In Section
13.3
we study the degrees of freedom, and we verify that their counting coincides with the known one
[
178
]
, without the drawback of being confined to the Landau gauge, which reassures of the fact that the number of degrees of freedom does not depend on a particular choice and that the alternative vectorial gauge fixing condition (
13.6
) is, indeed, a good one. In Section
13.4
we discuss our results.

13.1
The model

Let us consider the 4D theory of a symmetric tensor field
A_{\mu\nu}(x)
which transforms as (
13.1
).
The choice (
13.3
) is motivated by the fact that the theory defined by the symmetry (
13.1
) describes the so called “fractons”.
The most general action invariant under (
13.1
) was found in Section
12
to be

S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})=g_{1}S_{fract}+g_{2}S_{LG}\ ,

(13.8)

where

\displaystyle S_{fract}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x\left(A^{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}A_{\rho\nu}-A_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}A^{\mu\nu}\right)

(13.9)

\displaystyle S_{LG}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x\left(-A\partial^{2}A+A_{\mu\nu}\partial^{2}A^{\mu\nu}+2A\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}A^{\mu\nu}-2A^{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu\rho}\right)\ ,

(13.10)

and
A(x)
is the Minkowskian trace of
A_{\mu\nu}(x)

A=\eta^{\mu\nu}A_{\mu\nu}\ .

(13.11)

The action (
13.8
) appears to be the linear combination of two terms, which we recognize to be the action
S_{LG}
of

[
105
,
211
]
and the pure covariant fractonic action previously described
[
178
,
4
]
. The actions
S_{LG}
and
S_{fract}
are separately invariant under (
13.1
)

\delta S_{LG}=\delta S_{fract}=0\ ,

(13.12)

and
g_{1,2}
are constants, on which we will come back in a moment. Notice that while the space of 4D local integrated functionals invariant under (
13.1
) is the linear combination (
13.8
) of two elements, the infinitesimal diffeomorphism symmetry (
13.2
) uniquely determines one functional only : the
action
S_{LG}
(
13.10
)

\delta_{diff}S_{LG}=0\ ,

(13.13)

under which the fractonic action
S_{fract}
(
13.9
) is not invariant, as we have seen in (
12.12
). In other words, the “fractonic” symmetry (
13.1
) is less constraining than the diffeomorphism transformation (
13.2
), of which it is a particular case. The action (
13.8
) actually depends on one constant only, because of the possibility of redefining the gauge field by a multiplicative constant
c
without affecting the physical content of the theory:
A_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow cA_{\mu\nu}
. Nevertheless,
we will keep both
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, in order to track the contributions of the gravitational (
g_{1}\rightarrow 0
) and the fractonic (
g_{2}\rightarrow 0
) parts.
Concerning the gauge fixing, the standard way to realize the condition (
13.6
) is to add to the invariant action (
13.8
) the gauge fixing term

S_{gf}(\xi,\kappa)=-\frac{1}{2\xi}\int d^{4}x\left(\partial^{\nu}A_{\mu\nu}+\kappa\partial_{\mu}A\right)^{2}\ ,

(13.14)

as it has been done in
[
215
,
214
,
219
,
218
]
for
alone. In a fully equivalent way, (
13.14
) can be linearized by means of
a Lagrange multiplier
b^{\mu}(x)
, also known as Nakanishi-Lautrup field
[
107
,
108
]
:

S_{gf}(\xi,\kappa)=\int d^{4}x\left[b^{\mu}\left(\partial^{\nu}A_{\mu\nu}+\kappa\partial_{\mu}A\right)+\frac{\xi}{2}b_{\mu}b^{\mu}\right]\ .

(13.15)

In
S_{gf}(\xi,\kappa)
two gauge fixing parameters appear :
\xi
and
\kappa
. The first -
\xi
- governs the type of gauge fixing. For instance
\xi=0
and
\xi=1
are respectively the Landau and Feynman gauges. The second -
\kappa
- tunes the type of gauge fixing picked up by
\xi
. For instance, the Landau gauge in
corresponds to a class of gauge choices, and it is realized by
\xi=0
and generic
\kappa
.

13.2
Propagators

To find the propagators of the theory, we write the gauge fixed action

S(g_{1},g_{2};\xi,\kappa)=S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})+S_{gf}(\xi,\kappa)

(13.16)

in momentum space :

\int d^{4}p\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}(p)&\tilde{b}^{\gamma}(p)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\Omega}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}(p)&{\Lambda}^{*}_{\mu\nu,\lambda}(p)\\
{\Lambda}_{\gamma,\alpha\beta}(p)&{H}_{\gamma\lambda}(p)\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}(-p)\\
\tilde{b}^{\lambda}(-p)\end{pmatrix},

(13.17)

where
{\Omega}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}(p)
,
{\Lambda}^{*}_{\mu\nu,\lambda}(p)
and
{H}_{\gamma\lambda}(p)
are
p
-dependent tensor operators. The propagators of the theory are obtained by inverting the operator matrix appearing in (
13.17
). In order to do this, it is useful to write
{\Omega}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}(p)
,
{\Lambda}_{\alpha\beta,\mu}(p)
and
{H}_{\mu\alpha}(p)
on the corresponding tensorial basis, as follows

\displaystyle{\Omega}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{t}\,A_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}+{u}\,B_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}+{v}\,C_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}+{z}\,D_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}+{w}\,E_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

(13.18)

\displaystyle{\Lambda}_{\alpha\beta,\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}\left[{f}\,(d_{\alpha\mu}p_{\beta}+d_{\beta\mu}p_{\alpha})+{g}\,d_{\alpha\beta}p_{\mu}+{l}\,e_{\alpha\beta}p_{\mu}\right]

(13.19)

\displaystyle{H}_{\mu\alpha}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{r}\,d_{\mu\alpha}+{s}\,e_{\mu\alpha}\ ,

(13.20)

where
e_{\mu\nu}(p)
and
d_{\mu\nu}(p)
are transverse and longitudinal projectors, respectively,

e_{\mu\nu}=\frac{p_{\mu}p_{\nu}}{p^{2}}\quad;\quad d_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-e_{\mu\nu}\ ,

(13.21)

and the rank-4 tensor
{\Omega}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}(p)
(
13.18
) is expanded on a basis of operators

X_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}\equiv(A,B,C,D,E)_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

(13.22)

which can be found in Appendix
10.B
, together with their properties. The coefficients appearing in (
13.18
), (
13.19
) and (
13.20
) are found to be

\displaystyle{t}

\displaystyle=(g_{1}+2g_{2})p^{2}

;

\displaystyle{u}

\displaystyle=0

;

\displaystyle{v}

\displaystyle=(g_{1}-g_{2})p^{2}

;

\displaystyle{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g_{1}p^{2}

;

\displaystyle{w}

\displaystyle=0

(13.23)

\displaystyle{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}

;

\displaystyle{g}

\displaystyle=\kappa

;

\displaystyle{l}

\displaystyle=1+\kappa

;

\displaystyle{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi}{2}

;

\displaystyle{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi}{2}\ .

(13.24)

The propagators of the theory are organized in a matrix of tensor operators as well

\begin{pmatrix}\hat{{G}}^{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}&\hat{{G}}^{\alpha\beta,\tau}\\
\hat{{G}}^{*\lambda,\rho\sigma}&\hat{{G}}^{\lambda\tau}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(13.25)

where

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}=\langle\tilde{A}_{\alpha\beta}\;\tilde{A}_{\rho\sigma}\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{t}\,A_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}+\hat{u}\,B_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}+\hat{v}\,C_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}+\hat{z}\,D_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}+\hat{w}\,E_{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}

(13.26)

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{\alpha\beta,\rho}=\langle\tilde{A}_{\alpha\beta}\;\tilde{b}_{\rho}\rangle

\displaystyle=i\left[\hat{f}\,(d_{\alpha\rho}p_{\beta}+d_{\beta\rho}p_{\alpha})+\hat{g}\,d_{\alpha\beta}p_{\rho}+\hat{l}\,e_{\alpha\beta}p_{\rho}\right]

(13.27)

\displaystyle\hat{G}_{\alpha\rho}=\langle\tilde{b}_{\alpha}\;\tilde{b}_{\rho}\rangle

\displaystyle=\hat{r}\,d_{\alpha\rho}+\hat{s}\,e_{\alpha\rho}\ ,

(13.28)

and the set of coefficients

\left\{\hat{t}\,,\,\hat{u}\,,\,\hat{v}\,,\,\hat{z}\,,\,\hat{w}\,,\,\hat{f}\,,\,\hat{g}\,,\,\hat{l}\,,\,\hat{r}\,,\,\hat{s}\right\}

(13.29)

are determined by the request that the matrix of propagators (
13.25
) satisfies

\begin{pmatrix}{\Omega}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}&{\Lambda}^{*}_{\mu\nu,\lambda}\\
{\Lambda}_{\gamma,\alpha\beta}&{H}_{\gamma\lambda}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{G}^{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}&\hat{G}^{\alpha\beta,\tau}\\
\hat{G}^{*\lambda,\rho\sigma}&\hat{G}^{\lambda\tau}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{I}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \rho\sigma}&0\\
0&\delta_{\gamma}^{\ \tau}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(13.30)

where
\mathcal{I}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}
is the rank-4 tensor identity

\mathcal{I}_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}(\eta_{\mu\rho}\eta_{\nu\sigma}+\eta_{\mu\sigma}\eta_{\nu\rho})\ .

(13.31)

In Appendix
11.B.1
we show that the four tensor equations (
13.30
) are solved by

\displaystyle\hat{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\kappa+1)}{(\kappa+1)(g_{1}+2g_{2})p^{2}}

;

\displaystyle\hat{u}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(4\kappa+1)-2\xi(g_{1}+2g_{2})}{(\kappa+1)^{2}(g_{1}+2g_{2})p^{2}}

(13.32)

\displaystyle\hat{v}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(g_{1}-g_{2})p^{2}}

;

\displaystyle\hat{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\xi}{(2\xi g_{1}-1)p^{2}}

(13.33)

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=\frac{-4\kappa}{(\kappa+1)(g_{1}+2g_{2})p^{2}}

;

\displaystyle\hat{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2}{(2\xi g_{1}-1)p^{2}}

(13.34)

\displaystyle\hat{g}

\displaystyle=0

;

\displaystyle\hat{l}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(\kappa+1)p^{2}}

(13.35)

\displaystyle\hat{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{4g_{1}}{(2\xi g_{1}-1)}

;

\displaystyle\hat{s}

\displaystyle=0\ .

(13.36)

Now we can answer the first of our questions, concerning the fractonic limit : the vector gauge fixing condition (
13.6
) is a good one not only for
(
g_{1}=0
), but also for the pure fractonic case (
g_{2}=0
), as expected. On the other hand, we see that the propagators are singular in four cases :

\displaystyle g_{1}+2g_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(13.37)

\displaystyle g_{1}-g_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(13.38)

\displaystyle 2\xi g_{1}-1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(13.39)

\displaystyle\kappa+1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\ .

(13.40)

For what concerns the singularity (
13.40
), it simply implies that the “secondary” gauge fixing parameter
\kappa
, which tunes the gauge fixing choice of the “primary” parameter
\xi
in
S_{gf}
(
13.15
), should be

\kappa\neq-1\ ,

(13.41)

as it happens also in

[
215
,
218
,
214
,
219
]
. The remaining singularities involve the action parameters
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
and the gauge fixing parameter
\xi
. The poles in the propagators give us information on the structure of the theory. For instance, they might signal the presence of masses, possibly not standard, as in the topologically massive Maxwell-Chern-Simons theory
[
70
]
. Or they might indicate the presence of phase transitions in the theory, like in QCD
[
220
,
221
]
, or in the sigma model
[
222
,
223
]
, or in the fracton theory itself
[
178
]
. Therefore, the singularities appearing in the propagators of the theory
S_{inv}
should be treated separately and with care.

•

{g_{1}=g_{2}}

In this case, after a field redefinition, the invariant action (
13.8
) is

\left.S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})\right|_{g_{1}=g_{2}}=\int d^{4}p\ (\tilde{A}p^{2}\tilde{A}-2\tilde{A}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}+\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}p^{\rho}p_{\mu}\tilde{A}_{\nu\rho})\ .

(13.42)

Notice that defining

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\rho}\equiv p^{\rho}\tilde{A}-p_{\nu}\tilde{A}^{\rho\nu}\ ,

(13.43)

the action (
13.42
) trivializes into

\left.S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})\right|_{g_{1}=g_{2}}=-\int d^{4}p\ \tilde{\mathcal{A}}^{\rho}\tilde{\mathcal{A}}_{\rho}\ ,

(13.44)

which does not contain any kinetic term. Hence, the singularity at
g_{1}=g_{2}
is explained as a point where the theory trivializes and does not propagate, and this case will be excluded from now on.

•

{g_{1}+2g_{2}=0}

In this case the invariant action (
13.8
), after a field redefinition, reads

\left.S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})\right|_{g_{1}+2g_{2}=0}=\int d^{4}p\ \left(\tilde{A}p^{2}\tilde{A}-3\tilde{A}_{\mu\nu}p^{2}\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}-2\tilde{A}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}+4\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}p^{\rho}p_{\mu}\tilde{A}_{\nu\rho}\right)\ .

(13.45)

We see that with this choice the action does not depend on the trace
\tilde{A}(p)
. In fact, defining

{\bar{A}}_{\mu\nu}(p)\equiv\tilde{A}_{\mu\nu}(p)-\frac{1}{4}\eta_{\mu\nu}\tilde{A}(p)\ ,

(13.46)

with

{\bar{A}}(p)=0\ ,

(13.47)

the action (
13.45
) can be written in terms of
\bar{A}_{\mu\nu}(p)
only :

\left.S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})\right|_{g_{1}+2g_{2}=0}=\int d^{4}p\ \left(-3{\bar{A}}_{\mu\nu}p^{2}{\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}}+4{\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}}p^{\rho}p_{\mu}{\bar{A}_{\nu\rho}}\right)\ .

(13.48)

Hence, in this case the theory is traceless, and the singularity at the point
g_{1}+2g_{2}=0
indicates a change in the counting of the degrees of freedom, as we shall explicitly show. The gauge fixing term (
13.15
) does not depend on the trace
A(x)
anymore and, hence, on the gauge fixing parameter
\kappa
. In momentum space it reads

S_{gf}(\xi)=\int d^{4}p\ \left(-i\tilde{b}_{\mu}p_{\nu}\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{\xi}{2}\tilde{b}_{\mu}\tilde{b}^{\mu}\right)\ .

(13.49)

The propagators of the traceless theory are well defined, and the coefficients, computed in Appendix
11.B.2
, are

\displaystyle\hat{t}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3p^{2}}

\displaystyle\hat{u}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\xi}{(2\xi-1)p^{2}}

(13.50)

\displaystyle\hat{v}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3p^{2}}

\displaystyle\hat{z}

\displaystyle=\frac{-4\xi}{(4\xi+1)p^{2}}

(13.51)

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hat{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(4\xi+1)p^{2}}

(13.52)

\displaystyle\hat{g}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\hat{l}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2}{(2\xi-1)p^{2}}

(13.53)

\displaystyle\hat{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{8}{(4\xi+1)}

\displaystyle\hat{s}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{(2\xi-1)}\ .

(13.54)

From the above coefficients we see that the particular values of the primary gauge parameter
\xi=-1/4
and
\xi=1/2
should be excluded.

•

{2\xi g_{1}-1=0}

We first notice that this singularity is not present in the pure
case
g_{1}=0
, as it is readily seen from the coefficients
\hat{z}
(
13.33
),
\hat{f}
(
13.34
) and
\hat{r}
(
13.36
) of the propagators.
Then, we remark that
g_{1}
is a physical parameter, hence cannot depend on
\xi
, which is a gauge, unphysical, parameter. This means that
2\xi g_{1}-1=0
should be interpreted as a singularity in
\xi
as a function of
g_{1}
, and not
viceversa
. In other words, we shall not exclude values of
g_{1}
in order to admit a particular gauge, but, rather, the singularity must be read as a condition on the gauge fixing parameter
\xi
:

\xi\neq\frac{1}{2g_{1}}\ .

(13.55)

We consider the gauge fixing term (
13.14
), before the the introduction of the Lagrange multiplier
b^{\mu}(x)
. At
2\xi g_{1}-1=0
the gauge fixed action is

\left.S(g_{1},g_{2};\xi,\kappa)\right|_{2\xi g_{1}-1=0}=\int d^{4}p\ \left[(g_{2}-g_{1}\kappa^{2})\tilde{A}p^{2}\tilde{A}-(g_{2}-g_{1})\tilde{A}_{\mu\nu}p^{2}\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}+\right.\\
\left.-2(g_{2}+g_{1}\kappa)\tilde{A}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\tilde{A^{\mu\nu}}+2(g_{2}-g_{1})\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}p_{\mu}p^{\rho}\tilde{A}_{\nu\rho}\right]\ .

(13.56)

Using the general results of Appendix
11.B.1
it is easy to verify that this theory does not have propagators. As a remark, if we choose also
\kappa+1=0
, we find a curious result

\left.S(g_{1},g_{2};\xi,\kappa)\right|_{2\xi g_{1}-1=0,\kappa+1=0}=(g_{2}-g_{1})S_{LG}\ .

(13.57)

This means that with the particular gauge choice which involves both the singularities in the two gauge parameters
\xi
and
\kappa
, the gauge fixing procedure fails in choosing one representative for each gauge orbit, which is what the gauge fixing is supposed to do. In fact, according to (
13.57
), in this particular gauge, the fracton contribution disappears, and the gauge fixed action coincides with
S_{LG}
alone, which still needs to be gauge fixed. It also appears the fact which we already know that for
g_{1}=g_{2}
, which is the trivial, non propagating case already considered, the action vanishes. We thus showed that for
\xi=1/2g_{1}
and
\kappa=-1
the gauge fixed action
S(g_{1},g_{2};\xi,\kappa)
coincides with the invariant, not gauge fixed, action
S_{LG}
.

13.3
Degrees of freedom

The counting of the degrees of freedom of the theory described by the action
S_{inv}
(
13.8
) is a crucial point. This is true in general, but for this model it is even more true. What we already know from
[
178
]
is that, if we adopt the standard scalar gauge choice (
13.4
), which is the natural one when dealing with a gauge transformation depending on a scalar parameter, the degrees of freedom turn out to be six, as in
alone (five in the traceless case), as if the fractonic contribution
S_{fract}
(
13.9
) was not present. However, in
[
178
]
the choice of the Landau gauge appears to be mandatory, which is rather unpleasant, although the physical results should not depend on the gauge choice. Still, it would be preferable to avoid such a restriction, which instead seems unavoidable in the scalar gauge. This, together with the fact that
cannot be reached as a limit, leads us to conclude that the scalar gauge choice is not that natural, as the direct application of the Faddeev-Popov procedure would suggest. The aim is therefore to see whether the alternative and, at first sight, exotic choice of the
vector
gauge condition (
13.6
) is an acceptable, and possibly better, one. In the previous Subsection we passed the first test: we have seen that the vector gauge condition leads to well defined propagators, with a pole which corresponds to the traceless theory, in accordance to the scalar case
[
178
]
. In this Subsection, instead, we face the trickier point of the counting of the degrees of freedom. Not only we should recover the known result, but, and more important, we should show that the number of degrees of freedom does not depend on the gauge choice, which was impossible with the scalar gauge condition (
13.4
) frozen in the Landau gauge. This fact is not obvious, since the role of the gauge fixing is to eliminate the redundant degrees of freedom, in order to render finite the path integral
Z[J]
(
13.7
), and the justified fear is that the four conditions represented by the vector choice (
13.6
) might lead us to underestimate the degrees of freedom with respect to the unique scalar condition (
13.4
).
The usual way to count the degrees of freedom is to look for the constraints deriving from the equations of motion of the gauge fixed action

S(g_{1},g_{2};\xi,\kappa)=S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})+S_{gf}(\xi,\kappa)\ ,

(13.58)

where the invariant action
S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})
and the gauge fixing term
S_{gf}(\xi,\kappa)
are given by (
13.8
) and (
13.15
), respectively. In momentum space, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}}=

\displaystyle\;2g_{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}p^{2}\tilde{A}+2(g_{1}-g_{2})p^{2}\tilde{A}_{\mu\nu}-2g_{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}-2g_{2}p_{\mu}p_{\nu}\tilde{A}

(13.59)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(2g_{2}-g_{1})p^{\alpha}(p_{\mu}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\nu}+p_{\nu}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu})+\frac{i}{2}(p_{\nu}\tilde{b}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}\tilde{b}_{\nu})+i\kappa\eta_{\mu\nu}p_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta\tilde{b}^{\mu}}=

\displaystyle-ip^{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}-i\kappa p_{\mu}\tilde{A}+\xi\tilde{b}_{\mu}=0\ .

(13.60)

If our task was just to count the degrees of freedom, given that they must not depend on the gauge choice, we would immediately find the result by choosing
\xi=\kappa=0
in
S_{gf}
(
13.15
), which belongs to the class of Landau gauges. The
\tilde{b}^{\mu}
-equation of motion (
13.60
) gives

p^{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}=0\ ,

(13.61)

which are the four constraints needed to recover the six degrees of freedom (five in the traceless case) which we expect for the symmetric rank-2 tensor field
A_{\mu\nu}(x)
. But we want more, that is to show that this number does not depend on the gauge choice, which would render the vector gauge condition a good one under any respect. Achieved that, the vector choice would be even preferable to the scalar one, since the Landau gauge would not be the only possibility, and
could be obtained as a limit. Hence we proceed without choosing a particular gauge, and
we saturate (
13.59
) with
\eta^{\mu\nu}
,
e^{\mu\nu}(p)
(
13.21
) and
p^{\mu}
:

\displaystyle\eta^{\mu\nu}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=2(g_{1}+2g_{2})\left(p^{2}\tilde{A}-p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}\right)+i(1+4\kappa)p_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0

(13.62)

\displaystyle e^{\mu\nu}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=i(1+\kappa)p_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0

(13.63)

\displaystyle p^{\nu}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\tilde{A}^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=2g_{1}p^{\alpha}\left(p^{2}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}-p_{\mu}p^{\beta}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\beta}\right)+ip^{2}\tilde{b}_{\mu}+i(1+2\kappa)p_{\mu}p_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0\ .

(13.64)

Multiplying (
13.60
) by
p^{\mu}
, we get

p^{\mu}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\tilde{b}^{\mu}}=ip_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}+i\kappa p^{2}\tilde{A}-\xi p_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0\ .

(13.65)

We separately study the generic case
g_{1}+2g_{2}\neq 0
and the traceless case
g_{1}+2g_{2}=0
.

Case
g_{1}+2g_{2}\neq 0

From (
13.63
), remembering that
\kappa+1\neq 0
, we get the condition

p_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0\ ,

(13.66)

which, plugged in (
13.60
), (
13.62
), (
13.64
) and (
13.65
), yields

\displaystyle\xi\tilde{b}_{\mu}=i\left(p^{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}+\kappa p_{\mu}\tilde{A}\right)

(13.67)

\displaystyle(g_{1}+2g_{2})\left(p^{2}\tilde{A}-p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}\right)=0

(13.68)

\displaystyle 2g_{1}p^{\alpha}\left(p^{2}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}-p_{\mu}p^{\beta}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\beta}\right)+ip^{2}\tilde{b}_{\mu}=0

(13.69)

\displaystyle ip_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}+i\kappa p^{2}\tilde{A}=0\ .

(13.70)

Now, since we are outside the critical point
g_{1}+2g_{2}=0
(
13.37
), from (
13.68
) and (
13.70
) we have

\displaystyle p^{2}\tilde{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}

(13.71)

\displaystyle-\kappa p^{2}\tilde{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}\ ,

(13.72)

that is

(1+\kappa)p^{2}\tilde{A}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad p^{2}\tilde{A}=0\ ,

(13.73)

hence

p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\tilde{A}^{\alpha\beta}=0\ .

(13.74)

Notice that the conditions (
13.73
) and (
13.74
) are the ones holding for
alone (
g_{1}=0
)
[
214
,
219
]
. It appears, therefore, that the fracton contribution (
g_{2}=0
) (
13.9
) to the total invariant action (
13.8
) is irrelevant as far as the degrees of freedom are concerned, which is an unexpected result. Nonetheless we can directly check this result. Substituting the conditions (
13.73
) and (
13.74
) into (
13.69
), we find

-ip^{2}\tilde{b}_{\mu}=2g_{1}p^{2}p^{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}\ .

(13.75)

Using (
13.67
) and (
13.73
), assuming
\xi\neq 0
,
i.e.
excluding for the moment the Landau gauge, equation (
13.75
) becomes

(2g_{1}\xi-1)p^{2}p^{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}=0\ .

(13.76)

We previously studied the case
2g_{1}\xi-1=0
, which we now exclude. This means that

p^{2}p^{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}=0

(13.77)

and, from (
13.75
),

p^{2}\tilde{b}_{\mu}=0\ .

(13.78)

We now define

\tilde{J}_{\alpha}\equiv p^{\beta}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\beta}\ ,

(13.79)

which, because of (
13.74
), is a conserved current

p^{\alpha}\tilde{J}_{\alpha}=0\ .

(13.80)

The solution of (
13.80
) is

\tilde{J}_{\alpha}=\epsilon_{\alpha\mu\nu\rho}p^{\mu}\tilde{B}^{\nu\rho}\ ,

(13.81)

where
\tilde{B}^{\nu\rho}
is a generic antisymmetric tensor. Plugging (
13.79
) in (
13.77
), we have

p^{2}\tilde{J}_{\alpha}=p^{2}\epsilon_{\alpha\mu\nu\rho}p^{\mu}\tilde{B}^{\nu\rho}=0\ ,

(13.82)

and therefore

p^{2}\tilde{B}_{\rho\lambda}=p_{\rho}\tilde{B}_{\lambda}-p_{\lambda}\tilde{B}_{\rho}\ .

(13.83)

From (
13.81
) we then deduce that the current
J_{\alpha}
vanishes

\tilde{J}_{\alpha}=p^{\beta}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\beta}=0\ .

(13.84)

We now come back to the Landau gauge
\xi=0
, which has been excluded in achieving the above result. We now show that (
13.84
) holds also in this case. The
\tilde{b}^{\mu}
-equation of motion in the Landau gauge is

p^{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}=-\kappa p_{\mu}\tilde{A}\ .

(13.85)

The degrees of freedom must not depend on the gauge choice. We therefore choose
\kappa=0
and we get

{p^{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\alpha\mu}=0}\ ,

(13.86)

which represents four constraints on the 4D symmetric tensor field
\tilde{A}_{\mu\nu}(p)
. Hence the number of degrees of freedom are six, at least if
g_{1}+2g_{2}\neq 0
. This coincides with the number of degrees of freedom of
alone
[
214
,
219
]
.
We have seen that
g_{1}+2g_{2}=0
corresponds to the traceless case, which is interesting and will be treated separately.

Case
g_{1}+2g_{2}=0

As we have seen in Section
13.2
, this case corresponds to the traceless theory. The gauge fixed action can be written in terms of the traceless field
\bar{A}_{\mu\nu}(x)
(
13.46
), with
\kappa=0
, and, in momentum space, it reads

\left.S(g_{1},g_{2};\xi,\kappa)\right|_{g_{1}+2g_{2}=0,\kappa=0}=\int d^{4}p\ \left(-3\bar{A}_{\mu\nu}p^{2}\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}+4\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}p_{\mu}p^{\rho}\bar{A}_{\nu\rho}-i\tilde{b}_{\mu}p_{\nu}\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}+\frac{\xi}{2}\tilde{b}_{\mu}\tilde{b}^{\mu}\right)\ ,

(13.87)

whose equations of motion are

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=-6p^{2}\bar{A}_{\mu\nu}+4p^{\alpha}\left(p_{\mu}\bar{A}_{\alpha\nu}+p_{\nu}\bar{A}_{\alpha\mu}\right)-2\eta_{\mu\nu}p^{\alpha}p^{\beta}\bar{A}_{\alpha\beta}

(13.88)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{i}{2}\left(p_{\nu}\tilde{b}_{\mu}+p_{\mu}\tilde{b}_{\nu}\right)-\tfrac{i}{4}\eta_{\mu\nu}p_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S}{\delta\tilde{b}^{\mu}}

\displaystyle=-ip^{\nu}\bar{A}_{\mu\nu}+\xi\tilde{b}_{\mu}=0\ ,

(13.89)

where we remind that, for a traceless, symmetric tensor field

\frac{\delta\bar{A}^{\alpha\beta}}{\delta\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}\delta_{\nu}^{\beta}+\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}\delta_{\mu}^{\beta}\right)-\frac{1}{4}\eta_{\mu\nu}\eta^{\alpha\beta}\ .

(13.90)

Saturating (
13.88
) with
e^{\mu\nu}(p)
and
p^{\nu}
, we get

\displaystyle e^{\mu\nu}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=ip_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0

(13.91)

\displaystyle p^{\nu}\frac{\delta S}{\delta\bar{A}^{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=-2p^{2}p^{\alpha}\bar{A}_{\alpha\mu}+2p_{\mu}p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\bar{A}^{\alpha\beta}+\frac{i}{2}p^{2}\tilde{b}_{\mu}-\frac{i}{4}p_{\mu}p_{\alpha}\tilde{b}^{\alpha}=0\ .

(13.92)

From saturating (
13.89
) with
p^{\mu}
, and (
13.91
) we have

p_{\alpha}p_{\beta}\bar{A}^{\alpha\beta}=0\ .

(13.93)

If
\xi\neq 0
, the Lagrange multiplier
\tilde{b}_{\mu}(p)
can be obtained from (
13.89
) and, plugged in (
13.92
), using (
13.93
), we get

(4\xi+1)p^{2}p^{\alpha}\bar{A}_{\alpha\mu}=0\ .

(13.94)

We have already excluded the gauge choice
4\xi+1=0
, which is the propagator singularity
2\xi g_{1}-1=0
at
g_{1}+2g_{2}=0
, hence

p^{2}p^{\alpha}\bar{A}_{\alpha\mu}=0\ .

(13.95)

Now, using the same argument of Section
13.3
, involving
\tilde{J}_{\alpha}(p)
(
13.79
) and
\tilde{B}^{\nu\rho}(p)
(
13.81
), Eqs. (
13.93
) and (
13.95
) imply the four constraints

p^{\alpha}\bar{A}_{\alpha\mu}=0\ .

(13.96)

On the other hand, when
\xi=0
,
i.e.
in the Landau gauge, the equation of motion of the Lagrange multiplier (
13.89
) directly gives the constraint (
13.96
). Hence, in all cases we have four constraints on a traceless rank-2 symmetric tensor. Therefore, when
g_{1}+2g_{2}=0
, the degrees of freedom are five.

The results for the different values of the action constants
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
and of the gauge fixing parameters
\xi
and
\kappa
are summarized in Table
3
.

13.4
Final remarks

The aim of this Section was to find a well defined gauge fixed theory, not constrained to a particular gauge choice, and where both limiting cases, fractonic and linearized gravity, could be reached smoothly. The idea is simply to look at the left hand side of (
13.1
) and take the vectorial gauge fixing choice (
13.6
),
which, again, is the most general covariant one and is the same of linearized gravity. It is not obvious at all that it could work. First, what does it mean that “it works” ? Besides the possibility of getting both fractons and gravitons as a limit, we asked two minimal requirements: that propagators are defined in a generic gauge, and that the counting of the degrees of freedom of the theory coincides with the one found in
[
178
]
, but without referring to a particular gauge choice. The result is that both requests have been achieved. We have now a covariant gauge fixed theory of fractons and linearized gravity, which has six degrees of freedom, or five, since for a particular combination of fractons and gravitons the theory is traceless. All the results are gauge independent, therefore the vector gauge fixing (
13.6
) seems to be a better choice than the standard scalar one (
13.4
).

14
Fractons with Boundary

We now have a 4D covariant theory that describes fracton quasiparticles, whose gauge structure is peculiar but well defined. We are thus able to apply the
procedure of Chapter
2
to study the consequences of the introduction of a flat boundary, with the aim of investigating whether an induced 3D theory exists and, in that case, which is its physical meaning. Tightly related is the question of the existence of an algebraic structure on the boundary. The theory we are dealing with is not topological, and it was a common belief that only topological field theories show non-trivial boundary physics, until the study of the Maxwell case. Moreover, we have seen that when a boundary is introduced in a
, the gauge symmetry plays a fundamental role, since it is the breaking of gauge invariance caused by the presence of the boundary that gives rise to an algebraic structure on the boundary which “holographically” induces a lower-dimensional gauge theory. The fracton symmetry (
12.5
) is unusual, due to the presence of a double derivative, and considering a boundary on such a model
a\ priori
may have a non-trivial outcome. On the other hand it would not be the first case of a non-topological
exhibiting an induced theory on the boundary. In fact, as we mentioned, this also happens in the case of Maxwell theory in 3D
[
49
]
and 4D
[
1
]
, and we know from Section
12
that fracton models share many similarities with the electromagnetic theory
[
160
,
4
]
. Additionally, it has been shown that a fractonic
\theta
-term, which is a pure boundary term when
\theta
is constant, gives rise to a 3D Chern-Simons-like term and a generalized Witten effect
[
175
]
, with important consequences in condensed matter systems
[
55
,
184
]
.
A non-covariant Chern-Simons-like term of that kind was also studied in
[
185
]
, where the higher-spin formalism is associated to dipolar behaviours in the context of Hall systems. In order to find these answers, we proceed as follows

In particular in Section
14.1
the boundary is introduced in the invariant action (
12.7
) described in Section
12
together with the gauge fixing and the most general boundary term. From the total action the
and the most general
are computed. Because of the presence of the boundary, the Ward identities of the theory are broken, and this allows to identify the boundary
,
represented by two traceless symmetric rank-2 tensor fields. Moreover, the broken Ward identities give rise to an algebraic structure, which can be identified as a generalized
algebra, which, in Section
14.2
, we interpret as canonical commutators of a 3D action. In Section
14.3
the bulk/boundary correspondence is obtained by requiring that the
of the induced 3D action are compatible with the
of the 4D bulk theory. This can be achieved by suitably tuning the parameters appearing in the
and in the 3D action. To physically interpret the 3D theory we found,
in Section
14.4
we study its
, which appear to be
Gauss and Ampère-like laws for the boundary tensor fields, exactly as in the “ordinary” fracton theory. In particular our boundary theory can be identified with a
traceless fracton model. In Section
14.5
we analyze the effect of taking into account discrete symmetries such as parity (
\mathcal{P}
) and time reversal (
\mathcal{T}
). Finally in Section
14.6
we discuss our results.

14.1
The model with boundary

The action

We introduce the boundary in the invariant action
S_{inv}
(
12.7
) by means of a Heaviside step function in the action :

S_{bulk}=\int d^{4}x\;\theta(x^{3})\left\{\frac{g_{1}}{6}F^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu\rho}+g_{2}\left(\frac{1}{4}F^{\mu}_{\ \mu\nu}F_{\rho}^{\ \rho\nu}-\frac{1}{6}F^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu\rho}\right)\right\}\ .

(14.1)

Notice that in what follows we cannot just set
g_{1}=0
and restrict our results to
alone, because

S_{LG}
(
12.9
) is uniquely defined by the infinitesimal diffeomorphism transformation (
12.6
), and not by its subset (
12.5
).
The transformations (
12.6
) and (
12.5
) differs in two aspects : the first, (
12.6
), depends on a vector gauge parameter, while (
12.5
) has a scalar gauge parameter, hence the former is more restrictive. Secondly, (
12.6
) and (
12.5
) depend on one and two derivatives respectively. As we have seen, this results in a mismatch in the mass dimensions. In fact, since from the action (
14.1
) we have
[A_{\mu\nu}]=1
, due to the double derivatives in (
12.5
) it must be
[\Lambda]=-1
, which is an exotic dimension assignment for the scalar gauge parameter. Moreover, on the
x^{3}
-boundary, the field
A_{\mu\nu}(x)
and its
\partial_{3}
-derivative must be treated as independent fields
[
49
,
1
,
147
,
145
]
, like we have seen for the Maxwell case of Chapter
6
. Hence, on the boundary we define

\tilde{A}_{\mu\nu}\equiv\partial_{3}A_{\mu\nu}|_{x^{3}=0}\ ,

(14.2)

with
[\tilde{A}_{\mu\nu}]=2
.
We add to the invariant action
S_{inv}
(
12.7
) the gauge-fixing term

S_{gf}=\int d^{4}x\;\theta(x^{3})b^{\mu}A_{\mu 3}\ ,

(14.3)

which is of a vectorial type, as discussed in the previous Section
13
, where
b^{\mu}(x)
is a Nakanishi-Lautrup Lagrange multiplier
[
107
,
108
]
implementing the axial gauge condition

A_{\mu 3}=0\ .

(14.4)

As a consequence of the fact that the field and its
\partial_{3}
-derivative on the boundary are independent quantities, together with the usual external field
J^{ab}(x)
coupled to
A_{ab}(x)
, it is necessary to couple a source
\tilde{J}^{ab}(x)
also to the
\partial_{3}
-derivative of
A_{ab}(x)
on the boundary. The external source term is then

S_{J}=\int d^{4}x\left[\theta(x^{3})J^{ab}A_{ab}+\delta(x^{3})\tilde{J}^{ab}\tilde{A}_{ab}\right]\ .

(14.5)

The presence of a boundary in a
naturally rises the question of which
should be assigned to the quantum fields and/or their derivatives. A possible way is to impose them by hand, but one should worry about the dependence of the results on the particular choice. We have seen that this arbitrariness affecting
with boundary has been elegantly solved by Symanzik in his pioneering paper
[
10
]
, where a scalar
with boundary was considered. According to Symanzik’s approach, the
are not imposed by hand, but are determined by the theory itself. This is achieved by adding a boundary term to the action, as the most general one, satisfying the requests of locality, power counting and 3D Lorentz invariance. The
are then determined from the
, modified by the boundary term

S_{bd}=\int d^{4}x\delta(x^{3})\left[\xi_{0}A_{ab}A^{ab}+\xi_{1}\tilde{A}_{ab}A^{ab}+\xi_{2}\epsilon^{abc}A_{ai}\partial_{b}A_{c}^{i}+\xi_{3}A^{2}+\xi_{4}\tilde{A}A\right]\ ,

(14.6)

where, due to the gauge condition (
14.4
),

A\equiv\eta^{\mu\nu}A_{\mu\nu}=\eta^{ab}A_{ab}\quad;\quad\tilde{A}\equiv\eta^{\mu\nu}\tilde{A}_{\mu\nu}=\eta^{ab}\tilde{A}_{ab}\ ,

(14.7)

and
\xi_{i}
are constant parameters, whose mass dimensions are

[\xi_{0}]=[\xi_{3}]=1\quad;\quad[\xi_{1}]=[\xi_{2}]=[\xi_{4}]=0\ .

(14.8)

Notice that the general
requirements which constrain
S_{bd}
imply the presence of the Chern-Simons-like
\xi_{2}
-term, which can be traced back to the covariant fractonic
\theta
-term (
12.108
). In fact, this latter is

S_{\theta}=\int d^{4}x\theta(x^{3})\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu\lambda}\partial_{\rho}A_{\sigma}^{\lambda}\ ,

(14.9)

which, integrating by parts, reduces to

\int d^{3}X\epsilon^{abc}A_{a\lambda}\partial_{b}A_{c}^{\lambda}\ ,

(14.10)

which, on the gauge condition (
14.4
), coincides with the
\xi_{2}
-term in (
14.6
).
The total action is then

S_{tot}=S_{bulk}+S_{gf}+S_{J}+S_{bd}\ .

(14.11)

In

(14.24)

For

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\tilde{A}^{ij}(X)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=0\ .

Eq.(

[

\partial_{i}\left(\partial_{j}\tilde{A}^{ij}\right)=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\partial_{j}\tilde{A}^{ij}=\epsilon^{imn}\partial_{m}C_{n}\ ,

(14.27)

is a generic rank-2 tensor field. On the other hand,

is symmetric, hence

and we have

\tilde{A}^{ij}(X)\equiv\epsilon^{iab}\partial_{a}\tilde{a}_{b}^{\ j}(X)+\epsilon^{jab}\partial_{a}\tilde{a}_{b}^{\ i}(X)\ .

(14.29)

The tensor field
\tilde{a}_{ij}(X)
represents the
on the boundary, with

. Moreover, since

\tilde{a}_{ij}=\tilde{a}_{ji}\ ,

(14.30)

in order that the boundary

14.29

\tilde{A}(X)|_{\eqref{FBD-sol1}}=0\ ,

(14.31)

so that the condition (

Analogously, from the local Ward identity (

\tilde{J}^{ab}(x)=0

whose solution is

A^{ij}(X)\equiv\epsilon^{iab}\partial_{a}a_{b}^{\ j}(X)+\epsilon^{jab}\partial_{a}a_{b}^{\ i}(X)\ ,

where
a_{ij}(X)=a_{ji}(X)
are
on the boundary with

(14.34)

(14.36)

Importantly, the commutation relations (
