14.2
The induced 3D theory

Canonical variables

We look for the transformations of the boundary fields
a_{ij}(X),\ \tilde{a}_{ij}(X)
which preserve the solutions (
14.29
) and (
14.33
). The most general ones are

\displaystyle\delta a_{mn}=

\displaystyle\eta_{mn}\phi+\partial_{m}\xi_{n}+\partial_{n}\xi_{m}+\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\lambda\quad;\quad\delta\tilde{a}_{mn}=0

(14.38)

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}\tilde{a}_{mn}=

\displaystyle\eta_{mn}\tilde{\phi}+\partial_{m}\tilde{\xi}_{n}+\partial_{n}\tilde{\xi}_{m}+\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\tilde{\lambda}\quad;\quad\tilde{\delta}a_{mn}=0\ ,

(14.39)

where
\lambda(X),\ \tilde{\lambda}(X),\ \phi(X),\ \tilde{\phi}(X),\ \xi(X)
and
\tilde{\xi}(X)
are generic local parameters.
The solutions
A_{ij}(X)
(
14.29
) and
\tilde{A}_{ij}(X)
(
14.33
) remain unchanged,
i.e.

\delta A_{ij}=\tilde{\delta}\tilde{A}_{ij}=0
, if
\xi_{m}=\partial_{m}\xi^{\prime},\ \tilde{\xi}_{m}=\partial_{m}\tilde{\xi}^{\prime}
, so that (
14.38
) and (
14.39
) reduce to

\displaystyle\delta a_{mn}=

\displaystyle\eta_{mn}\phi+\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\lambda\quad;\quad\delta\tilde{a}_{mn}=0

(14.40)

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}\tilde{a}_{mn}=

\displaystyle\eta_{mn}\tilde{\phi}+\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\tilde{\lambda}\quad;\quad\tilde{\delta}a_{mn}=0\ .

(14.41)

We decompose the boundary fields
a_{ij}(X)
and
\tilde{a}_{ij}(X)
in terms of their trace and traceless contributions,
i.e.

\displaystyle a_{ij}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{ij}+\frac{1}{3}\eta_{ij}a

(14.42)

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}+\frac{1}{3}\eta_{ij}\tilde{a}\ ,

(14.43)

where
a\equiv\eta^{ij}a_{ij},\ \tilde{a}\equiv\eta^{ij}\tilde{a}_{ij}
, and
\alpha_{ij}(X),\ \tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
are symmetric traceless fields

\eta^{ij}\alpha_{ij}=\eta^{ij}\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}=0\ ,

(14.44)

which transform as

\displaystyle\delta\alpha_{mn}=

\displaystyle\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\lambda-\frac{1}{3}\eta_{mn}\partial^{2}\lambda\quad;\quad\delta\tilde{\alpha}_{mn}=0

(14.45)

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}\tilde{\alpha}_{mn}=

\displaystyle\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\tilde{\lambda}-\frac{1}{3}\eta_{mn}\partial^{2}\tilde{\lambda}\quad;\quad\tilde{\delta}\alpha_{mn}=0\ .

(14.46)

The solutions (
14.29
) and (
14.33
) depend only on the traceless components

\begin{split}\tilde{A}^{ij}(X)&\equiv\epsilon^{iab}\partial_{a}\tilde{\alpha}_{b}^{\ j}(X)+\epsilon^{jab}\partial_{a}\tilde{\alpha}_{b}^{\ i}(X)\\
A^{ij}(X)&\equiv\epsilon^{iab}\partial_{a}\alpha_{b}^{\ j}(X)+\epsilon^{jab}\partial_{a}\alpha_{b}^{\ i}(X)\ ,\end{split}

(14.47)

and the trace contributions disappear. The
of the boundary theory are then described by the rank-2 traceless tensor fields
\alpha_{ij}(X)
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
. This is consistent with the fact that, as we showed, on the boundary the bulk fields
A_{ij}(x)
and
\tilde{A}_{ij}(x)
have only five components, exactly as the 3D boundary fields
\alpha_{ij}(X)
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
, which are symmetric and traceless. The solutions (
14.47
) highly simplify the definitions of
\Delta A(X)
(
14.37
) and
\Delta\tilde{A}(X)
(
14.36
) :

\displaystyle\Delta A|_{\eqref{FBD-sol2}}

\displaystyle=4(g_{1}-g_{2})\epsilon^{0\textsc{mn}}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial^{\textsc{a}}\alpha_{\textsc{na}}

(14.48)

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{A}|_{\eqref{FBD-sol1}}

\displaystyle=4(g_{1}-g_{2})\epsilon^{0\textsc{mn}}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial^{\textsc{a}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{na}}\ ,

(14.49)

where we observe that only spatial derivatives appear. Considering the following combinations of the commutators (
14.34
) and (
14.35
) and their traces

\displaystyle\left[\Delta\tilde{A}\ ,\ \!A_{\textsc{df}}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\eta^{\textsc{mn}}A_{\textsc{mn}}^{\prime}\right]=

\displaystyle\!\quad\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{f}}+\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{f}}-\eta^{\textsc{mn}}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\right)\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})

(14.50)

\displaystyle\left[\Delta A\ ,\ \!\tilde{A}_{\textsc{df}}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\eta^{\textsc{mn}}\tilde{A}_{\textsc{mn}}^{\prime}\right]=

\displaystyle\!-\!\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{f}}+\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{f}}-\eta^{\textsc{mn}}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\right)\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\!\ ,

(14.51)

and using the solutions (
14.47
), we can identify the following two canonical commutation relations at the boundary (the details can be found in Appendix
11.C.1.b)
)

\displaystyle\left[q_{\textsc{ab}}\ ,\ p^{\prime\textsc{cd}}\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{b}}+\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{b}}-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})

(14.52)

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{q}_{\textsc{ab}}\ ,\ \tilde{p}^{\prime\textsc{cd}}\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{b}}+\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{b}}-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ ,

(14.53)

where

\displaystyle q_{\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle\equiv\alpha_{\textsc{ab}}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\alpha^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}

(14.54)

\displaystyle p^{\textsc{cd}}

\displaystyle\equiv 2g_{12}\left(\tilde{f}^{\textsc{cd}0}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\tilde{f}^{\ a0}_{a}\right)

(14.55)

\displaystyle\tilde{q}_{\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{ab}}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}

(14.56)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{\textsc{cd}}

\displaystyle\equiv-2g_{12}\left(f^{\textsc{cd}0}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{cd}}f^{\ a0}_{a}\right)\ ,

(14.57)

and

g_{12}\equiv 2(g_{1}-g_{2})\ .

(14.58)

In analogy to
F_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)
(
12.19
),
f_{abc}(X)
and
\tilde{f}_{abc}(X)
are defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{abc}

\displaystyle\equiv\partial_{a}\tilde{\alpha}_{bc}+\partial_{b}\tilde{\alpha}_{ac}-2\partial_{c}\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}

(14.59)

\displaystyle f_{abc}

\displaystyle\equiv\partial_{a}\alpha_{bc}+\partial_{b}\alpha_{ac}-2\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}\ .

(14.60)

It is interesting to notice that a canonical commutator similar to those we found in (
14.52
) and (
14.53
), appears in
[
213
]
, in the context of the traceless fracton models
[
160
,
161
]
.
The aim of
[
213
]
is to build a non-abelian model for fractons in 2+1 dimensions. To do so the abelian traceless theory needs to be defined first. As for any fracton theory
[
54
,
55
,
160
,
161
]
, the “electric field”
E_{\textsc{ij}}(x)
is the conjugate momentum of
A_{\textsc{ij}}(x)
, from which the commutator holds

[E^{\textsc{ij}},A_{\textsc{mn}}]=i(\delta^{\textsc{i}}_{\textsc{m}}\delta^{\textsc{j}}_{\textsc{n}}+\delta^{\textsc{j}}_{\textsc{m}}\delta^{\textsc{i}}_{\textsc{n}})\ .

(14.61)

After that, the scalar Gauss constraints is imposed, together with a tracelessness condition :

\partial_{\textsc{i}}\partial_{\textsc{j}}E^{\textsc{ij}}=\rho\quad;\quad E^{\ \textsc{i}}_{\textsc{i}}=0\ ,

(14.62)

which imply three conservation equations :

\int\rho=const\quad;\quad\int\vec{x}\rho=const\quad;\quad\int x^{2}\rho=const\ ,

(14.63)

of charge, dipole and a component of the quadrupole, respectively. The main characteristic of fracton theories,
i.e.
the limited mobility, is here translated into the fact that single charges cannot move, while dipole bound states can only move along their transverse direction. The constraints (
14.62
) imply that the tensor field
A_{\textsc{ij}}(x)
transforms exactly as (
14.40
) and (
14.41
), which is a remarkable check of our reasoning. However, while in our case it is natural to identify the
of the theory with the traceless fields
\alpha_{ij}(X)
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
, in
[
213
]
the tracelessness condition is imposed as a kind of gauge fixing, while for us it comes from the solutions (
14.47
). As a consequence, the definition (
14.61
) of the canonical commutator is no longer valid (since
A^{\ \textsc{i}}_{\textsc{i}}=E^{\ \textsc{i}}_{\textsc{i}}=0
would not commute), and the commutator for the traceless theory of fractons is defined as Dirac brackets
[
228
]
, which turns out to be identical to ours (
14.52
) and (
14.53
).

The most general 3D action

The action of the 3D boundary theory is constructed as the most general local integrated functional of the traceless rank-2 symmetric tensor fields
\alpha_{ij}(X)
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
compatible with

•

power-counting
[\alpha]=0,\ [\tilde{\alpha}]=1
;

•

symmetry
\delta S=\tilde{\delta}S=0
, where
\delta
and
\tilde{\delta}
are defined in (
14.45
) and (
14.46
) ;

•

canonical variables identified in (
14.52
) and (
14.53
) :
\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{kin}}{\partial\dot{q}}=p
.

In Appendix
11.C.2
we show that the most general 3D action satisfying these three requests is

S_{3D}=\int d^{3}X\left(-\frac{2}{3}g_{12}\,\varphi_{abc}\tilde{\varphi}^{abc}+\omega_{5}\,\epsilon^{abc}\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\tilde{\varphi}_{dbc}\right)\ ,

(14.64)

where we defined

\displaystyle\varphi_{abc}=\varphi_{bac}

\displaystyle\equiv f_{abc}+\frac{1}{4}\left(-2\eta_{ab}f^{d}_{\ dc}+\eta_{bc}f^{d}_{\ da}+\eta_{ac}f^{d}_{\ db}\right)

(14.65)

\displaystyle=-2\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}+\partial_{a}\alpha_{bc}+\partial_{b}\alpha_{ac}-\eta_{ab}\partial^{d}\alpha_{dc}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{bc}\partial^{d}\alpha_{da}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{ac}\partial^{d}\alpha_{db}\ ,

and, analogously,
\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}(X)
in terms of
\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}(X)
, with the following properties of ciclicity and “tracelessness”

\displaystyle\varphi_{abc}+\varphi_{cab}+\varphi_{bca}=0=\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}+\tilde{\varphi}_{cab}+\tilde{\varphi}_{bca}

(14.66)

\displaystyle\eta^{ab}\varphi_{abc}=\eta^{bc}\varphi_{abc}=\eta^{ab}\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}=\eta^{bc}\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}=0\ .

(14.67)

The
\omega_{5}
term in (
14.64
) looks like a Chern-Simons term, and the similarity is even more evident if we explicit the
\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}(X)
dependence, since
\epsilon^{abc}\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\tilde{\varphi}_{dbc}\propto\,\epsilon^{abc}\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\partial_{b}\tilde{\alpha}_{cd}
.
Intriguingly, this Chern-Simons-like term resembles the massless limit of 3D self-dual massive gravity
[
229
,
230
]
. This theory contains a 3D Fierz-Pauli mass term that breaks the gauge invariance
[
214
,
215
]
. However, it has been shown
[
231
]
that it is dual to linearized topologically massive gravity
[
232
]
, which is gauge invariant and contains the Chern-Simons-like term together with the linearized 3D Einstein-Hilbert action. These two equivalent theories were originally proposed as a viable way to describe a single propagating massive graviton in 3D in contrast with the standard Einstein-Hilbert theory, which is topological in 3D and does not support any propagating spin-2 particle. In our case, the linearized Einstein-Hilbert term is replaced by the tensorial Maxwell-like term such that our boundary action still supports a propagating “graviton”. Notice that a non-covariant version of our Chern-Simons-like term has been also considered in
[
185
,
233
]
in the context of fractional quantum Hall effect and chiral fractons. However, these non-covariant field theories, that can be seen as dual one to each other, do not take into account any tensorial Maxwell-like terms.
Notice also that if
\omega_{5}=0
it is possible to decouple the fields. In fact, by defining

\alpha_{ab}^{\pm}\equiv\sqrt{M}\,\alpha_{ab}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{M}}\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\varphi^{\pm}_{abc}=\sqrt{M}\,\varphi_{abc}\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{M}}\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}\ ,

(14.68)

where
M
is a parameter with mass dimension
[M]=1
and
[\alpha^{\pm}]=\frac{1}{2}
, the 3D action (
14.64
) becomes

\begin{split}S_{3D}=\int d^{3}X&\left[\frac{g_{12}}{6}\left(\varphi^{-}_{abc}\varphi^{-\,abc}-\varphi^{+}_{abc}\varphi^{+\,abc}\right)+\right.\\
&\left.\;+\frac{M\omega_{5}}{4}\left(\alpha^{+\,d}_{\,a}\epsilon^{abc}\varphi^{+}_{dbc}-2\alpha^{+\,d}_{\,a}\epsilon^{abc}\varphi^{-}_{dbc}+\alpha^{-\,d}_{\,a}\epsilon^{abc}\varphi^{-}_{dbc}\right)\right]\ ,\end{split}

(14.69)

which for
\omega_{5}=0
decouples:

S_{3D}[\alpha^{+},\alpha^{-},\omega_{5}=0]=S^{+}_{3D}[\alpha^{+}]+S^{-}_{3D}[\alpha^{-}]\

(14.70)

with

S^{\pm}_{3D}\equiv\mp\frac{g_{12}}{6}\int d^{3}X\,\varphi^{\pm}_{abc}\varphi^{\pm\,abc}\ .

(14.71)

As we shall show in Section
14.5
, this second case keeps the
\mathcal{T}
-invariance of the boundary in agreement with the
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry of the bulk action (
14.1
).

14.4
Physical interpretation of the 3D theory

To understand the physical content of the 3D theory described by the action
S_{3D}
(
14.64
), we study its
. The first
(
14.72
) for
m=n=0
gives

0=\partial_{\textsc{a}}\tilde{\varphi}^{00\textsc{a}}=-2\partial_{\textsc{a}}\partial^{\textsc{a}}\tilde{\alpha}^{00}+2\partial_{\textsc{a}}\partial^{0}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{a}0}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}\partial_{b}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{a}b}\ .

(14.99)

Taking the
\partial_{\textsc{n}}
-derivative of (
14.72
) for
m=0,\ n=\textsc{n}
, we get

0=\partial_{\textsc{n}}\partial_{a}\tilde{\varphi}^{0\textsc{n}a}=-\frac{1}{4g_{12}}\partial_{\textsc{a}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}p^{\textsc{a}\textsc{n}}\ ,

(14.100)

where we used (
14.99
), the cyclicity property (
14.66
) and the definition of conjugate momentum in terms of
\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}(X)
(
14.65
)

p^{\textsc{mn}}=2g_{12}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{mn}0}\ .

(14.101)

We see that (
14.100
) is a Gauss-like equation, analogous to the one related to the traceless scalar charge model of fractons in vacuum
[
160
,
161
]
, which is at the base of the limited mobility property. We therefore realize that the induced 3D theory shows fractonic properties. To analyze the second
(
14.73
), we first compute the conjugate momentum of
\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}(X)
:

\tilde{p}^{\textsc{mn}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{3D}}{\partial\dot{\tilde{\alpha}}_{\textsc{mn}}}=2g_{12}\varphi^{\textsc{mn}0}-\frac{3}{2}\omega_{5}\left(\epsilon^{0\textsc{am}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{n}}_{\ \textsc{a}}+\epsilon^{0\textsc{an}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{a}}\right)\ .

(14.102)

The
for
\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}(X)
(
14.73
) at
m=n=0
is

\partial_{\textsc{a}}\varphi^{00\textsc{a}}=\frac{3\omega_{5}}{g_{12}}\epsilon^{0\textsc{ab}}\partial_{\textsc{a}}\tilde{\alpha}^{0}_{\ \textsc{b}}\ ,

(14.103)

and, as in the previous case, taking the
\partial_{\textsc{n}}
-derivative of (
14.73
) at
m=0,\ n=\textsc{n}
we have

\begin{split}0=&-2g_{12}\partial_{a}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\varphi^{0\textsc{n}a}+3\omega_{5}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\left(\epsilon^{0\textsc{ab}}\partial_{\textsc{a}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{n}}_{\ \textsc{b}}+\epsilon^{\textsc{n}ab}\partial_{a}\tilde{\alpha}^{0}_{\ b}\right)\\
=&\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\tilde{p}^{\textsc{mn}}-\frac{3}{2}\omega_{5}\epsilon^{0\textsc{am}}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{n}}_{\ \textsc{a}}\ ,\end{split}

(14.104)

where we used the cyclic property of
\varphi_{abc}(X)
(
14.66
) and (
14.103
). Here again we find a Gauss-like equation for the traceless scalar charge theory of fractons
[
160
,
161
]
, but with a matter contribution at the right hand side

\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\tilde{p}^{\textsc{mn}}=\tilde{\rho}_{5}=\omega_{5}\tilde{\rho}\ ,

(14.105)

where

\tilde{\rho}\equiv{3}\epsilon^{0\textsc{am}}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{n}}_{\ \textsc{a}}

(14.106)

plays the role of charge. This gives an interesting interpretation of the Chern-Simons-like term in the induced 3D action (
14.64
) as “internal” matter. Notice that this term coincides with the charge identified by Pretko in
[
175
]
, where a non-covariant Chern-Simons-like term is studied. In that case the Chern-Simons term comes from a non-covariant fractonic
\theta
-term in the bulk, and it is written in terms of a spatial traceless tensor. The charge
\tilde{\rho}(X)
(
14.106
) comes from a constraint generated by a Lagrange multiplier that is inherited by the Chern-Simons action from the definition of the
\theta
-term. We observe that this
\tilde{\rho}(X)
charge implies, by definition, a dipole conservation. The 3D theory (
14.64
) depends on two fields
\alpha_{ij}(X)
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
, hence the conjugate momenta are two as well (
14.101
) and (
14.102
), which in fracton models play the role of “electric” fields :

\displaystyle E^{\textsc{mn}}

\displaystyle\equiv p^{\textsc{mn}}=2g_{12}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{mn}0}

(14.107)

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{\textsc{mn}}

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{p}^{\textsc{mn}}=2g_{12}\varphi^{\textsc{mn}0}-\frac{3}{2}\omega_{5}\left(\epsilon^{0\textsc{am}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{n}}_{\ \textsc{a}}+\epsilon^{0\textsc{an}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{a}}\right)\ ,

(14.108)

which satisfy the Gauss equations (
14.100
) and (
14.105
), which we write

\displaystyle\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}E^{\textsc{mn}}=

\displaystyle 0

(14.109)

\displaystyle\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\tilde{E}^{\textsc{mn}}=

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{5}\ .

(14.110)

Concerning the corresponding “magnetic” fields for this theory, inspired by the ordinary 4D electromagnetism, where
B_{i}\propto\epsilon_{0ijk}F^{jk}
, it is natural to define

\displaystyle B^{\textsc{m}}\equiv g\epsilon_{0\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{mab}}\quad;\quad B_{\textsc{m}}\equiv-g\epsilon^{0\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\varphi}_{\textsc{mab}}

(14.111)

\displaystyle\tilde{B}^{\textsc{m}}\equiv\tilde{g}\epsilon_{0\textsc{ab}}\varphi^{\textsc{mab}}\quad;\quad\tilde{B}_{\textsc{m}}\equiv-\tilde{g}\epsilon^{0\textsc{ab}}\varphi_{\textsc{mab}}\ .

(14.112)

Notice that, while in 4D fracton theories both electric and magnetic fields are rank-2 tensors
[
4
]
, in our 3D case, the electric field is still a tensor, while the magnetic field is a vector. Moreover, the definitions (
14.111
) and (
14.112
) are consistent with the fact that in ordinary 3D electromagnetism the electric field
\vec{E}(x)
is a vector, while the magnetic field is a pseudo-scalar
B=\epsilon^{0ij}F_{ij}

[
234
]
. As we shall see, our guess (
14.111
) and (
14.112
) will be confirmed by a consistent physical interpretation of a fractonic “magnetic-like” behaviour. In terms of
\alpha_{ab}(X),\ \tilde{\alpha}_{ab}(X)
the magnetic fields read

B^{\textsc{m}}=-3g\epsilon_{0\textsc{ab}}\left(\partial^{\textsc{b}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{ma}}+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{ma}}\partial_{d}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{b}d}\right)\quad;\quad\tilde{B}^{\textsc{m}}=-3\tilde{g}\epsilon_{0\textsc{ab}}\left(\partial^{\textsc{b}}\alpha^{\textsc{ma}}+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{ma}}\partial_{d}\alpha^{\textsc{b}d}\right)\ ,

(14.113)

which imply

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{abc}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3g}\left(\epsilon^{0\textsc{ac}}B^{\textsc{b}}+\epsilon^{0\textsc{bc}}B^{\textsc{a}}\right)

(14.114)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\textsc{abc}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3\tilde{g}}\left(\epsilon^{0\textsc{ac}}\tilde{B}^{\textsc{b}}+\epsilon^{0\textsc{bc}}\tilde{B}^{\textsc{a}}\right)\ \ .

(14.115)

Due to tracelessness property (
14.67
), we get

\varphi_{\textsc{a}}^{\ \textsc{ab}}=\varphi^{00\textsc{b}}=-\frac{2}{3\tilde{g}}\epsilon^{0\textsc{ab}}\tilde{B}_{\textsc{a}}\quad;\quad\tilde{\varphi}_{\textsc{a}}^{\ \textsc{ab}}=\tilde{\varphi}^{00\textsc{b}}=-\frac{2}{3g}\epsilon^{0\textsc{ab}}B_{\textsc{a}}\ .

(14.116)

Notice that

\displaystyle\partial_{\textsc{m}}B^{\textsc{m}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}g\,\epsilon_{0\textsc{ab}}\partial^{\textsc{a}}\left(\partial_{0}\tilde{\alpha}^{0\textsc{b}}-\partial_{\textsc{c}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{bc}}\right)\neq 0

(14.117)

\displaystyle\partial_{\textsc{m}}\tilde{B}^{\textsc{m}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{2}\tilde{g}\,\epsilon_{0\textsc{ab}}\partial^{\textsc{a}}\left(\partial_{0}\alpha^{0\textsc{b}}-\partial_{\textsc{c}}\alpha^{\textsc{bc}}\right)\neq 0\ ,

(14.118)

which would suggest the presence, in the 3D theory (
14.64
), of a fractonic “magnetic”-like vortex. Consistently with the fact of having non-vanishing divergences of the magnetic vector fields, we find also a broken Bianchi identity, which also suggests the presence of a kind of magnetic fracton vortex. This would imply that a part of our fracton fields give rise to 2D fracton vortex defects that represent a lower-dimensional version of the 3D fracton magnetic monopole proposed in
[
175
]
. In fact, we have

\epsilon^{mbc}\partial_{m}\varphi_{abc}=-\frac{3}{2}\epsilon_{mac}\partial^{m}\partial_{d}\alpha^{cd}\neq 0\quad;\quad\epsilon^{mbc}\partial_{m}\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}=-\frac{3}{2}\epsilon_{mac}\partial^{m}\partial_{d}\tilde{\alpha}^{cd}\neq 0\ ,

(14.119)

which for
a=0
give the non-vanishing divergences (
14.117
) and (
14.118
). Setting instead
a=\textsc{a}
we find

\displaystyle\frac{1}{g}\partial_{0}B_{\textsc{a}}+\frac{1}{2g_{12}}\epsilon^{0\textsc{mb}}\partial_{\textsc{m}}E_{\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\epsilon_{m\textsc{a}b}\partial^{m}\partial_{d}\tilde{\alpha}^{bd}+\epsilon^{\textsc{m0b}}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\tilde{\varphi}_{\textsc{0ab}}\neq 0

(14.120)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\tilde{g}}\partial_{0}\tilde{B}_{\textsc{a}}+\frac{1}{2g_{12}}\epsilon^{0\textsc{mb}}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\tilde{E}_{\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle=\frac{9}{4}\frac{\omega_{5}}{g_{12}}\partial^{\textsc{m}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{ma}}+\frac{3}{2}\epsilon_{m\textsc{a}b}\partial^{m}\partial_{d}\alpha^{bd}+\epsilon^{\textsc{m0b}}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\varphi_{\textsc{0ab}}\neq 0

(14.121)

14.5
Discrete symmetries : parity and time reversal

As extensively shown in the recent literature concerning non-perturbative aspects of quantum field theories, discrete symmetries play a central role in the identification of global anomalies and anomaly inflow, which are related to topological obstructions and impose strong constraints on the renormalization group flows, massive boundary states, quantum dualities and the vacua of quantum field theories
[
235
,
236
,
237
,
238
,
239
,
240
,
241
]
. Moreover, the anomaly inflow has been also extended to certain non-covariant fracton models
[
182
]
. Thus, here we analyze some discrete symmetries, such as
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{P}
in the context of the induced theory we just derived. In fact, we can further constrain the induced 3D action by requiring a matching between the discrete symmetries in the bulk and on the boundary. In particular, under
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{T}
the bulk fields transform as follows

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{T}\Bigl\{A_{00}\,,\,A_{0\textsc{a}}\,,\,A_{\textsc{ab}}\Bigr\}&=\Bigl\{A_{00}\,,\,-A_{0\textsc{a}}\,,\,A_{\textsc{ab}}\Bigr\}\\
\mathcal{T}\Bigl\{\tilde{A}_{00}\,,\,\tilde{A}_{0\textsc{a}}\,,\,\tilde{A}_{\textsc{ab}}\Bigr\}&=\Bigl\{\tilde{A}_{00}\,,\,-\tilde{A}_{0\textsc{a}}\,,\,\tilde{A}_{\textsc{ab}}\Bigr\}\end{split}

(14.134)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{P}\Bigl\{A_{00}\,,\,A_{0\textsc{a}}\,,\,A_{\textsc{ab}}\Bigr\}&=\Bigl\{A_{00}\,,\,-A_{0\textsc{a}}\,,\,A_{\textsc{ab}}\Bigr\}\\
\mathcal{P}\Bigl\{\tilde{A}_{00}\,,\,\tilde{A}_{0\textsc{a}}\,,\,\tilde{A}_{\textsc{ab}}\Bigr\}&=\Bigl\{\!-\tilde{A}_{00}\,,\tilde{A}_{0\textsc{a}}\,,-\tilde{A}_{\textsc{ab}}\Bigr\}\end{split}

(14.135)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}A=A\quad;\quad\mathcal{T}\tilde{A}=\tilde{A}\quad

\displaystyle\ \ ;\ \ \quad\mathcal{P}A=A\quad;\quad\mathcal{P}\tilde{A}=-\tilde{A}\ .

(14.136)

The bulk action (
14.1
) is invariant under
\mathcal{T}
. Instead, due to the presence of the boundary
x^{3}=0
, the action is no longer
\mathcal{P}
-invariant. We now consider the boundary term (
14.6
), and distinguish between space and time indices

\displaystyle S_{bd}=\int d^{4}x\delta(x^{3})

\displaystyle\left[\xi_{0}\left(A_{00}A^{00}+2A_{0\textsc{a}}A^{0\textsc{a}}+A_{\textsc{ab}}A^{\textsc{ab}}\right)+\xi_{1}\left(\tilde{A}_{00}A^{00}+2\tilde{A}_{0\textsc{a}}A^{0\textsc{a}}+\tilde{A}_{\textsc{ab}}A^{\textsc{ab}}\right)\right.+

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\left.\xi_{2}\epsilon^{0\textsc{ab}}\left(A_{0i}\partial_{\textsc{a}}A_{\textsc{b}}^{i}-A_{\textsc{a}i}\partial_{0}A_{\textsc{b}}^{i}+A_{\textsc{a}i}\partial_{\textsc{b}}A_{0}^{i}\right)+\xi_{3}A^{2}+\xi_{4}\tilde{A}A\right]\ .

(14.137)

We then observe that

•

\mathcal{P}S_{bd}=S_{bd}
if
\xi_{1}=\xi_{4}=0
;

•

\mathcal{T}S_{bd}=S_{bd}
if
\xi_{2}=0
;

•

\mathcal{T}\mathcal{P}S_{bd}=S_{bd}
if
\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}=\xi_{4}=0
.

Under these considerations, we can update Table

From Table

14.6
Summary and discussion

The questions that were raised at the beginning of this analysis in Section
14
, have thus found their answers. Indeed an algebraic structure on the boundary does exist, as a consequence of the breaking of the Ward identities, and it can be interpreted as a generalization of the standard
U(1)

algebra, characterized by a double derivative, as it appears for instance also in
[
184
]
, further stressing the suspected relations between fractons and higher order topological insulators. From the two broken Ward identities,
the boundary
of the induced theory are identified as two symmetric traceless rank-2 tensors
\alpha_{ij}(X)
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
. It is worth to remark that
on the boundary some
disappear, since the boundary tensor fields turn out to be traceless. This might be due to the presence of a hidden symmetry, a guess that should be further investigated.
The procedure to recover the induced theory leads to
the action
S_{3D}
(
14.64
), which is composed of a term similar to a higher-rank Maxwell contribution, written in terms of traceless rank-3 field strengths, which
mixes both fields
\alpha_{ij}(X)
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
, with a coefficient depending on the bulk constants
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
, and a Chern-Simons-like term for
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
with a free coefficient.
Concerning the physical interpretation of our 3D induced theory
S_{3D}
(
14.64
), this can be identified with the “traceless scalar charge” model of fractons
[
160
,
161
,
213
]
. In fact the transformations of the boundary fields, the canonical commutators, the traceless conjugate momenta,
i.e.
the “electric fields”, coincide with what appears in the literature.
This claim is confirmed also by the
of the 3D induced theory, from which two Gauss-like laws are derived, which imply the defining property of the fracton quasiparticles,
i.e.
their limited mobility. Thus, one of the main results of this Section is that a non-standard covariant 3D traceless fracton theory turns out to be holographically induced from a 4D ordinary traceful covariant fracton theory. This claim gets even stronger confirmation from other components of the
, which can be identified with the Ampère-like equations
of fractons
[
148
]
, further stressing the relation of fracton models with Maxwell theory. Concerning this analogy, we remark a close resemblance of our 3D action
S_{3D}
(
14.64
) with Maxwell-Chern-Simons theory
[
70
]
, of which it appears to be a kind of spin-two generalization. A similar observation can also be found in
[
231
]
in the context of self-dual massive gravity, where an identical covariant Chern-Simons term appears, and whose relation with our 3D model is worth to be further investigated. However, differently from the standard Maxwell-Chern-Simons theory, here all the coefficients are dimensionless, hence no topological mass can be identified.
Therefore to better analyze this analogy, the study of the propagators would be helpful.
Notice also that the Chern-Simons coefficient is free, thus it can be switched off. The choice of keeping the Chern-Simons-like term or not is relevant for the physical interpretation of the model: by switching it off, the 3D action
S_{3D}
(
14.64
) can be decoupled into two Maxwell-like terms, and the boundary theory is compatible with
\mathcal{T}
-symmetry, which characterizes the phenomenology involved. For instance, we have seen in Chapters
4
and
5
that the physics on the boundary of the topological BF models
[
34
,
242
,
77
,
142
]
is identified with the effective description of the edge states of
, where
\mathcal{T}
is preserved both on the bulk and on the boundary. On the other hand, keeping the Chern-Simons-like term,
i.e.
relaxing the
\mathcal{T}
constraint, the
get a matter contribution. In particular the Chern-Simons-like term plays the role of fractonic charge
\tilde{\rho}(X)
(
14.106
) and current
\tilde{J}_{\textsc{ij}}(X)
(
14.128
) in two of the Maxwell-like equations, in accordance with
[
175
]
.
Some final physical remarks are in order. Differently from the standard electromagnetic theory and the 4D traceful fracton model, here the magnetic-like vectors
B_{\textsc{a}}(X)
and
\tilde{B}_{\textsc{a}}(X)
do not have zero divergence, nor a Bianchi identity exists for the traceless rank-3 field strengths
\varphi_{abc}(X),\ \tilde{\varphi}_{abc}(X)
, which suggests the presence of fractonic 3D vortices. Additionally, 3D fracton models are known to be related to the elasticity theory of topological defects through a duality
[
148
]
. For instance the traceful Ampère-like equation can be seen as describing the motion of these defects. Under this respect, it would be interesting to understand if and how our traceless boundary theory can be related to topological defects. Finally, there seems to be an interesting possible interpretation of the fractonic Chern-Simons-like term as associated to torsion contributions, as in
[
233
]
, which also would be worth to further analyze.

Linearized Gravity

In this Chapter, we consider the 4D theory of
with a planar boundary, motivated by the guessed relation between
algebras
[
26
,
27
]
and

[
243
,
244
,
227
]
, providing a precise interpretation of the physical quantity characterizing
algebras – its central charge. According to the “general rule”, the central charge should be related to the
coupling constant, normally set to one by a redefinition of the field, which in
is a symmetric rank-2 tensor field. Therefore the positivity of the central charge could also give us a way to determine the sign of the
“coupling” constant, which in this case cannot be inferred from an energy constraint.
Apart from the
algebra of conserved current, the introduction of a boundary yields more. The presence of a boundary in the 3D Chern-Simons theory induces a 2D theory, the Tomonaga-Luttinger theory of a chiral scalar field
[
245
,
246
,
115
,
87
]
, which we may refer to as a “holographic” reduction of the 3D bulk theory. Similar dimensional reductions – lower dimensional theories induced by the presence of a boundary in higher-dimensional ones – can be performed in other theories, both topological and non-topological. For instance we have seen in the previous Chapter the case of the fractonic theory, whose symmetry is a particular case of the diffeomorphisms, from which we have observed that fractons always come together with
. Thus the theory with boundary studied in Section
14
comprises both fractonic contribution and
, but without allowing for the limit of
g_{1}=0

i.e.
pure
, which is not contained in that analysis. The reason is that, as explained,
is a theory defined by a more general symmetry (the diff symmetry), which we know from Chapter
2
plays an important role in the search for the induced boundary theory. Indeed it is the breaking of the symmetry due to the presence of the boundary that leads to the algebra and the degrees of freedom on the lower dimensional theory. If the symmetry changes, the boundary theory may change as well. Therefore here we will apply the same mechanism of the previous Chapters to the case of pure
to find the holographically induced 3D theory of 4D
with a planar boundary. In particular in Section
15
we will first briefly review the theory of
and its bulk physical content, and in Section
16
we will proceed with the introduction of a planar boundary.

15
The theory of gravitational waves

We will therefore consider the theory of a symmetric tensor field
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
which transforms under diffeomorphisms

\delta_{diff}h_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}\ .

(15.1)

As we mentioned previously, this theory contains the theory of fractons of Chapter
7
through a reduction of the diff transformation (
15.1
) into the longitudinal one (
12.1
), but the physical content is quite different, hence the difference in the notation : the symmetric rank-2 tensor field
A_{\mu\nu}(x)
of the previous Chapter was of fractonic nature, here we will refer to
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
as the rank-2 symmetric tensor field of
. We are stepping from the microscopic world of condensed matter and immobile quasiparticles, to the linearized theory of gravity describing gravitational waves. Indeed this theory can be either derived from a pure
point of view as the theory invariant under the diff symmetry (
15.1
), as a perturbation theory in General Relativity, where the gauge field
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
is a perturbation around the flat Minkowskian metric. In the first case,
i.e.

perspective, the transformation (
15.1
) immediately gives the invariant
action we already mentioned in the previous Chapters

S_{LG}=\int d^{4}x\left(\partial_{\mu}h\partial^{\mu}h-\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}h^{\mu\nu}-2\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\nu}h^{\mu\nu}+2\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\mu}h^{\nu\rho}\right)\ ,

(15.2)

which describes a theory of a massless symmetric rank-2 tensor field
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
in a flat spacetime. It is relevant to mention here an important generalization in which this gauge field is given a mass by breaking gauge invariance. This is known as the Fierz-Pauli action
[
211
,
215
,
214
,
219
]

S_{FP}=\int d^{4}x\left[m^{2}(h^{2}-h_{\mu\nu}h^{\mu\nu})+\partial_{\mu}h\partial^{\mu}h-\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\rho}h^{\mu\nu}-2\partial_{\mu}h\partial_{\nu}h^{\mu\nu}+2\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\mu}h^{\nu\rho}\right]\ ,

(15.3)

which describes a single massive spin 2 degree of freedom of mass
m
. This model is of particular interest nowadays since it seems that observations of gravitational waves, which we will show to be the physics related to the
action (
15.2
), seem to indicate that gravitons are not massless indeed. Instead, starting from the the Einstein-Hilbert action of General Relativity

S_{EH}=\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,R\ ,

(15.4)

one can consider a weak field limit, in which the metric of the spacetime
g_{\mu\nu}(x)
is a perturbation around flat spacetime

g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+h_{\mu\nu}\ .

(15.5)

Under this assumption translational invariance in curved spacetime, which is encoded in the Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}_{\xi}

[
105
]
, generates the diff transformation (
15.1
) through linearization :

\mathcal{L}_{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}\quad\xrightarrow{\eqref{g->h}}\quad\delta_{diff}h_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}\ .

(15.6)

At the same time the Einstein’s

\frac{\delta S_{EH}}{\delta g^{\mu\nu}}=\mathcal{G}_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R=0\ ,

(15.7)

matches the
recoverd from the
action (
15.2
) in the weak-field limit (
15.5
), for which at first order in the perturbation the Ricci tensor and scalar are respectively

R_{\mu\nu}\sim\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\lambda}h_{\lambda\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\partial^{\lambda}h_{\lambda\mu}-\Box h_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h\right)\quad;\quad R\sim\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}-\Box h\ ,

(15.8)

thus recovering

\mathcal{G}_{\mu\nu}\sim\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\lambda}h_{\nu\lambda}+\partial_{\nu}\partial^{\lambda}h_{\mu\lambda}-\Box h_{\mu\nu}-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}h-\eta_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}h_{\alpha\beta}+\eta_{\mu\nu}\Box h=0\ .

(15.9)

In order to analyze the physical content of this equation the first step is to write the gauge field
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
in terms of the irreducible representation of rotations
[
105
]
, as we have also done in Section
14
in (
11.C.49
)-(
11.C.51
). Therefore we have

h_{00}=-2\phi\quad;\quad h_{0i}=w_{i}\quad;\quad h^{k}_{\,k}=-6\psi\quad;\quad h_{ij}=2(s_{ij}-\eta_{ij}\psi)\ ,

(15.10)

thus we have two scalars -
\phi(x),\psi(x)
-, a vector -
w_{i}(x)
-, and a traceless symmetric spatial tensor -
s_{ij}(x)
-. One then has to fix a gauge which gives four conditions on the model, due to the nature of the gauge parameter of the symmetry. The typical (non-
) way to proceed
[
105
]
is thus to impose the so called “transverse gauge”

\partial^{i}w_{i}=0\quad;\quad\partial^{i}s_{ij}=0\ .

(15.11)

Clearly the same results can be obtained also by means of other choices, however this is the best one to quickly recover the physical content. Indeed, from this choice and the decomposition (
15.10
), the
(
15.9
) can be analyzed in terms of its time and space components as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{00}

\displaystyle=2\nabla^{2}\psi=0\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad\psi=0

(15.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{0i}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{0}\partial_{i}\cancel{\psi}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{2}w_{i}=0\quad

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\quad w_{i}=0

(15.13)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{ij}

\displaystyle=\left(\eta_{ij}\nabla^{2}-\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\right)(\phi-\cancel{\psi})+

\displaystyle\hskip-20.00003pt2\eta_{ij}\partial_{0}^{2}\cancel{\psi}+\partial_{0}(\cancel{\partial_{i}w_{j}+\partial_{j}w_{i}})-\Box s_{ij}=0

(15.14)

where (
15.12
) and (
15.13
) give Poisson’s equations, whose smooth solutions are only given by vanishing fields
[
105
]
. By computing the trace of the last equation and the fact that
\psi=w_{i}=0
, we get another Poisson’s equation for
\phi(x)
, which thus must be zero as well. We therefore get

\Box s_{ij}=0\ ,

(15.15)

which is a wave equation for a traceless symmetric field. This is the core of gravitational waves. In terms of the gauge field
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
, the above conditions (Poisson’s solutions and transverse gauge (
15.11
)), are usually (misleadingly) translated into the so called "transverse-traceless" (
tt
) gauge. We say misleading because this does not only contain a gauge, but also solutions to the
and, additionally, the results could be recovered also in different gauges. Anyway, the gauge field in the “
tt
-gauge”
h^{\textsc{tt}}_{\mu\nu}(x)
satisfy the following relations

\displaystyle h^{\textsc{tt}}_{0\mu}

\displaystyle=0

(15.16)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mu\nu}h^{\textsc{tt}}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0\quad\mbox{(traceless)}

(15.17)

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}h^{\textsc{tt}}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0\quad\mbox{(transverse)}

(15.18)

\displaystyle\Box h^{\textsc{tt}}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0\ .

(15.19)

From (
15.19
) we can find plane waves solution

h^{\textsc{tt}}_{\mu\nu}=c_{\mu\nu}e^{ik_{\lambda}x^{\lambda}}\ ,

(15.20)

with
c_{\mu\nu}
being purely spatial and traceless (
i.e.\ c_{0\mu}=0=\eta^{\mu\nu}c_{\mu\nu}
), and
k_{\lambda}
is a lightlike vector, meaning that the gravitational waves move at the speed of light (unless some kind of mass term can be introduced in the theory, like for instance through the Fierz-Pauli term (
15.3
)). By choosing an appropriate reference frame, for example by fixing an axis along the direction of propagation
k^{\mu}=(\omega,0,0,\omega)
, and considering the transversality equation (
15.18
), one finally get that
h^{\textsc{tt}}_{\mu\nu}(x)
is represented by the following matrix

h^{\textsc{tt}}_{\mu\nu}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&0&0&0\\
0&h_{+}&h_{\times}&0\\
0&h_{\times}&-h_{+}&0\\
0&0&0&0\end{array}\right)\ ,

(15.21)

which means that the gravitational wave has two polarizations which oscillates transversely to its path
[
105
]
. These oscillations causes stretches of the space, which can be measured and are the bases around which experiments on detection of gravitational waves are built
[
105
]
.

16
Linearized gravity with boundary

The analysis of this Section on the effects of the presence of a boundary in the diff invariant theory of
is organized as follows.

Specifically in Section
16.1
, we consider the 4D
theory with a planar boundary, implemented using a Heaviside theta distribution in the bulk action. We derive the most general
following a method introduced by Symanzik, without imposing them by hand. The presence of the boundary breaks the invariance under diffeomorphisms, resulting in a breaking of the diffeomorphism Ward identity. From this, conserved currents and their
algebra are derived using standard
methods. The central charge appears to be proportional to the inverse of the
“coupling” constant, as in topological field theories. Section
16.2
focuses on the identification of the holographically induced 3D theory. By first solving the current conservation equation, we find the 3D degrees of freedom and determine the most general transformations that preserve their invariance. Remarkably, we discover that these transformations are diffeomorphisms, which is not obvious. Thus, diffeomorphism invariance emerges as a consequence of our procedure rather than a mere requirement. Once we have the quantum fields with their transformations, we arrive at the most general 3D action, satisfying the additional
requirements of locality and power counting. Using this, in Section
16.3
we establish the holographic connection, yielding two solutions for the induced 3D theory. Our results are summarized in Section
16.4
.

16.1
The bulk model

is the theory of a rank-2 symmetric tensor field
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
on a flat Minkowskian background. We introduce a planar boundary at
x^{3}=0
in the
action by means of a Heaviside step function, which confines the model on a half space with single-sided planar boundary

S_{bulk}=\lambda\int d^{4}x\;\theta(x^{3})\left(\frac{1}{4}F^{\mu}_{\ \mu\nu}F_{\rho}^{\ \rho\nu}-\frac{1}{6}F^{\mu\nu\rho}F_{\mu\nu\rho}\right)\ ,

(16.1)

where
F^{\mu\nu\rho}(x)
is the rank-3 tensor field strength associated to the tensor field
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
introduced in Section
12

[
6
,
4
]

F_{\mu\nu\rho}=F_{\nu\mu\rho}=\partial_{\mu}h_{\nu\rho}+\partial_{\nu}h_{\mu\rho}-2\partial_{\rho}h_{\mu\nu}\ ,

(16.2)

satisfying the following properties

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu\rho}+F_{\nu\rho\mu}+F_{\rho\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

(16.3)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\alpha\mu\nu\rho}\partial^{\mu}F^{\beta\nu\rho}

\displaystyle=0\ .

(16.4)

Notice that this field strength, defined in the context of Section
12
, is invariant unader the longitudinal diff (fracton) symmetry (
12.1
), but it is not invarint under the more general diff transformation (
15.1
). The constant
\lambda
in (
16.1
) could be reabsorbed through a redefinition of
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
, however we maintain it in order to keep track of the bulk contributions. Due to the presence of the boundary at
x^{3}=0
the
x^{3}
-derivative of the gauge field at
x^{3}=0
must be considered as independent from
h_{\mu\nu}(x)

[
147
]
, thus we define

\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\equiv\partial_{3}h_{\mu\nu}|_{x^{3}=0}\ ,

(16.5)

and the fields have the mass dimensions

[h_{\mu\nu}]=1\quad;\quad[\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}]=2\ .

(16.6)

As already discussed, the
theory (
16.1
) in the absence of a boundary is invariant under the infinitesimal diffeomorphism transformation

\delta_{diff}h_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\Lambda_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\Lambda_{\mu}\ ,

(16.7)

where
\Lambda_{\mu}(x)
is a local vector parameter.
is a gauge field theory, for which a gauge should be fixed. We choose the axial gauge, as customary in presence of a boundary

h_{\mu 3}=0\ .

(16.8)

This can be realized by means of a vector Lagrange multiplier
b^{\mu}(x)
through the gauge-fixing term

S_{gf}=\int d^{4}x\;\theta(x^{3})b^{\mu}h_{\mu 3}\ .

(16.9)

Moreover, the following source term is needed

S_{J}=\int d^{4}x\left[\theta(x^{3})J^{ab}h_{ab}+\delta(x^{3})\tilde{J}^{ab}\tilde{h}_{ab}\right]\ ,

(16.10)

where, together with the external field
J^{ab}(x)
associated to the tensor gauge field
h_{ab}(x)
, on the boundary an additional external source
\tilde{J}^{ab}(x)
is coupled to
\tilde{h}_{ab}(x)
(
16.5
). The presence of a boundary requires
. We follow Symanzik’s approach
[
10
]
previously discussed, by adding to the action a boundary term constrained only by power-counting and locality. This modifies the
by a boundary contribution, and the
are then obtained by means of a simple variational principle. For our model the boundary term is

S_{bd}=\int d^{4}x\delta(x^{3})\left[\xi_{0}h_{ab}h^{ab}+\xi_{1}\tilde{h}_{ab}h^{ab}+\xi_{2}\epsilon^{abc}h_{ai}\partial_{b}h_{c}^{i}+\xi_{3}h^{2}+\xi_{4}\tilde{h}h\right]\ ,

(16.11)

where

h\equiv\eta^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}\quad;\quad\tilde{h}\equiv\eta^{\mu\nu}\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}\ ,

(16.12)

and the
\xi_{i},\ i=\{0,...,4\}
, are constant parameters with mass dimensions

[\xi_{0}]=[\xi_{3}]=1\quad;\quad[\xi_{1}]=[\xi_{2}]=[\xi_{4}]=0\ .

(16.13)

Notice that, due to the gauge-fixing condition (
16.8
), the nontrivial part of the trace
h(x)
is
\eta^{ab}h_{ab}(x)
. The full action of the model finally is

S_{tot}=S_{bulk}+S_{gf}+S_{J}+S_{bd}\ .

(16.14)

Equations of motion and boundary conditions

Ward identities

From the
for
h_{\mu\nu}(x)
(
16.16
) we get

0=\int dx^{3}\partial_{a}\frac{\delta S_{tot}}{\delta h_{ab}}=2\lambda\left(\partial^{b}\tilde{h}-\partial_{a}\tilde{h}^{ab}\right)_{x^{3}=0}+\int dx^{3}\theta(x^{3})\partial_{a}J^{ab}\ ,

(16.29)

where we used the
(
16.18

\int dx^{3}\theta(x^{3})\partial_{a}J^{ab}=-2\lambda\left(\partial^{b}\tilde{h}-\partial_{a}\tilde{h}^{ab}\right)_{x^{3}=0}\ ,

(16.30)

which is broken on the boundary
x^{3}=0
.
In the same way, from the
of
\tilde{h}_{\mu\nu}(x)
(

0=\int dx^{3}\partial_{a}\frac{\delta S_{tot}}{\delta\partial_{3}h_{ab}}=\partial_{a}\tilde{J}^{ab}|_{x^{3}=0}-2\lambda\left(-\partial_{a}h^{ab}+\partial^{b}h\right)_{x^{3}=0}\ ,

(16.31)

which represents a local Ward identity, broken by the boundary

\partial_{a}\tilde{J}^{ab}|_{x^{3}=0}=2\lambda\left(-\partial_{a}h^{ab}+\partial^{b}h\right)_{x^{3}=0}\ .

(16.32)

Notice that the r.h.s. describes the conservation on the boundary of the current
K^{ab}(X)
(

(
16.24
), hence we may write

\partial_{a}\tilde{J}^{ab}|_{x^{3}=0}=0\ .

(16.33)

Going on-shell (
J=\tilde{J}=0
), the broken Ward identity (
16.30
) yields

\left(\partial^{b}\tilde{h}-\partial_{a}\tilde{h}^{ab}\right)_{x^{3}=0}=0\ ,

(16.34)

which, again, is a current conservation equation

\partial_{a}\tilde{K}^{ab}=0\ ,

(16.35)

with

\tilde{K}^{ab}\equiv\tilde{h}^{ab}-\eta^{ab}\tilde{h}\ .

(16.36)

Hence, the presence of a planar boundary in
theory has as a consequence the presence of conserved currents, which consist of the particular combinations (

Kac-Moody Algebra

By computing the functional derivative with respect to
J^{mn}(x^{\prime})
of the broken Ward identity (
16.30
),
i.e.

\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{mn}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{LG-LG-ward1}
:

\int_{0}^{\infty}dx^{3}\partial_{a}\left(\frac{\delta^{a}_{m}\delta^{b}_{n}+\delta^{a}_{n}\delta^{b}_{m}}{2}\delta^{(4)}(x-x^{\prime})\right)=-2\lambda\left(\eta_{ac}\partial^{b}-\delta^{b}_{c}\partial_{a}\right)\frac{\delta Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta\tilde{J}_{ac}\delta J^{\prime mn}}\ ,

(16.37)

we get the commutation relations

\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta^{a}_{m}\delta^{b}_{n}+\delta^{a}_{n}\delta^{b}_{m}\right)\partial_{a}\delta^{(3)}(X-X^{\prime})=-2i\lambda\left(\eta_{ac}\eta^{b0}-\delta^{b}_{c}\delta^{0}_{a}\right)\left[\tilde{h}^{ac}\ ,\ h^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\ ,

(16.38)

where we used the on-shell constraint (
16.34
). By setting

•

b=0
we have

\left(\delta^{a}_{m}\delta^{0}_{n}+\delta^{a}_{n}\delta^{0}_{m}\right)\partial_{a}\delta^{(3)}(X-X^{\prime})=4i\lambda\left[\tilde{h}^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{d}}\ ,\ h^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\ ,

(16.39)

from which, integrating over time,

–

m=n=0

\left[\tilde{h}^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{d}}\ ,\ h^{\prime}_{00}\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}=0\ .

(16.40)

–

\left[\tilde{h}^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{d}}\ ,\ h^{\prime}_{0\textsc{n}}\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}=-\frac{i}{4\lambda}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ .

(16.41)

This can be identified as a

[

–

m=\textsc{m},\ n=\textsc{n}

(16.44)

•

b=\textsc{b}

(16.45)

–

m=n=0

\left[\tilde{h}^{0\textsc{b}}\ ,\ h^{\prime}_{00}\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}=0\ .

(16.46)

(16.47)

(16.48)

Here again a

We now compute the functional derivative of the broken Ward identity (

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{h}^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{d}}\ ,\ h^{\prime}_{0\textsc{n}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{4\lambda}\left(\delta^{\textsc{a}}_{\textsc{m}}\delta^{\textsc{b}}_{\textsc{n}}+\delta^{\textsc{a}}_{\textsc{n}}\delta^{\textsc{b}}_{\textsc{m}}\right)\partial_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ .

The above

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{4\lambda}\left(\delta^{\textsc{a}}_{m}\delta^{\textsc{b}}_{n}+\delta^{\textsc{a}}_{n}\delta^{\textsc{b}}_{m}-2\eta^{\textsc{a}\textsc{b}}\eta_{mn}\right)\partial_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ .

(16.55)

The existence of a
algebraic structure for conserved currents on the boundary of 4D
confirms the guess made in
[

16.2
The boundary

The degrees of freedom

The presence of a 3D boundary in the 4D theory described by the action
S_{tot}
(
16.14
) induces a 3D theory, whose field content is determined by the solution of the on-shell broken Ward identity (
16.30
)

\partial_{a}\left(\tilde{h}^{ab}-\eta^{ab}\tilde{h}\right)_{x^{3}=0}=0

(16.56)

and of the
(
16.24
)

\partial_{a}\left(h^{ab}-\eta^{ab}h\right)_{x^{3}=0}=0\ .

(16.57)

Let us consider first (
16.56
). Define

\tilde{C}^{ab}\equiv\tilde{h}^{ab}-\eta^{ab}\tilde{h}\ ,

(16.58)

whose trace is

\tilde{C}=\eta_{ab}\tilde{C}^{ab}=-2\tilde{h}\ .

(16.59)

Eq. (
16.56
) then reads

\partial_{a}\tilde{C}^{ab}=0\ .

(16.60)

In order to find the most general solution, let us parametrize the symmetric tensor
\tilde{C}^{ab}(X)
as follows

\tilde{C}^{ab}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\epsilon^{amn}\partial_{m}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{\ b}+\epsilon^{bmn}\partial_{m}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{\ a}\right)\ .

(16.61)

Because of (
16.60
) it must be

\epsilon^{bmn}\partial_{m}\partial_{a}\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{\ a}=0\ ,

(16.62)

which is solved by

\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{\ a}=\epsilon^{acd}\partial_{c}\tilde{\sigma}_{nd}+\partial_{n}\phi^{a}\ ,

(16.63)

but we observe that the
\phi^{a}(X)
contribution trivializes
\tilde{C}^{ab}(X)
(
16.61
). Hence

\tilde{\Sigma}_{n}^{\ a}=\epsilon^{acd}\partial_{c}\tilde{\sigma}_{nd}\ .

(16.64)

In terms of this result,
\tilde{C}^{ab}(X)
(
16.61
) solves (
16.60
), and reads

\tilde{C}^{ab}=\epsilon^{bmn}\epsilon^{acd}\partial_{m}\partial_{c}\tilde{\sigma}_{nd}\ ,

(16.65)

with
\tilde{\sigma}_{ab}(X)=\tilde{\sigma}_{ba}(X)
as a consequence of the fact that
\tilde{C}^{ab}(X)
is symmetric
\tilde{C}^{ab}(X)=\tilde{C}^{ba}(X)
, and with
[\tilde{\sigma}]=0
. Thus the general solution for
\tilde{h}^{ab}(X)
is

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{ab}

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}^{ab}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{ab}\tilde{C}

(16.66)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{ab}(\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{\;n}-\partial^{m}\partial^{n}\tilde{\sigma}_{mn})+\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}+\partial^{a}\partial^{b}\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{\;n}-\partial_{c}(\partial^{b}\tilde{\sigma}^{am}+\partial^{a}\tilde{\sigma}^{bc})\ .

The Eq.(
16.57
) for
h^{ab}(x)
has the same structure as (
16.56
), therefore the solution has the same form (
16.66
). We finally get

\displaystyle\tilde{h}^{ab}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{bmn}\epsilon^{acd}\partial_{m}\partial_{c}\tilde{\sigma}_{nd}+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{ab}(\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{\;n}-\partial^{m}\partial^{n}\tilde{\sigma}_{mn})

(16.67)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{ab}(\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{\;n}-\partial^{m}\partial^{n}\tilde{\sigma}_{mn})+\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}+\partial^{a}\partial^{b}\tilde{\sigma}_{n}^{\;n}-\partial_{c}(\partial^{b}\tilde{\sigma}^{ac}+\partial^{a}\tilde{\sigma}^{bc})

\displaystyle h^{ab}

\displaystyle=\epsilon^{bmn}\epsilon^{acd}\partial_{m}\partial_{c}\sigma_{nd}+\frac{1}{2}\eta^{ab}(\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\sigma_{n}^{\;n}-\partial^{m}\partial^{n}\sigma_{mn})

(16.68)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{ab}(\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\sigma_{n}^{\;n}-\partial^{m}\partial^{n}\sigma_{mn})+\partial_{m}\partial^{m}\sigma^{ab}+\partial^{a}\partial^{b}\sigma_{n}^{\;n}-\partial_{c}(\partial^{b}\sigma^{ac}+\partial^{a}\sigma^{bc})\ ,

which means that the fields of the induced 3D theory are identified as the rank-2 symmetric tensors
\sigma^{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}(X)
. Moreover, these solutions are invariant under the following transformations of the boundary fields
\sigma_{ab}(X),\ \tilde{\sigma}_{ab}(X)

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}\tilde{h}_{ab}=0\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\tilde{\delta}\tilde{\sigma}_{mn}=\partial_{m}\tilde{\xi}_{n}+\partial_{n}\tilde{\xi}_{m}

(16.69)

\displaystyle\delta h_{ab}=0\quad

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow\quad\delta\sigma_{mn}=\partial_{m}\xi_{n}+\partial_{n}\xi_{m}\ ,

(16.70)

which remarkably means that the induced boundary theory must be invariant under infinitesimal diffeomorphisms, which therefore is a consequence of the general method we followed to introduce a boundary in LG, without need of requiring it explicitly.

Most general 3D action

As a consequence of the solutions
\tilde{h}_{ab}(x)
(
16.67
) and
h_{ab}(x)
(
16.68
) and of their mass dimensions (
16.6
), the boundary fields
\sigma_{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{ab}(X)
should have mass dimensions
[\sigma]=-1
and
[\tilde{\sigma}]=0
. However, in 3D the canonical choices for the mass dimensions of the tensor fields are two :

1.

[\sigma]=[\tilde{\sigma}]=1
, which can be realized by rescaling as follows

\tilde{\sigma}\to\tilde{M}^{-1}\tilde{\sigma}\quad;\quad\sigma\to M^{-2}\sigma\ .

(16.71)

However in this case power-counting and locality constrain the action to the following Chern-Simons/BF-like action
[
14
]
,

S=\int d^{3}x\epsilon^{abc}\left(a_{1}\sigma_{ad}\partial_{b}\sigma_{c}^{\ d}+a_{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{ad}\partial_{b}\sigma_{c}^{\ d}+a_{3}\tilde{\sigma}_{ad}\partial_{b}\tilde{\sigma}_{c}^{\ d}\right)

(16.72)

which is not invariant under the diffeomorphism transformations
\delta
(
16.70
) and
\tilde{\delta}
(
16.69
) :

\displaystyle\delta S

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\epsilon^{abc}\left(2a_{1}\sigma_{ad}+a_{2}\tilde{\sigma}_{ad}\right)\partial_{b}\partial^{d}\xi_{c}

(16.73)

\displaystyle\tilde{\delta}S

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\epsilon^{abc}\left(a_{2}\sigma_{ad}+2a_{3}\tilde{\sigma}_{ad}\right)\partial_{b}\partial^{d}\tilde{\xi}_{c}\ ,

(16.74)

which indeed vanish only at the trivial case (
a_{1}=a_{2}=a_{3}=0
). Thus we must discard this possibility.

2.

[\sigma]=[\tilde{\sigma}]=\frac{1}{2}
, achieved by rescaling

\tilde{\sigma}\to\tilde{M}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{\sigma}\quad;\quad\sigma\to M^{-\frac{3}{2}}\sigma\ ,

(16.75)

which, instead, leads to a nontrivial solution, as we shall see in what follows.

The most general action invariant under the infinitesimal diffeomorphisms
\tilde{\delta}
(
16.69
) and
\delta
(
16.70
) has the following structure

S_{3D}[\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}]=\kappa S_{LG}[\sigma]+\tilde{\kappa}\tilde{S}_{LG}[\tilde{\sigma}]+\kappa_{m}S_{mix}[\sigma,\tilde{\sigma}]\ ,

(16.76)

where
\kappa,\ \tilde{\kappa},\ \kappa_{m}
are dimensionless constants, and
S_{LG}[\sigma]
and
\tilde{S}_{LG}[\tilde{\sigma}]
are
contributions analogous to (
16.1
), written in terms of the boundary tensor field
\sigma_{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{ab}(X)
, respectively

\displaystyle S_{LG}

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\left(\frac{1}{4}f^{a}_{\ ac}f_{b}^{\ bc}-\frac{1}{6}f^{abc}f_{abc}\right)

(16.77)

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{LG}

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}x\left(\frac{1}{4}\tilde{f}^{a}_{\ ac}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ bc}-\frac{1}{6}\tilde{f}^{abc}\tilde{f}_{abc}\right)\ ,

(16.78)

with

\displaystyle f_{abc}

\displaystyle=f_{bac}=\partial_{a}\sigma_{bc}+\partial_{b}\sigma_{ac}-2\partial_{c}\sigma_{ab}

(16.79)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{abc}

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}_{bac}=\partial_{a}\tilde{\sigma}_{bc}+\partial_{b}\tilde{\sigma}_{ac}-2\partial_{c}\tilde{\sigma}_{ab}\ ,

(16.80)

satisfying the ciclicity property

\displaystyle f^{abc}+f^{bca}+f^{cab}

\displaystyle=0

(16.81)

\displaystyle\tilde{f}^{abc}+\tilde{f}^{bca}+\tilde{f}^{cab}

\displaystyle=0\ .

(16.82)

Notice that no Chern-Simons or BF contributions like in (
16.72
) are allowed as a consequence of the diffeomorphism invariances
\delta S_{3D}=\tilde{\delta}S_{3D}=0
. The
S_{mix}
term in (
16.76
) is the most general one depending on both
\sigma_{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{ab}(X)
, compatible with power-counting and the invariances
\delta S_{mix}=\tilde{\delta}S_{mix}=0
. Excluding again Chern-Simons/BF-like contributions, which are not invariant under diffeomorphisms, we have

S_{mix}=\int d^{3}x\left\{a_{0}\partial_{a}\sigma\partial^{a}\tilde{\sigma}+a_{1}\partial_{c}\sigma_{ab}\partial^{c}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}+a_{2}\partial_{a}\sigma\partial_{b}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}+a_{3}\partial_{a}\tilde{\sigma}\partial_{b}\sigma^{ab}+a_{4}\partial_{c}\sigma_{ab}\partial^{a}\tilde{\sigma}^{bc}\right\}\ .

(16.83)

Under the diff transformation
\delta
(
16.70
) we get

\begin{split}\delta S_{mix}&=-\int d^{3}x\left\{\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}\left[\left(2a_{1}+a_{4}\right)\partial_{a}\partial^{2}\xi_{b}+\left(2a_{2}+a_{4}\right)\partial_{a}\partial_{b}\partial_{m}\xi^{m}\right]+2\tilde{\sigma}\left(a_{0}+a_{3}\right)\partial_{m}\partial^{2}\xi^{m}\right\}\ ,\end{split}

(16.84)

which means that the action is invariant,
i.e.\ \delta S_{mix}=0
, if

\begin{split}&a_{3}=-a_{0}\\
&a_{4}=-2a_{1}\\
&a_{2}=a_{1}\ .\end{split}

(16.85)

The
\delta
-invariant
S_{mix}
action term is

S_{mix}=\int d^{3}x\left\{a_{0}\partial_{a}\sigma\partial^{a}\tilde{\sigma}+a_{1}\partial_{c}\sigma_{ab}\partial^{c}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}+a_{1}\partial_{a}\sigma\partial_{b}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}-a_{0}\partial_{a}\tilde{\sigma}\partial_{b}\sigma^{ab}-2a_{1}\partial_{c}\sigma_{ab}\partial^{a}\tilde{\sigma}^{bc}\right\}\ .

(16.86)

Requiring now invariance under
\tilde{\delta}
(
16.69
), we get

\begin{split}\tilde{\delta}S_{mix}=0&=2\int d^{3}x\left\{\sigma_{ab}\left(a_{0}+a_{1}\right)\partial^{a}\partial^{b}\partial^{m}\tilde{\xi}_{m}-\sigma\left(a_{0}+a_{1}\right)\partial_{m}\partial^{2}\tilde{\xi}^{m}\right\}\ ,\end{split}

(16.87)

hence it must be

a_{1}=-a_{0}\ .

(16.88)

Therefore the mixed action term (
16.86
) invariant under both
\tilde{\delta}
(
16.69
) and
\delta
(
16.70
) is

S_{mix}=a_{0}\int d^{3}x\left\{\partial_{a}\sigma\partial^{a}\tilde{\sigma}-\partial_{c}\sigma_{ab}\partial^{c}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}-\partial_{a}\sigma\partial_{b}\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}-\partial_{a}\tilde{\sigma}\partial_{b}\sigma^{ab}+2\partial_{c}\sigma_{ab}\partial^{a}\tilde{\sigma}^{bc}\right\}\ .

(16.89)

After reabsorbing the
a_{0}
parameter into
\kappa_{m}
in (
16.76
), we observe that using the definitions of
f^{abc}(X)
(
16.79
) and
\tilde{f}^{abc}(X)
(
16.80
)
S_{mix}
(
16.89
) can be written as

S_{mix}=\int d^{3}x\left(\frac{1}{4}f^{a}_{\ ac}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ bc}-\frac{1}{6}f^{abc}\tilde{f}_{abc}\right)\ .

(16.90)

The most general invariant action therefore is

\begin{split}S_{3D}&=\kappa S_{LG}+\tilde{\kappa}\tilde{S}_{LG}+\kappa_{m}S_{mix}\\
&=\int d^{3}x\left\{\kappa\left(\tfrac{1}{4}f^{a}_{\ ac}f_{b}^{\ bc}-\tfrac{1}{6}f^{abc}f_{abc}\right)+\tilde{\kappa}\left(\tfrac{1}{4}\tilde{f}^{a}_{\ ac}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ bc}-\tfrac{1}{6}\tilde{f}^{abc}\tilde{f}_{abc}\right)+\right.\\
&\qquad\qquad\!\!\!\!+\left.\kappa_{m}\left(\tfrac{1}{4}f^{a}_{\ ac}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ bc}-\tfrac{1}{6}f^{abc}\tilde{f}_{abc}\right)\right\}\ .\end{split}

(16.91)

We finally observe that
S_{mix}
(
16.90
) can be written as

S_{mix}=\int d^{3}x\epsilon^{abc}\epsilon^{def}\sigma_{ad}\partial_{b}\partial_{e}\tilde{\sigma}_{cf}\ ,

(16.92)

hence, replacing
\tilde{\sigma}_{ab}(X)
with
\sigma_{ab}(X)
we have an alternative way to write the 3D
action

\begin{split}S_{LG}&=\int d^{3}x(\epsilon^{abc}\partial_{b}\sigma_{am})(\epsilon^{pnm}\partial_{n}\sigma_{pc})\ ,\end{split}

(16.93)

whose
are

\epsilon^{ap_{1}p_{2}}\epsilon^{bp_{3}p_{4}}\partial_{p_{1}}\partial_{p_{3}}h_{p_{2}p_{4}}=0\ ,

(16.94)

which are those of
written in an alternative and more compact way. A similar expression holds for 4D
, whose
can be written as

\epsilon^{\mu\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}\epsilon^{\nu\alpha_{4}\alpha_{5}\alpha_{6}}\eta_{\alpha_{3}\alpha_{6}}\partial_{\alpha_{1}}\partial_{\alpha_{4}}h_{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{5}}=0\ .

(16.95)

Equations of motion of the 3D induced theory

From the ciclicity property of
f^{abc}(X)
and
\tilde{f}^{abc}(X)
(
16.81
), (
16.82
), we find the following
for the boundary fields
\sigma_{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{ab}(X)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{3D}}{\delta\sigma_{mn}}=

\displaystyle\kappa\left[-\partial_{a}f^{mna}+\eta^{mn}\partial_{a}f_{b}^{\ ba}-\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\partial^{m}f_{b}^{\ bn}+\partial^{n}f_{b}^{\ bm}\right)\right]+

(16.96)

\displaystyle+\frac{\kappa_{m}}{2}\left[-\partial_{a}\tilde{f}^{mna}+\eta^{mn}\partial_{a}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ ba}-\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\partial^{m}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ bn}+\partial^{n}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ bm}\right)\right]=0

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S_{3D}}{\delta\tilde{\sigma}_{mn}}=

\displaystyle\tilde{\kappa}\left[-\partial_{a}\tilde{f}^{mna}+\eta^{mn}\partial_{a}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ ba}-\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\partial^{m}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ bn}+\partial^{n}\tilde{f}_{b}^{\ bm}\right)\right]+

(16.97)

\displaystyle+\frac{\kappa_{m}}{2}\left[-\partial_{a}f^{mna}+\eta^{mn}\partial_{a}f_{b}^{\ ba}-\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\partial^{m}f_{b}^{\ bn}+\partial^{n}f_{b}^{\ bm}\right)\right]=0\ .

16.4
Summary of results

In this Section we studied the effect of the presence of a planar boundary on 4D
, realized by means of a Heaviside step function in the action (
16.1
). Following the
approach presented in Chapter
2
we observed that the presence of the boundary breaks the invariance under diffeomorphisms, which are the symmetry transformations of
. Correspondingly, the Ward identity which describes the invariance under diffeomorphisms (
16.30
) acquires a breaking, which is crucial, because from it the main information of the theory might be derived, namely the fields content, the symmetry transformations and the boundary algebra. We wrote “might” because it is not obvious that this can always be done. In fact, we stressed throughout this whole Thesis that this seemed to work for all
, where non trivial boundary dynamics has been first observed, and for a long time this property has been believed to be peculiar of these kind of theories. But we have seen that more recently, similar results have been found in non-topological field theories, like Maxwell theory
[
1
]
, and this motivated the boundary investigations of this Thesis for more general theories, like we did in this Section for
. A first remarkable result is that on the boundary we found two conserved currents (
16.28
) and (
16.36
) which form the algebraic structure (
16.54
) and (
16.55
) of the
type, whose central charge is proportional to the inverse of the
“coupling” constant (
16.42
). This confirms what has been guessed in
[
227
]
, where it was suspected the existence, in 4D
, of a
algebra as a particularly interesting possibility in connection with Weinberg’s soft graviton theorems
[
247
,
248
,
249
]
.
Since the central charge of a
algebra must be positive, this is mostly useful to determine the sign of the overall
action, which otherwise should be determined by imposing that the energy density, that is the 00-component of the energy-momentum tensor, is positive, which in gravity is a known tricky issue
[
105
,
250
]
. Moreover, we were able to solve the on-shell Ward identity (
16.56
) and the universal
(
16.57
) getting (
16.67
) and (
16.68
), which allowed us to express, on the boundary, the 4D bulk fields
h^{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{h}^{ab}(X)
in terms of 3D fields which are the degrees of freedom of the induced 3D theory. We found that these latter, like their 4D ancestors, are rank-2 symmetric tensor fields :
\sigma^{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}(X)
. This, as
shows, seems to be peculiar of non topological
. Indeed what is usually found in
is that the fields living on the
d-1
-dimensional boundary are tensors of lower rank with respect their
d
-dimensional counterpart : from rank-2 tensors one finds vectors in the topological 4D BF theory
[
88
]
and the boundary reduction of the gauge field in Chern-Simons theory gives scalars, as we have seen in Chapters
4
and
5
. Here, instead, the 3D boundary fields are rank-2 symmetric tensor fields as those of 4D
. And, quite interestingly, the transformation which keeps invariant the definition of the boundary fields turns out to be the diffeomorphisms (
16.69
) and (
16.70
), which therefore are a consequence of the introduction of the boundary, rather than an
a priori
request. Given the dynamical fields and the symmetry transformations, requiring locality and power counting allowed us to find the most general 3D action
S_{3D}
(
16.91
), which consists of three terms. Each term being invariant by its own,
S_{3D}
depends on three constants which we do not reduced by redefining the 3D fields as we could, but we fixed them by establishing a “holographic” contact as our last step. This has been realized by requiring that the
of the 3D action
S_{3D}
coincide with the
of the 4D theory. To do that, we had at our disposal the 4 parameters on which
S_{bd}
(
16.11
) depends and the three constants in
S_{3D}
(
16.91
). As an outcome of this tuning, we found two possibilities, depending on the value of one particular parameter appearing in
S_{bd}
:
S_{3D}
describes either
for one single tensor field (
16.125
), or the action (
16.126
), containing two decoupled
terms for the boundary tensor fields
\sigma^{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{\sigma}^{ab}(X)
and one term which mixes them. As a last, but probably not least, fact, we remark that in any case the
S_{bd}
action term which governs the holographic contact contains a mass term (
16.114
) for the bulk tensor field
h_{ab}(x)
of the particular Fierz-Pauli type
[
211
,
214
,
215
,
218
,
219
]
, with a free parameter
\xi_{0}
which we can interpret as a mass.

Concluding Remarks

We started with the idea of an analysis of boundary effects on theories both standard and not, within a formal
approach. We ended up doing this and more. Indeed the formal requirements of
, together with phenomenological inputs, also pushed us into the development of a new, well defined,
for fracton quasiparticles
[
4
]
. This was surely necessary in order to study boundary effects on fracton models in a precise and
-oriented way, but the fact of having a proper
for such models means even more, since one is now able to delve into the secrets of fractons without boundary as well, and without the necessity of invoking “intuitions” as it is explicitly admitted in the seminal papers
[
160
,
161
]
, but just by applying straightforwardly
first principles. All that would not have happened if it were not for phenomenological inputs, which made us look with interest into the theory of fractons, together with the desire to only trust first principles and that “high flexibility and perfection of the
formalism” that motivated Symanzik. With this spirit we were thus able to build a new theory, but also to expand the dictionary of
with boundary : we observed the effect of a metric in
to have a non-trivial effect on the edge states, but we also showed that the bulk-boundary paradigm is much wider than expected, being extensible also to non-
. We have remarked many times that dealing with boundaries may have surprising outcomes, and this just by thinking about the standard, textbook example of
with boundary in flat spacetime, from which one starts with an unphysical theory and end up talking about experimental observation on the edge of Hall systems. But, as we shall see in more details in what follows, the results presented in this Thesis concerns an even wider variety of areas of physics : condensed matter (
,
, higher order
,
Effect, fracton phases…), elasticity, gravity (massive, self dual, torsional effects…) all these are words that have appeared when discussing about the results we obtained on the edge of either
, fractons or
with boundary.

Final overview on the specific results

The results achieved in this Thesis could be quickly summarized by saying that on the boundary of
on a generic manifold in curved spacetime Hall systems with accelerated edge modes are found, a 3D traceless fracton model can be recovered as induced by a traceful scalar charge theory of fractons in the bulk, and finally a 3D
-like theory exists on the edge of 4D
itself. But there is much more to say about the results obtained from the works collected in this Thesis, both dealing with boundary effects and not. In particular

TQFTs in curved spacetimes :

even though the metric does not affect the bulk of the
, by definition of a topological theory, a memory of it is kept by the boundary, from which we observed that the metric does intervene in the local observables of the lower-dimensional theory, namely in the chiral velocities. The dictionary has thus been updated as follows

Flat

with boundary

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad\mbox{{constant} chiral edge velocities}

Curved

with boundary

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\qquad\mbox{{local} chiral edge velocities}\ .

This allows for a formal theoretical explanation of the observed accelerated edge modes of some condensed matter systems, which could not be justified in terms of the standard flat bulk-boundary approach, but only through an
ad hoc
introduction coming from phenomenological requirements. Moreover, with our results we can make a contact between these phenomenological requirements (confining potential…) and the
procedure : the induced metric
\gamma(x)
play the role of the phenomenological potential
V(x)
which makes the edge velocities acquire spacetime dependence
i.e.\ v=v(x)
. This same thing happen, also in the flat case, when thinking about the Chern-Simons coupling constant
\kappa
, which represents the filling factor
\nu
of the phenomenological model on the boundary. These results can be found in
[
2
]
and
[
3
]
.

New QFT for fractons :

built from a
symmetry-based approach, this new
covariant
theory for fractons allowed to recover all the results of the Literature from first principles of
, and more. Indeed we observed

•

an immediate relation with
. This claim can be found in the fracton Literature
[
198
,
199
,
200
,
177
]
, but it is immediately apparent from the field theoretical point of view. This relation appears as an additional term in the action with its own “coupling”. The fact that a quadratic, Lorentz and gauge invariant theory depends on one unavoidable constant is quite uncommon, if not unique. It is not even clear how to call this constant, since it cannot be a “coupling” constant, being the theory
S_{inv}
(
12.7
) free and non interacting, nor a mass, being dimensionless. This peculiarity originates from the fact that the space of functionals invariant under the covariant fractonic transformation (
12.5
) has dimension two, instead of one as it commonly happens. To our knowledge, the only exception is given by the 3D Maxwell-Chern-Simons theory
[
70
]
, which depends on one “true” constant as well, but in that case the constant can be identified as a mass. We might say that gravitons may exist alone, while fractons necessarily come with gravitons, lacking, up to now, a symmetry which uniquely determines them.

•

the presence of an energy momentum tensor which has strong analogies with the standard electromagnetic one. The possibility of computing it as a variation of the metric was allowed only by the fact that we had a covariant theory, and confirmed, through the computation of the “Lorentz” force, that the theory concerned fully mobile dipoles, as one would expect from a fracton model. We however observe that such energy-momentum tensor is not conserved, which, again, could be a confirmation of the theory we are dealing with : standard non-covariant fracton models display broken translational symmetry, as a consequence of dipole conservation. In our covariant theory this can be encoded in the non-conservation of the energy-momentum tensor (its spatial components in particular). From a formal point of view one can also notice that this is in agreement with Weinberg-Witten theorem
[
251
]
when keeping in mind that here we have massless spin two objects

Theorem 2 : A theory that allows the construction of a conserved Lorentz covariant energy-momentum tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
for which
\int d^{3}xT^{\mu\nu}
is the energy-momentum four-vector cannot contain massless particles of spin
j>1
.

Additionally, another interesting observation on that regard is that the stress-energy tensor is not traceless, hence the theory is not scale invariant, differently from the classical Maxwell theory. Instead, tracelessness is recovered in 6D. Had we dealt with a field theory exercise, it would have been better to face the problem in 6D, where the gauge field has mass dimensions
[A_{\mu\nu}]=2
and, consequently, the gauge parameter would have been given vanishing dimensions, as usual in gauge field theory. But the most general invariant action (
12.7
) consists of two terms :
S_{LG}
(
12.9
) and
S_{fract}
(
12.8
) which are respectively the actions for linearized gravity and for fractons, both physically relevant in 4D.

•

every physical quantity is invariant at sight. The definition of the invariant rank-3 field strength
F_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)
in terms of which the electric and magnetic tensor fields
E^{ij}(x)
(
12.40
) and
B^{ij}(x)
(
12.48
) of fractons can be written, makes them immediately invariant quantities under the fracton symmetry (
12.5
), allowing for them to be identifiable as the physical objects of the theory.

•

a nontrivial boundary physics. As a consequence of the peculiarity of the fractonic symmetry, a “generalized”
algebra is recovered on the boundary, which matches exactly the one observed in the context of higher order
, thus adding a checkmark on the always suspected relation between these materials and edge states of fracton models
[
184
,
55
]
. The boundary theory display a curious phenomenon for which the edge
turn out to be traceless, and the physical model appears to be related to the traceless scalar charge theory of fractons where a higher rank Chern-Simons contribution plays the role of matter, in particular as the charge in the Gauss constraint and of current in the Ampère law. This means that the particle content of the boundary theory involves fractons as the immobile charges, and dipoles as one-dimensional particles (lineons). A broken Faraday equation also suggest the possible presence of fractonic vortices.

These results are collected in
[
4
]
,
[
5
]
and
[
6
]
.

we observe the emergence of a standard
algebra on the boundary which thus makes us able to give a confirmation on speculations about its existence on the boundary of
. This example highlights the drastic consequences of considering a different symmetry in the context of a theory with boundary. Indeed, in comparison with the results on the boundary of the theory of fractons and
