Concerning the Future

We conclude the Thesis with some remarks on future prospects which we think will be possible thanks to the results achieved here. From Chapter
5
,
i.e.

with boundary in curved spacetime, the first thing to mention is that as a consequence of the necessity of an update of the dictionary of
with boundary for explaining the observed local velocities of edge states of
, we are now also able to predict the existence of local velocities of edge modes in other Hall systems, like, in particular,
. Another interesting aspect to analyze from this new dictionary, is the relation between the induced metric on the boundary of
, and the confining/interaction potential responsible in the phenomenological models for such local velocities. For instance one could think of a perturbation expansion of the induced metric
\gamma_{ij}(x)=\eta_{ij}+h_{ij}(x)
, as one does in
, isolating the spacetime dependent part (which would be related to the metric perturbation
h_{ij}(x)
) from the standard, constant, contribution, and see how this non-constant, metric perturbation can encode the
ad hoc
potential typically introduced in the models. That would complete the “duality” of the dictionary : we knew the relation between the coupling and the filling factor
\kappa\leftrightarrow\nu
, and now we also add the relation between the metric and the potential
h_{ij}\leftrightarrow V
. Concerning Part
iii
, the fact of having found a non-trivial physics on the boundary of non-
, on its own, is already a striking result which should enhance the interest in investigating other non-
with boundary. Even more because of the richness in the physical content of those analyzed in the present work. Indeed form the study of both fracton and
theories with boundary, many questions and insights emerge. For instance on the boundary of the theory of fractons described by the action (
14.1
) a 3D traceless scalar charge theory arise, which is a new 3D fracton model. The tracelessness of the theory is an unexpected consequence of the
induced by the boundary, thus a consequence of the fracton symmetry as a breaking from a Ward identity. We see in fact that a different, more general, symmetry such as the one defining
(
i.e.
diffs) gives traceful
, meaning that maybe these disappearance of trace dependence is encoded in something hidden, which still has to be looked for. Concerning this 3D theory, it is known
[
148
]
that fractons in 3D possess a duality with the theory of elasticity, as an analog higher-rank version of the particle-vortex duality
[
90
,
91
]
, therefore the possibility that the 3D action recovered on the boundary shares a form of such higher spin duality is highly nontrivial and worth to be investigated. It will also be interesting to further analyze the relations which both the pure theory of
(
16.1
) and the fracton one (
14.1
) seem to have with a variety of models of gravity and massive gravity, on the boundary. Indeed we have seen that the Symanzik boundary term (
16.11
), necessary in
, needs to contain a Fierz-Pauli mass term (
15.3
) in order to induce a lower-dimensional physics. Moreover, the Chern-Simons-like contribution contained in the 3D traceless theory (
14.64
) induced by fractons, strongly reminds a gravity model known as “self-dual massive gravity”, and the full action appears as a higher rank Maxwell-Chern-Simons term. However it is interesting to notice that the coupling seems to be dimensionless, which means that to better understand the theory and relations with the above-mentioned models the gauge structure and propagators of the theory will have to be computed. But in this Thesis results does not only involve boundaries. Indeed also the covariant
of fractons is a novelty, and one of great interest. In fact, having now a new and well defined theory for these quasiparticles opens the doors at many other
-oriented computations and physical analysis. The first that comes to mind is to apply the same approach of the four-dimensional bulk theory to a three-dimensional one, with the same spirit of building everything from first principles like symmetry, power-counting and covariance, and from this to look at the duality between fractons and elasticity from the
side. In this context 3D fracton models also seem to share relations with theories with torsion
[
233
]
, and since fractons and
are strongly intertwined (remind that no symmetry principle allow us to isolate fractons from gravity!), this fact makes very promizing the idea of investigating torsional effect from a theory of gravity, in order to see if fractons may come out of it and thus better understand their nature. This could also help us with the task of finding a way (symmetry principle,…) to isolate fractons from
without the need of switching off any coefficient by hand. Sharing so many similarities with Maxwell theory, a final remark concerns the possibility of promoting the fracton theory to a non-abelian one, and the possible quantization of the action (
12.7
). As it is well known, the quantization of
is a long standing issue. As far as we know, a quantum field theory of fractons has not been achieved yet. In view of this, the covariant formulation adopted in this Thesis should be quite suitable, especially because the fractonic part (
12.8
) of the action (
12.7
) impressively reminds the electromagnetic Maxwell theory. Hence, one might think about a kind of “fracton QED”, where matter is coupled to fractons. Under this respect, the vector gauge fixing studied in Section
13
can be very useful, not being restricted to the Landau gauge.

Some technical remarks

Appendix 10.A
Derivative of the Heaviside step distribution in curved spacetime

The scalar Dirac delta distribution
\delta^{(n)}(x-x^{\prime})
and the corresponding density
\tilde{\delta}^{(n)}(x-x^{\prime})
are related by
[
113
,
252
]

\delta^{(n)}(x-x^{\prime})=\frac{\tilde{\delta}^{(n)}(x-x^{\prime})}{\sqrt{-g}}\ ,

(10.A.1)

with
g\equiv g(x)
, acting on a test function
f(x)
as follows

\int d^{n}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,\delta^{(n)}(x-x^{\prime})f(x)=\int d^{n}x\,\tilde{\delta}^{(n)}(x-x^{\prime})f(x)=f(x^{\prime})\ .

(10.A.2)

Hence, the functional derivative of a generic dual vector field
V_{\mu}(x)
is the (1,1) tensor

\frac{\delta V_{\mu}(x)}{\delta V_{\nu}(x^{\prime})}=\delta^{\nu}_{\mu}\,\delta^{(n)}(x-x^{\prime})\ .

(10.A.3)

The Heaviside theta distribution is used to describe a boundary. Its general form is
\theta(f(x))
, where
f(x)=f(t,r,\theta)=0
describes the equation of the hypersurface
\partial\mathcal{M}
of the manifold
\mathcal{M}
,
i.e.

\theta(f(x))=\begin{cases}1\;,&\mbox{if }x\in\mathcal{M}\\
0\;,&\mbox{if }x\not\in\mathcal{M}\ .\end{cases}

(10.A.4)

For a generic manifold
\mathcal{M}
, Stokes’ theorem states that

\int_{\mathcal{M}}d^{n}x\;\sqrt{-g}\,\nabla_{\mu}V^{\mu}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}d^{n-1}y\;\sqrt{-\gamma}\;e_{\mu}V^{\mu}\ ,

(10.A.5)

where
e_{\mu}
is the unit vector normal to the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
described by the equation
f(x)=0
,
i.e.

e_{\mu}=-\frac{\partial_{\mu}f}{\sqrt{g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}f\partial_{\nu}f}}\ ,

(10.A.6)

and
\gamma_{ij}
is the induced metric on
\partial\mathcal{M}
. The presence of the boundary
f(x)=0
in the l.h.s. of (
10.A.5
) can be implemented through the introduction of the step distribution (
10.A.4
) :

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{M}}d^{n}x\sqrt{|g|}\ \nabla_{\mu}V^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\int d^{n}x\sqrt{|g|}\ \theta(f(x))\nabla_{\mu}V^{\mu}

(10.A.7)

\displaystyle=-\int d^{n}x\sqrt{|g|}\ \nabla_{\mu}\theta(f(x))V^{\mu}\ .

The integration on the r.h.s. of (
10.A.7
) is performed over all spacetime (
i.e.
boundary at infinity), then, when integrating by parts on the second line, the boundary term vanishes. We can also observe, from (
10.A.7
), that the step function is a scalar quantity. Identifying the r.h.s. of (
10.A.5
) and (
10.A.7
) we get

-\int d^{n}x\sqrt{|g|}\ \nabla_{\mu}\theta(f(x))V^{\mu}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}d^{n-1}x\sqrt{|\gamma|}\ e_{\mu}V^{\mu}\ .

(10.A.8)

In (
10.A.8
) we can identify
\nabla_{\mu}\theta(f(x))
as a scalar Dirac delta
\delta_{\partial\mathcal{M}}
10
10

10

We adopted the notation of
[
253
]
, p.13 Eq.(4.10).

-e_{\mu}\;\delta_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\equiv\nabla_{\mu}\theta(f(x))\ .

(10.A.9)

To see it explicitly and deduce how this distribution acts, we insert (
10.A.9
) back in (
10.A.8
)

\int d^{n}x\sqrt{|g|}\;\delta_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\;e_{\mu}V^{\mu}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}d^{n-1}x\sqrt{|\gamma|}\ e_{\mu}V^{\mu}\ .

(10.A.10)

Considering a constant, radial boundary

f(x)=R-r\quad\Rightarrow\quad e_{\mu}=\frac{\delta_{\mu}^{r}}{\sqrt{g^{rr}}}=\delta_{\mu}^{r}\frac{\sqrt{|g|}}{\sqrt{|\gamma|}}\ ,

(10.A.11)

where we used
[
254
]
:

\sqrt{g^{rr}}=\frac{\sqrt{-\gamma}}{\sqrt{-g}}\ ,

(10.A.12)

we have

\delta_{\partial\mathcal{M}}=\frac{\sqrt{|\gamma|}}{\sqrt{|g|}}\delta(f(x))=\frac{\sqrt{|\gamma|}}{\sqrt{|g|}}\delta(R-r)\ .

(10.A.13)

Therefore, using (
10.A.11
), the derivative of the step function (
10.A.9
) simplifies to

\nabla_{\mu}\theta(R-r)=\partial_{\mu}\theta\left(f(x)\right)=-\delta_{\mu}^{r}\;\delta(r-R)\ .

(10.A.14)

Appendix 10.B
Basis for the
\Omega
-tensors

In the momentum space gauge fixed action
S(g_{1},g_{2};\xi,\kappa)
(
13.16
) the kinetic operator
\Omega_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}(p)
displays the following symmetries

{\Omega}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}(p)={\Omega}_{\nu\mu,\alpha\beta}(p)={\Omega}_{\mu\nu,\beta\alpha}(p)={\Omega}_{\alpha\beta,\mu\nu}(p)\ .

(10.B.1)

It can be expanded on a basis formed by a set of five rank-4 tensors, collectively denoted
X_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}(p)

[
215
,
218
,
214
,
219
,
178
]

X_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}\equiv(A,B,C,D,E)_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

(10.B.2)

with the same symmetry properties (
10.B.1
). Explicitly, the
X
-tensors read
[
215
,
218
,
214
,
219
,
178
,
255
]

\displaystyle A_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{\mu\nu}d_{\alpha\beta}}{3}

(10.B.3)

\displaystyle B_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=e_{\mu\nu}e_{\alpha\beta}

(10.B.4)

\displaystyle C_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(d_{\mu\alpha}d_{\nu\beta}+d_{\mu\beta}d_{\nu\alpha}-\frac{2}{3}d_{\mu\nu}d_{\alpha\beta}\right)

(10.B.5)

\displaystyle D_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(d_{\mu\alpha}e_{\nu\beta}+d_{\mu\beta}e_{\nu\alpha}+e_{\mu\alpha}d_{\nu\beta}+e_{\mu\beta}d_{\nu\alpha}\right)

(10.B.6)

\displaystyle E_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{\mu\nu}\eta_{\alpha\beta}}{4}\;,

(10.B.7)

and
e_{\mu\nu}(p)
and
d_{\mu\nu}(p)
are the transverse and longitudinal projectors (
13.21
),
which are idempotent and orthogonal

e_{\mu\lambda}{e^{\lambda}}_{\nu}=e_{\mu\nu},\ \mathrm{}\ d_{\mu\lambda}{d^{\lambda}}_{\nu}=d_{\mu\nu},\ \mathrm{}\ e_{\mu\lambda}{d^{\lambda}}_{\nu}=0\>.

(10.B.8)

The
X
-tensors have the following properties:

•

decomposition of the rank-4 tensor identity
\mathcal{I}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}
:

A_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}+B_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}+C_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}+D_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}=\mathcal{I}_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}

(10.B.9)

\mathcal{I}_{\mu\nu,\rho\sigma}=\frac{1}{2}(\eta_{\mu\rho}\eta_{\nu\sigma}+\eta_{\mu\sigma}\eta_{\nu\rho})

(10.B.10)

•

idempotency :

X_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \rho\sigma}X_{\rho\sigma,\alpha\beta}=X_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}\ ;

(10.B.11)

•

orthogonality of
A
,
B
,
C
and
D
:

X_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}{{X^{\prime}}^{\alpha\beta}}_{\rho\sigma}=0\ \ \mbox{if}\ (X,X^{\prime})\neq E\ \mbox{and}\ X\neq X^{\prime}\ ;

(10.B.12)

•

contractions with
E
:

\displaystyle A_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}{E^{\alpha\beta}}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{\mu\nu}\eta_{\rho\sigma}}{4}

(10.B.13)

\displaystyle B_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}{E^{\alpha\beta}}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{e_{\mu\nu}\eta_{\rho\sigma}}{4}

(10.B.14)

\displaystyle C_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}{E^{\alpha\beta}}_{\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=D_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}{E^{\alpha\beta}}_{\rho\sigma}=0\ .

(10.B.15)

†
†
margin:

11

Detailed calculations

Appendix 11.A
Solutions of the boundary conditions for the BF model

The
for the 3D bulk theory are given by (
10.14
):

\left.\Lambda^{IJ}X_{J}\right|_{r=R}=0\ ,

(11.A.1)

where
I,J=\{i;j\}=\{0,2;0,2\}
,

\Lambda^{IJ}\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha^{ij}&\zeta^{ij}-\kappa\epsilon^{i1j}\\
\zeta^{ji}&\beta^{ij}\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\alpha^{00}&\alpha^{02}&\zeta^{00}&\zeta^{02}-\hat{\kappa}\\
\alpha^{20}&\alpha^{22}&\zeta^{20}+\hat{\kappa}&\zeta^{22}\\
\zeta^{00}&\zeta^{20}&\beta^{00}&\beta^{02}\\
\zeta^{02}&\zeta^{22}&\beta^{02}&\beta^{22}\end{array}\right)\ ,

(11.A.2)

and

X_{J}\equiv\left(\begin{array}[]{c}A_{j}\\
B_{j}\end{array}\right)\ ,

(11.A.3)

and
\hat{\kappa}
is given by (
10.17
). Being the linear system of equations (
10.14
) homogeneous, three of the four components
X_{J}
can be written in terms of the fourth, provided that

\det\Lambda=0\ .

(11.A.4)

The choice we make is

\displaystyle B_{\theta}(X)

\displaystyle=-l_{1}B_{t}(X)

(11.A.5)

\displaystyle A_{\theta}(X)

\displaystyle=-l_{2}B_{t}(X)

(11.A.6)

\displaystyle A_{t}(X)

\displaystyle=-l_{3}B_{t}(X)\ ,

(11.A.7)

with

\displaystyle{l_{1}}

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{-\alpha^{00}(\beta^{00}\zeta^{22}-\beta^{02}\zeta^{20})+\alpha^{02}(\beta^{00}\zeta^{02}-\beta^{02}\zeta^{00})+\zeta^{00}\det\zeta}{\alpha^{00}(\beta^{02}\zeta^{22}-\beta^{22}\zeta^{20})+\alpha^{02}(\beta^{22}\zeta^{00}-\beta^{02}\zeta^{02})-(\zeta^{02}-\hat{\kappa})\det\zeta}

(11.A.8)

\displaystyle{l_{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{\alpha^{00}\det\beta-\beta^{00}\zeta^{02}(\zeta^{02}-\hat{\kappa})-\hat{\kappa}\beta^{02}\zeta^{00}+2\beta^{02}\zeta^{00}\zeta^{02}-\beta^{22}(\zeta^{00})^{2}}{\alpha^{00}(\beta^{02}\zeta^{22}-\beta^{22}\zeta^{20})+\alpha^{02}(\beta^{22}\zeta^{00}-\beta^{02}\zeta^{02})-(\zeta^{02}-\hat{\kappa})\det\zeta}

(11.A.9)

\displaystyle{l_{3}}

\displaystyle\equiv-\frac{-\alpha^{02}\det\beta+(\zeta^{02}-\hat{\kappa})(\beta^{00}\zeta^{22}-\beta^{02}\zeta^{20})+\zeta^{00}(\beta^{22}\zeta^{20}-\beta^{02}\zeta^{22})}{\alpha^{00}(\beta^{02}\zeta^{22}-\beta^{22}\zeta^{20})+\alpha^{02}(\beta^{22}\zeta^{00}-\beta^{02}\zeta^{02})-(\zeta^{02}-\hat{\kappa})\det\zeta}\ ,

(11.A.10)

where the request of non-Dirichelet solutions implies the requirements
l_{i}\neq 0
and
l_{i}^{-1}\neq 0
.
We remind that these coefficients are local, depending on the induced metric determinant and/or components from the bulk parameters. In the case of
\mathcal{T}
-invariant
S_{bd}
(
10.129
) discussed in Section
10.4
, the coefficients (
10.136
) are given by

\displaystyle{l_{1}}|_{\eqref{BF-Thp}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma^{t\theta}\frac{\hat{\beta}\alpha^{00}\hat{\zeta}^{20}+\hat{\alpha}\beta^{00}\hat{\zeta}^{02}}{-\alpha^{00}\beta^{22}\hat{\zeta}^{20}+\hat{\zeta}^{02}\left[\hat{\zeta}^{20}(\hat{\zeta}^{02}-\hat{\kappa})-\hat{\alpha}\hat{\beta}\left(\gamma^{t\theta}\right)^{2}\right]}

(11.A.11)

\displaystyle l_{2}|_{\eqref{BF-Thp}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha^{00}\det\beta-\beta^{00}\hat{\zeta}^{02}(\hat{\zeta}^{02}-\hat{\kappa})}{-\alpha^{00}\beta^{22}\hat{\zeta}^{20}+\hat{\zeta}^{02}\left[\hat{\zeta}^{20}(\hat{\zeta}^{02}-\hat{\kappa})-\hat{\alpha}\hat{\beta}\left(\gamma^{t\theta}\right)^{2}\right]}

(11.A.12)

\displaystyle l_{3}|_{\eqref{BF-Thp}}

\displaystyle=-\gamma^{t\theta}\frac{-\hat{\alpha}\det\beta-\hat{\beta}\hat{\zeta}^{20}(\hat{\zeta}^{02}-\hat{\kappa})}{-\alpha^{00}\beta^{22}\hat{\zeta}^{20}+\hat{\zeta}^{02}\left[\hat{\zeta}^{20}(\hat{\zeta}^{02}-\hat{\kappa})-\hat{\alpha}\hat{\beta}\left(\gamma^{t\theta}\right)^{2}\right]}\ .

(11.A.13)

Appendix 11.B
Calculation of the fracton propagators

11.b.1

g_{1}+2g_{2}\neq 0

The matrix equation (
13.30
) yields

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}\hat{G}^{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}+\Lambda^{*}_{\mu\nu,\lambda}\hat{G}^{*\lambda,\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\mu\nu}^{\ \ \rho\sigma}

(11.B.1)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu\nu,\alpha\beta}\hat{G}^{\alpha\beta,\tau}+\Lambda^{*}_{\mu\nu,\lambda}\hat{G}^{\lambda\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.2)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\gamma,\alpha\beta}\hat{G}^{\alpha\beta,\rho\sigma}+H_{\gamma\lambda}\hat{G}^{*\lambda,\rho\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.3)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\gamma,\alpha\beta}\hat{G}^{\alpha\beta,\tau}+H_{\gamma\lambda}\hat{G}^{\lambda\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\gamma}^{\tau}\ .

(11.B.4)

The first equation (
11.B.1
), using the expansions (

), (
13.19
), (
13.26
) and the properties of the
X
-basis listed in Appendix

, gives a system of six equations (remember that our aim is to find the set of coefficients of the propagators (

))

\displaystyle 4{t}\hat{t}+3{t}\hat{w}-4{v}\hat{v}+6p^{2}{g}\hat{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.5)

\displaystyle{t}\hat{w}+2p^{2}{g}\hat{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.6)

\displaystyle p^{2}l\hat{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2

(11.B.7)

\displaystyle p^{2}l\hat{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.8)

\displaystyle{v}\hat{v}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

(11.B.9)

\displaystyle{z}\hat{z}+p^{2}{f}\hat{f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\ .

(11.B.10)

In the same way, from (
11.B.2
), (

) and (

) we get three more sets of equations

\displaystyle 2{z}\hat{f}+{f}\hat{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.11)

\displaystyle 4{t}\hat{g}+2{g}\hat{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.12)

\displaystyle{l}\hat{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.13)

\displaystyle{f}\hat{z}+2{r}\hat{f}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.14)

\displaystyle 4{g}\hat{t}+3{g}\hat{w}+{l}\hat{w}+8{s}\hat{g}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.15)

\displaystyle 4{l}\hat{u}+3{g}\hat{w}+{l}\hat{w}+8{s}\hat{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(11.B.16)

\displaystyle p^{2}{f}\hat{f}+{r}\hat{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

(11.B.17)

\displaystyle 3p^{2}{g}\hat{g}+p^{2}{l}\hat{l}+2{s}\hat{s}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\ .

(11.B.18)

The above systems of equations are easily solved and, using the coefficients of the kinetic term (

) and (

), we finally get

\displaystyle\hat{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{(4\kappa+1)}{(\kappa+1)(g_{1}+2g_{2})p^{2}}

;

\displaystyle\hat{u}

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa(4\kappa+1)-2\xi(g_{1}+2g_{2})}{(\kappa+1)^{2}(g_{1}+2g_{2})p^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(g_{1}-g_{2})p^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\xi}{(2\xi g_{1}-1)p^{2}}

(11.B.20)

\displaystyle=\frac{-4\kappa}{(\kappa+1)(g_{1}+2g_{2})p^{2}}

\displaystyle\hat{f}

\displaystyle=\frac{-2}{(2\xi g_{1}-1)p^{2}}

(11.B.21)

\displaystyle\hat{g}

\displaystyle=0

;

\displaystyle\hat{l}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{(\kappa+1)p^{2}}

(11.B.22)

\displaystyle\hat{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{4g_{1}}{(2\xi g_{1}-1)}

;

\displaystyle\hat{s}

\displaystyle=0\ .

(11.B.23)

11.b.2

g_{1}+2g_{2}=0

The action of the gauge fixed theory, after a field redefinition and setting
\kappa=0
because this is the traceless case, is

\left.S(g_{1},g_{2};\xi,\kappa)\right|_{g_{1}+2g_{2}=0;\kappa=0}=\left.S_{inv}(g_{1},g_{2})\right|_{g_{1}+2g_{2}=0}+S_{gf}(\xi)\ ,

\displaystyle=-3p^{2}

\displaystyle=-p^{2}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=1

;

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi}{2}\ .

Following the same steps of the general case, we find the system of equations for the coefficients of the propagators

\displaystyle{v}\hat{v}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle=0

(11.B.28)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=2

(11.B.29)

\displaystyle 4{t}\hat{g}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle 4{u}\hat{l}+2\hat{s}

\displaystyle=0

(11.B.30)

\displaystyle 4{t}\hat{t}+3{t}\hat{w}

\displaystyle=4

\displaystyle\hat{w}+8{s}\hat{g}

\displaystyle=0

(11.B.31)

\displaystyle{u}\hat{w}+2p^{2}\hat{g}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle=4

(11.B.32)

\displaystyle{t}\hat{w}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle 4\hat{u}+\hat{w}+8{s}\hat{l}

\displaystyle=0\ ,

which is simpler than the general case. In particular, since

Appendix 11.C
Fractons with boundary : detailed calculations

11.c.1
Commutators

a)
The bulk : generalized Ka0c-Moody algebra

Considering the first Ward identity (
14.20
)

\int dx^{3}\theta(x^{3})\partial_{i}\partial_{j}J^{ij}=2(g_{2}-g_{1})\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\tilde{A}^{ij}-2g_{2}\partial_{i}\partial^{i}\tilde{A}|_{x^{3}=0}\ ,

(11.C.1)

we compute

\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{mn}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{FBD-wi1A}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\delta^{(3)}(X-X^{\prime})

\displaystyle=2(g_{2}-g_{1})\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\frac{\delta^{2}Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta J^{mn}(X^{\prime})\tilde{J}_{ij}(X)}-2g_{2}\eta_{ij}\partial_{a}\partial^{a}\frac{\delta^{2}Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta J^{mn}(X^{\prime})\tilde{J}_{ij}(X)}

\displaystyle=2i\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{l}_{j}-g_{2}\eta^{kl}\eta_{ij}\right]\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\langle T(A_{mn}(X^{\prime})\tilde{A}^{ij}(X))\rangle

\displaystyle=2i\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{l}_{j}-g_{2}\eta^{kl}\eta_{ij}\right]\cancel{\langle T(A_{mn}(X^{\prime})\partial_{k}\partial_{l}\tilde{A}^{ij})\rangle}+

\displaystyle+2i\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\delta^{k}_{i}\delta^{l}_{j}-g_{2}\eta^{kl}\eta_{ij}\right]\left\{\left[\partial_{l}\tilde{A}^{ij}(X),A_{mn}(X^{\prime})\right]\delta^{0}_{k}\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})+\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\partial_{k}\left(\left[\tilde{A}^{ij}(X),A_{mn}(X^{\prime})\right]\delta^{0}_{l}\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=2i\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\left(\partial_{j}\tilde{A}^{0j}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}\tilde{A}^{0\textsc{a}}\right)-g_{2}\partial^{0}\tilde{A}\ ,\ A^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})+

\displaystyle+2i\partial_{0}\left\{\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\tilde{A}^{00}+g_{2}\tilde{A}\ ,\ A^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\right\}\ ,

(11.C.2)

where we used the conserved current equation (
14.23
). Integrating over
dx^{0}
, we finally get to the following equal time commutators

\displaystyle\left[\Delta\tilde{A}(X)\ ,\ A_{0n}(X^{\prime})\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}

\displaystyle=0

(11.C.3)

\displaystyle\left[\Delta\tilde{A}(X)\ ,\ A_{\textsc{mn}}(X^{\prime})\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}

\displaystyle=i\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\{\delta(x^{1}-x^{\prime 1})\delta(x^{2}-x^{\prime 2})\}\ ,

(11.C.4)

where we defined

\Delta\tilde{A}\equiv 2(g_{1}-g_{2})\left(\partial_{j}\tilde{A}^{0j}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}\tilde{A}^{0\textsc{a}}\right)+2g_{2}\partial^{0}\tilde{A}\ .

(11.C.5)

In the same way, we now compute

\frac{\delta}{\delta\tilde{J}^{mn}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{FBD-wi1A}
:

\begin{split}0&=2(g_{2}-g_{1})\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\frac{\delta^{2}Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta\tilde{J}^{mn}(X^{\prime})\tilde{J}_{ij}(X)}-2g_{2}\eta_{ij}\partial_{a}\partial^{a}\frac{\delta^{2}Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta\tilde{J}^{mn}(X^{\prime})\tilde{J}_{ij}(X)}\\
&=2i\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\left(\partial_{j}\tilde{A}^{0j}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}\tilde{A}^{0\textsc{a}}\right)-g_{2}\partial^{0}\tilde{A}\ ,\ \tilde{A}^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})+\\
&\quad+2i\partial_{0}\left\{\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\tilde{A}^{00}+g_{2}\tilde{A}\ ,\ \tilde{A}^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\right\}\ ,\end{split}

(11.C.6)

where we used again the conserved current equation (
14.23
). By integrating over time and using the definition (
11.C.5
), we find the following equal time commutator

\left[\Delta\tilde{A}(X)\ ,\ \tilde{A}_{mn}(X^{\prime})\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}=0\ .

(11.C.7)

Taking the second broken Ward identity (
14.22
)

\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\tilde{J}^{ij}|_{x^{3}=0}=-2(g_{2}-g_{1})\partial_{i}\partial_{j}A^{ij}+2g_{2}\partial_{i}\partial^{i}A|_{x^{3}=0}\ .

(11.C.8)

we compute
\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{mn}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{FBD-wi2A}
:

\begin{split}0&=-2(g_{2}-g_{1})\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\frac{\delta^{2}Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta J^{mn}(X^{\prime})J_{ij}(X)}+2g_{2}\eta_{ij}\partial_{a}\partial^{a}\frac{\delta^{2}Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta J^{mn}(X^{\prime})J_{ij}(X)}\\
&=-2i\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\left(\partial_{j}A^{0j}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}A^{0\textsc{a}}\right)-g_{2}\partial^{0}A\ ,\ A^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})+\\
&\quad-2i\partial_{0}\left\{\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})A^{00}+g_{2}A\ ,\ A^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\right\}\ ,\end{split}

(11.C.9)

where we used (
14.32
). Integrating over
dx^{0}
we find the equal time commutator

\left[\Delta A(X)\ ,\ A_{mn}(X^{\prime})\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}=0\ ,

(11.C.10)

where
\Delta A(X)
is defined as (
11.C.5
)

\Delta A\equiv 2(g_{1}-g_{2})\left(\partial_{j}A^{0j}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}A^{0\textsc{a}}\right)+2g_{2}\partial^{0}A\ .

(11.C.11)

We finally compute
\frac{\delta}{\delta\tilde{J}^{mn}(x^{\prime})}\eqref{FBD-wi2A}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{m}\partial_{n}\delta^{(3)}(X-X^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-2(g_{2}-g_{1})\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\frac{\delta^{2}Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta\tilde{J}^{mn}(X^{\prime})J_{ij}(X)}+2g_{2}\eta_{ij}\partial_{a}\partial^{a}\frac{\delta^{2}Z_{c}[J,\tilde{J}]}{\delta\tilde{J}^{mn}(X^{\prime})J_{ij}(X)}

\displaystyle=-2i\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})\left(\partial_{j}A^{0j}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}A^{0\textsc{a}}\right)-g_{2}\partial^{0}A\ ,\ \tilde{A}^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})+

\displaystyle\quad-2i\partial_{0}\left\{\left[(g_{2}-g_{1})A^{00}+g_{2}A\ ,\ \tilde{A}^{\prime}_{mn}\right]\delta(x^{0}-x^{\prime 0})\right\}\ ,

(11.C.12)

where we used (
14.32
) and from which, integrating over
dx^{0}
, we find

\displaystyle\left[\Delta A(X)\ ,\ \tilde{A}_{0n}(X^{\prime})\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}

\displaystyle=0

(11.C.13)

\displaystyle\left[-\Delta A(X)\ ,\ \tilde{A}_{\textsc{mn}}(X^{\prime})\right]_{x^{0}=x^{\prime 0}}

\displaystyle=i\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\{\delta(x^{1}-x^{\prime 1})\delta(x^{2}-x^{\prime 2})\}\ .

(11.C.14)

b)
The boundary : canonical commutators

We take the commutator (
14.34
) and its trace, in the following equal time combination

\left[\Delta\tilde{A}\ ,\ A_{\textsc{df}}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\eta^{\textsc{mn}}A_{\textsc{mn}}^{\prime}\right]=\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{f}}+\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{f}}-\eta^{\textsc{mn}}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\right)\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ .

(11.C.15)

In terms of the solutions on the boundary (
14.47
) we have

\displaystyle A_{\textsc{df}}(X)|_{\eqref{FBD-sol2}}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\textsc{d}ab}\partial^{a}\alpha^{b}_{\ \textsc{f}}(X)+\epsilon_{\textsc{f}ab}\partial^{a}\alpha^{b}_{\ \textsc{d}}(X)

(11.C.16)

\displaystyle\eta^{\textsc{mn}}A_{\textsc{mn}}|_{\eqref{FBD-sol2}}

\displaystyle=2\epsilon_{0\textsc{bc}}\partial^{\textsc{b}}\alpha^{0\textsc{c}}=A_{00}(X)|_{\eqref{FBD-sol2}}

(11.C.17)

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{A}|_{\eqref{FBD-sol1}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\left[g_{12}\left(\epsilon^{0\textsc{mb}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{b}}^{\ \textsc{n}}+\epsilon^{0\textsc{nb}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{b}}^{\ \textsc{m}}\right)\right]\ ,

(11.C.18)

where
g_{12}\equiv 2(g_{1}-g_{2})
. As a consequence of the tracelessness of
A_{ij}(X)
, we can use (
11.C.17
) and write the commutator (
11.C.3
) for
n=0
as follows

\left[\Delta\tilde{A}(X)|_{\eqref{FBD-sol1}}\ ,\ A_{00}(X^{\prime})|_{\eqref{FBD-sol2}}\right]=\left[\Delta\tilde{A}(X)|_{\eqref{FBD-sol1}}\ ,\ \eta^{\textsc{mn}}A_{\textsc{mn}}(X^{\prime})|_{\eqref{FBD-sol2}}\right]=0\ ,

(11.C.19)

then, using (
11.C.16
), (
11.C.18
) and (
11.C.19
), the commutator (
11.C.15
) becomes

\begin{split}\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\left\{...\right\}^{\textsc{mn}}_{\textsc{df}}&=\left[\Delta\tilde{A}(X)|_{\eqref{FBD-sol1}}\ ,\ A_{\textsc{df}}(X^{\prime})|_{\eqref{FBD-sol2}}\right]\\
&=\partial_{\textsc{m}}\partial_{\textsc{n}}\left[g_{12}\left(\epsilon^{0\textsc{mb}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{b}}^{\ \textsc{n}}(X)+\epsilon^{0\textsc{nb}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{b}}^{\ \textsc{m}}(X)\right)\ ,\ \epsilon_{\textsc{d}ab}\partial^{a}\alpha^{b}_{\ \textsc{f}}(X^{\prime})+\epsilon_{\textsc{f}ab}\partial^{a}\alpha^{b}_{\ \textsc{d}}(X^{\prime})\right]\ ,\end{split}

(11.C.20)

from which we can identify the following canonical commutation relation

\left[Q^{\textsc{mn}}\ ,\ P^{\prime}_{\textsc{df}}\right]=i\left(\frac{\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{f}}+\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{f}}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{mn}}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ ,

(11.C.21)

with

\displaystyle Q^{\textsc{mn}}=Q^{\textsc{nm}}

\displaystyle\equiv\epsilon^{0\textsc{mb}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{b}}^{\ \textsc{n}}+\epsilon^{0\textsc{nb}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{b}}^{\ \textsc{m}}

(11.C.22)

\displaystyle P_{\textsc{df}}=P_{\textsc{fd}}\

\displaystyle\equiv g_{12}\left(\epsilon_{\textsc{d}ab}\partial^{a}\alpha^{b}_{\ \textsc{f}}+\epsilon_{\textsc{f}ab}\partial^{a}\alpha^{b}_{\ \textsc{d}}\right)

(11.C.23)

and
Q^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}=P^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}=0
. We can go further, multiplying both right and left hand sides of (
11.C.21
) by
\epsilon_{0\textsc{ma}}\epsilon^{0\textsc{fk}}=\delta_{\textsc{m}}^{\textsc{k}}\delta_{\textsc{a}}^{\textsc{f}}-\delta_{\textsc{m}}^{\textsc{f}}\delta_{\textsc{a}}^{\textsc{k}}

\left[\epsilon_{0\textsc{ma}}Q^{\textsc{mn}}\ ,\ \epsilon^{0\textsc{fk}}P^{\prime}_{\textsc{df}}\right]=i(\delta_{\textsc{m}}^{\textsc{k}}\delta_{\textsc{a}}^{\textsc{f}}-\delta_{\textsc{m}}^{\textsc{f}}\delta_{\textsc{a}}^{\textsc{k}})\left(\frac{\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{f}}+\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{f}}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{mn}}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ ,

(11.C.24)

for which

\begin{split}\epsilon_{0\textsc{ma}}Q^{\textsc{mn}}&=\epsilon_{0\textsc{ma}}\left(\epsilon^{0\textsc{mb}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{b}}^{\ \textsc{n}}+\epsilon^{0\textsc{nb}}\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{b}}^{\ \textsc{m}}\right)\\
&=-2\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{a}}^{\ \textsc{n}}+\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{a}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}\end{split}

(11.C.25)

which is the traceless spatial part of
\tilde{\alpha}_{{mn}}(X)
. Then

\begin{split}\epsilon^{0\textsc{fk}}P_{\textsc{df}}&=g_{12}\left[(\delta^{0}_{a}\delta^{\textsc{k}}_{b}-\delta^{0}_{b}\delta^{\textsc{k}}_{a})\partial^{a}\alpha^{b}_{\ \textsc{d}}+\epsilon^{0\textsc{fk}}\epsilon_{0\textsc{da}}\partial^{\textsc{a}}\alpha^{0}_{\ \textsc{f}}-\epsilon^{0\textsc{fk}}\epsilon_{0\textsc{db}}\partial^{0}\alpha^{\textsc{b}}_{\ \textsc{f}}\right]\\
&=g_{12}\left[2(\partial^{0}\alpha^{\textsc{k}}_{\ \textsc{d}}-\partial^{\textsc{k}}\alpha^{0}_{\ \textsc{d}})+\delta^{\textsc{k}}_{\textsc{d}}(\partial^{\textsc{a}}\alpha^{0}_{\ \textsc{a}}-\partial^{0}\alpha^{\textsc{b}}_{\ \textsc{b}})\right]\ .\end{split}

(11.C.26)

Finally, at the right hand side we have

(\delta_{\textsc{m}}^{\textsc{k}}\delta_{\textsc{a}}^{\textsc{f}}-\delta_{\textsc{m}}^{\textsc{f}}\delta_{\textsc{a}}^{\textsc{k}})\left(\frac{\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{f}}+\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{m}}_{\textsc{f}}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{mn}}\eta_{\textsc{df}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{k}}_{\textsc{d}}-\delta^{\textsc{n}}_{\textsc{d}}\delta^{\textsc{k}}_{\textsc{a}}-\eta_{\textsc{ad}}\eta^{\textsc{kn}}\right)\ .

(11.C.27)

By properly raising and lowering the indices with
\eta^{\textsc{dl}}\eta_{\textsc{nb}}
, we finally get

4g_{12}\biggl[\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{ab}}-\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}\ ,\ (\partial^{0}\alpha^{\textsc{kl}}-\partial^{\textsc{k}}\alpha^{0\textsc{l}})^{\prime}+\eta^{\textsc{kl}}(\partial_{\textsc{f}}\alpha^{0\textsc{f}}-\partial^{0}\alpha^{\textsc{f}}_{\ \textsc{f}})^{\prime}\biggr]=\tfrac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{k}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{l}}_{\textsc{b}}+\delta^{\textsc{l}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{k}}_{\textsc{b}}-\eta^{\textsc{kl}}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ ,

(11.C.28)

where the primed quantities depend on
X^{\prime}
. At the right hand side we have the index symmetry
\textsc{a}\leftrightarrow\textsc{b}
and
\textsc{c}\leftrightarrow\textsc{d}
, while at the left hand side the symmetry is only for
\textsc{a}\leftrightarrow\textsc{b}
. We thus symmetrize the result as follows

\frac{1}{2}\left(\biggl[..._{\textsc{ab}}\ ,\ ...^{\textsc{cd}}\biggr]+\biggl[..._{\textsc{ab}}\ ,\ ...^{\textsc{dc}}\biggr]\right)=\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{b}}+\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{b}}-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ ,

(11.C.29)

obtaining

\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{ab}}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}\ ,\ -g_{12}\left(2f^{\prime\textsc{cd}0}-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}f^{\prime{\ a}0}_{a}\right)\right]=\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{b}}+\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{b}}-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ ,

(11.C.30)

where
f_{abc}(X)
is analogous to
F_{\mu\nu\rho}(x)
(
12.19
), but referred to
\alpha_{ab}(X)
,
i.e.

f_{abc}\equiv\partial_{a}\alpha_{bc}+\partial_{b}\alpha_{ac}-2\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}\ .

(11.C.31)

From (
11.C.30
) we can identify the new canonical variables as

\displaystyle\tilde{q}_{\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle\equiv\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{ab}}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}

(11.C.32)

\displaystyle\tilde{p}^{\textsc{cd}}

\displaystyle\equiv-2g_{12}\left(f^{\textsc{cd}0}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{cd}}f^{\ a0}_{a}\right)=-2g_{12}\left[(\partial^{\textsc{c}}\alpha^{0\textsc{d}}+\partial^{\textsc{d}}\alpha^{0\textsc{c}}-2\partial^{0}\alpha^{\textsc{cd}})-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\partial_{a}\alpha^{0a}\right]\ .

(11.C.33)

Not surprisingly, starting from the commutator (
14.35
), and proceeding as we just did, we land on an analogous result (up to a sign) with
\alpha\leftrightarrow\tilde{\alpha}
switched,
i.e.
we get

\left[\alpha_{\textsc{ab}}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\alpha^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}\ ,\ g_{12}\left(2\tilde{f}^{\prime\textsc{cd}0}-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\tilde{f}^{\prime{\ a}0}_{a}\right)\right]=\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{b}}+\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{b}}-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ ,

(11.C.34)

where
\tilde{f}_{abc}(X)
refers to
\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}(X)

\tilde{f}_{abc}\equiv\partial_{a}\tilde{\alpha}_{bc}+\partial_{b}\tilde{\alpha}_{ac}-2\partial_{c}\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}\ .

(11.C.35)

From (
11.C.34
) we can identify another set of canonical variables

\displaystyle q_{\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle\equiv\alpha_{\textsc{ab}}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\alpha^{\textsc{m}}_{\ \textsc{m}}

(11.C.36)

\displaystyle p^{\textsc{cd}}

\displaystyle\equiv 2g_{12}\left(\tilde{f}^{\textsc{cd}0}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\tilde{f}^{\ a0}_{a}\right)=2g_{12}\left[(\partial^{\textsc{c}}\tilde{\alpha}^{0\textsc{d}}+\partial^{\textsc{d}}\tilde{\alpha}^{0\textsc{c}}-2\partial^{0}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{cd}})-\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\partial_{a}\tilde{\alpha}^{0a}\right]\ .

(11.C.37)

We observe that the canonical variables
\tilde{q}_{\textsc{ab}}(X),\ q_{\textsc{ab}}(X)
in (
11.C.30
) and (
11.C.34
), depend on the traceless spatial part of the fields
\tilde{\alpha}_{ij}(X)
and
\alpha_{ij}(X)
.

11.c.2
The most general action

The most general action of the 3D boundary theory must be compatible with

•

power-counting
[\alpha]=0,\ [\tilde{\alpha}]=1
;

•

symmetry
\delta S=\tilde{\delta}S=0
, where
\delta
and
\tilde{\delta}
are defined in (
14.45
) and (
14.46
) ;

•

canonical variables,
i.e.\ \frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{kin}}{\partial\dot{q}}=p
, identified in (
14.52
) and (
14.53
).

From the first two requests we have that the most general action must be

\displaystyle S_{3D}

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}X\left\{\omega_{1}\left(\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}\partial^{c}\alpha^{ab}-\tfrac{3}{2}\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}\partial^{a}\alpha^{bc}\right)+\omega_{2}\left(\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}\partial^{c}\tilde{\alpha}^{ab}-\tfrac{3}{2}\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}\partial^{a}\tilde{\alpha}^{bc}\right)-\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-3\omega_{3}\,\alpha^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\partial_{b}\alpha_{cd}-3\omega_{4}\,\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\partial_{b}\alpha_{cd}-3\omega_{5}\,\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\partial_{b}\tilde{\alpha}_{cd}\right\}

(11.C.38)

\displaystyle=\int d^{3}X\left\{\tfrac{\omega_{1}}{6}\,\varphi_{abc}\varphi^{abc}+\tfrac{\omega_{2}}{6}\,\varphi_{abc}\tilde{\varphi}^{abc}+\omega_{3}\,\alpha^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\varphi_{dbc}+\omega_{4}\,\alpha^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\tilde{\varphi}_{dbc}+\omega_{5}\,\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\tilde{\varphi}_{dbc}\right\}

where
\omega_{i}
are constants,
[\omega_{2}]=[\omega_{5}]=0,\ [\omega_{1}]=[\omega_{4}]=1,\ [\omega_{3}]=2
, and we defined the tensor

\begin{split}\varphi_{abc}&\equiv f_{abc}+\frac{1}{4}\left(-2\eta_{ab}f^{d}_{\ dc}+\eta_{bc}f^{d}_{\ da}+\eta_{ac}f^{d}_{\ db}\right)\\
&=-2\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}+\partial_{a}\alpha_{bc}+\partial_{b}\alpha_{ac}-\eta_{ab}\partial^{d}\alpha_{dc}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{bc}\partial^{d}\alpha_{da}+\frac{1}{2}\eta_{ac}\partial^{d}\alpha_{db}\ ,\end{split}

(11.C.39)

and its analog
\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}(X)
with respect to
\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}(X)
, with the following properties

\displaystyle\varphi_{abc}=\varphi_{bac}\quad;\quad\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}=\tilde{\varphi}_{bac}

(11.C.40)

\displaystyle\varphi_{abc}+\varphi_{cab}+\varphi_{bca}=0=\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}+\tilde{\varphi}_{cab}+\tilde{\varphi}_{bca}

(11.C.41)

\displaystyle\delta\varphi_{abc}=\tilde{\delta}\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}=0

(11.C.42)

\displaystyle\eta^{ab}\varphi_{abc}=\eta^{bc}\varphi_{abc}=\eta^{ab}\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}=\eta^{bc}\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}=0\ .

(11.C.43)

Notice that

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{mn}0}

\displaystyle=\tilde{f}^{\textsc{mn}0}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{mn}}\tilde{f}_{a}^{\ a0}=\frac{1}{2g_{12}}p^{\textsc{mn}}

(11.C.44)

\displaystyle\varphi^{\textsc{mn}0}

\displaystyle=f^{\textsc{mn}0}-\frac{1}{2}\eta^{\textsc{mn}}f_{a}^{\ a0}=-\frac{1}{2g_{12}}\tilde{p}^{\textsc{mn}}\ .

(11.C.45)

We rewrite the fields
\alpha_{ab}(X)
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{ab}(X)
according to the representation of the rotation group, as follows

\displaystyle\alpha_{00}

\displaystyle=-4\psi=\alpha^{\textsc{a}}_{\textsc{a}}

(11.C.46)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0\textsc{a}}

\displaystyle=v_{\textsc{a}}

(11.C.47)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle=2s_{\textsc{ab}}-2\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\psi\quad;\quad s_{\textsc{ab}}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{\textsc{ab}}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\alpha^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{d}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}q_{\textsc{ab}}

(11.C.48)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{00}

\displaystyle=-4\tilde{\psi}=\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{a}}_{\textsc{a}}

(11.C.49)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{0\textsc{a}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{v}_{\textsc{a}}

(11.C.50)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle=2\tilde{s}_{\textsc{ab}}-2\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\psi}\quad;\quad\tilde{s}_{\textsc{ab}}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{\textsc{ab}}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\alpha}^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{d}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{q}_{\textsc{ab}}\ ,

(11.C.51)

in terms of which the commutators (
14.52
) and (
14.53
) become

\displaystyle\left[2s_{\textsc{ab}}\ ,\ 2g_{12}\tilde{\varphi}^{\prime\textsc{cd}0}\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{b}}+\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{b}}-\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})

(11.C.52)

\displaystyle\left[2\tilde{s}_{\textsc{ab}}\ ,\ -2g_{12}\varphi^{\prime\textsc{cd}0}\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{2}\left(\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{b}}+\delta^{\textsc{d}}_{\textsc{a}}\delta^{\textsc{c}}_{\textsc{b}}-\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\eta^{\textsc{cd}}\right)\delta^{(2)}(X-X^{\prime})\ .

(11.C.53)

Compatibility of the action
S_{3D}
(
11.C.38
) with the first commutator (
11.C.52
),
i.e.

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{3D}}{\partial\dot{q}_{\textsc{ab}}}=p^{\textsc{ab}}\ ,

(11.C.54)

requires

\omega_{1}=\omega_{3}=\omega_{4}=0\ ,

(11.C.55)

and
\omega_{5}
is left free. Finally, distinguishing between time and space indices in the term of (
11.C.38
) which has
\omega_{2}
as coefficient, we have

\varphi_{abc}\tilde{\varphi}^{abc}=\frac{3}{2}\varphi_{00\textsc{a}}\tilde{\varphi}^{00\textsc{a}}+2\varphi_{0\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\varphi}^{0\textsc{ab}}+\varphi_{\textsc{ab}0}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{ab}0}+\varphi_{\textsc{abc}}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{abc}}\ ,

(11.C.56)

where we observed that
\varphi_{\textsc{a}00}=-\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{00\textsc{a}}
(and the same for
\tilde{\varphi}_{\textsc{a}00}
). Additionally, by using (
11.C.46
), (
11.C.47
) and (
11.C.48
), we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{00\textsc{a}}

\displaystyle=6\partial_{\textsc{a}}\psi+\partial_{0}v_{\textsc{a}}+\partial^{\textsc{b}}q_{\textsc{ab}}

(11.C.57)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\textsc{ab}0}

\displaystyle=-2\partial_{0}q_{\textsc{ab}}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}v_{\textsc{b}}+\partial_{\textsc{b}}v_{\textsc{a}}-\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\partial^{\textsc{d}}v_{\textsc{d}}

(11.C.58)

\displaystyle\varphi_{0\textsc{ab}}

\displaystyle=-2\partial_{\textsc{b}}v_{\textsc{a}}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}v_{\textsc{b}}+\partial_{0}q_{\textsc{ab}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\partial^{\textsc{d}}v_{\textsc{d}}

(11.C.59)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\textsc{abc}}

\displaystyle=-2\partial_{\textsc{c}}q_{\textsc{ab}}+\partial_{\textsc{b}}q_{\textsc{ac}}+\partial_{\textsc{a}}q_{\textsc{bc}}+6\eta_{\textsc{ab}}\partial_{\textsc{c}}\psi-3\eta_{\textsc{bc}}\partial_{\textsc{a}}\psi-3\eta_{\textsc{ac}}\partial_{\textsc{b}}\psi-

\displaystyle\quad-\partial^{0}(\eta_{\textsc{ab}}v_{\textsc{c}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ac}}v_{\textsc{b}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{bc}}v_{\textsc{a}})-\partial^{\textsc{d}}(\eta_{\textsc{ab}}q_{\textsc{dc}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{ac}}q_{\textsc{db}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\eta_{\textsc{bc}}q_{\textsc{da}})\ .

(11.C.60)

The only terms containing
\dot{q}
contributions are related to
\varphi_{\textsc{ab}0}
and
\varphi_{0\textsc{ab}}
,
i.e.
in the 3D Lagrangian they only appear in
\frac{\omega_{2}}{6}(2\varphi_{0\textsc{ab}}\tilde{\varphi}^{0\textsc{ab}}+\varphi_{\textsc{ab}0}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{ab}0})
. Keeping in mind this, consistently with its definition, for a traceless tensor in
d
dimensions we have

\frac{\partial s_{\mu\nu}}{\partial s_{\alpha\beta}}=\frac{\delta^{\alpha}_{\mu}\delta^{\beta}_{\nu}+\delta^{\alpha}_{\nu}\delta^{\beta}_{\mu}}{2}-\frac{1}{d}\eta^{\alpha\beta}\eta_{\mu\nu}\ ,

(11.C.61)

the compatibility condition (
11.C.54
) implies

\begin{split}2g_{12}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{ab}0}&=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{3D}}{\partial\dot{q}_{\textsc{ab}}}\\
&=\frac{\omega_{2}}{6}\left(2\frac{\partial\varphi_{0\textsc{mn}}}{\partial\dot{q}_{\textsc{ab}}}\tilde{\varphi}^{0\textsc{mn}}+\frac{\partial\varphi_{\textsc{mn}0}}{\partial\dot{q}_{\textsc{ab}}}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{mn}0}\right)\\
&=\frac{\omega_{2}}{6}\left(-2\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{ab}0}+\tilde{\varphi}^{0\textsc{ab}}+\tilde{\varphi}^{0\textsc{ba}}\right)\\
&=-\frac{\omega_{2}}{2}\tilde{\varphi}^{\textsc{ab}0}\ ,\end{split}

(11.C.62)

due to the cyclicity of
\tilde{\varphi}_{abc}(X)
(
11.C.41
). We thus find

\omega_{2}=-4g_{12}\ ,

(11.C.63)

from which the 3D action (
11.C.38
) becomes

\begin{split}S_{3D}&=\int d^{3}X\left[-4g_{12}\left(\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}\partial^{c}\tilde{\alpha}^{ab}-\frac{3}{2}\partial_{c}\alpha_{ab}\partial^{a}\tilde{\alpha}^{bc}\right)+3\omega_{5}\,\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\partial_{b}\tilde{\alpha}_{cd}\right]\\
&=\int d^{3}X\left[-g_{12}\left(\frac{2}{3}f_{abc}\tilde{f}^{abc}-\frac{1}{2}f_{a}^{\ ab}\tilde{f}^{c}_{\ cb}\right)+\omega_{5}\,\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\tilde{f}_{dbc}\right]\\
&=\int d^{3}X\left(-\frac{2}{3}g_{12}\,\varphi_{abc}\tilde{\varphi}^{abc}+\omega_{5}\,\tilde{\alpha}^{d}_{a}\epsilon^{abc}\tilde{\varphi}_{dbc}\right)\ .\end{split}

(11.C.64)

The second commutator (
11.C.53
) yields the same result, with
\alpha\leftrightarrow\tilde{\alpha}
. The two choices are alternative and equivalent, in fact by choosing the first (
11.C.52
) we have
q\sim\alpha
,
p\sim\tilde{\alpha}
, while the second (
11.C.53
) corresponds to
\tilde{q}\sim\tilde{\alpha}
,
\tilde{p}\sim\alpha
. Something similar also happens in Maxwell theory
[
1
]
.
